Dust Extraction System

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Tiqua Jackson ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T23:21:54+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. Somewhere in a workshop, there’s a dust extractor already plugged in and switched on. It prevents the debris from going up in the air. This makes the job of an air scrubber or air purifier a lot easier. However, its effectiveness comes down to a handful of practical decisions. Owning one just won’t cut it. Match Extraction Power to the Tools Being Used A shop vac or basic extractor pulls a fraction of the airflow that a properly sized system delivers, and airflow is what captures dust at the source. Sanders and saws all produce different volumes of dust at different particle sizes. An extractor undersized for the tool it’s connected to leaves a meaningful portion of that dust airborne. Festool tools and accessories built around the CT extractor range work because the extraction unit and the power tool are engineered to communicate directly. In that way, the extractor automatically activates and adjusts suction the moment the tool starts running. This coordinated integration is better than manually operating these devices, as this practice leaves gaps where dust escapes. Get the Hose Diameter and Length Right A narrow hose for the extractor’s rated airflow creates more problems. It reduces suction at the tool end regardless of how powerful the extraction unit itself is. Similarly, an excessively long hose run between the extractor and the tool increases resistance and drops effective suction well below what the same extractor would deliver over a shorter run. Getting hose diameter matched correctly to the extractor’s output, and keeping hose runs as short and direct as the workshop layout reasonably allows, preserves the airflow at the point where it matters most, right at the tool’s dust extraction port. Seal the Tool Connection Properly A loose or poorly fitted connection between the tool’s dust port and the hose creates a leak point. This is where unfiltered air gets pulled in instead of dust-laden air from the cutting or sanding zone. This reduces effective extraction even when the extractor itself is running at full power. Do this wrong, and some of that suction is being wasted pulling in ambient air through the leak. Checking that every connection point, tool to hose, hose to extractor, seals properly without gaps ensures that there’s no leak anywhere in that chain. Maintain Filter Condition A clogged filter reduces airflow through the entire system, and this happens gradually enough that a lot of workshop owners don’t notice declining performance until it’s already worse. Regularly checking and cleaning or replacing filters according to the manufacturer’s recommended schedule keeps the system performing at the level it was designed for. This matters particularly for fine dust from sanding, since filters clog faster with very fine particulate than with coarser sawdust, and a filter that’s coping fine with router waste might already be greatly restricted when it comes to fine sanding dust from the same workshop. Position the Extractor and Manage Cable and Hose Layout Sensibly An extractor positioned awkwardly discourages consistent use because it’s inconvenient to set up for a quick job. A workshop layout that keeps the extractor within easy reach of the most frequently used tools increases how often extraction gets used.

Life After Trump

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Dick Price, Ken Levy ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T21:56:34+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. The pre-Trump world is gone. But what replaces it? That was the central question posed by Ken Levy, LMFT, during his October 3 presentation, Life After Trump , at the 4th Annual Left Coast Forum at Cal State LA. Levy, a community-focused psychotherapist, longtime activist, and Coordinating Committee member of the Northeast LA Alliance for Democracy, challenged participants to look beyond the daily turbulence of the Trump presidency and confront a more fundamental problem: America cannot simply return to the political arrangements that made Trumpism possible. The session rejected two competing visions of America's future. One is the authoritarian agenda embodied in the Right's Project 2025. The other is the neoliberal Democratic Party's familiar promise to restore normalcy without fundamentally changing the political and economic arrangements that produced the crisis. Neither, Levy argued, offers an adequate path forward. Life After Trump: Rebuilding Democracy in a World That Cannot Go Back (53:25) The Old World Is Dying Levy began by invoking Italian political philosopher Antonio Gramsci's concept of an interregnum —a period when an old social order is collapsing but a new one has not yet emerged. Gramsci's frequently cited observation that the old world is dying while the new struggles to be born provided a framework for understanding the present political moment. Levy described Trumpism not as the creation of one individual but as the convergence of political movements that developed over decades. He identified an alliance involving white supremacists, Christian nationalists, economic elites, fossil-fuel interests, and wealthy technology entrepreneurs seeking to concentrate power and weaken democratic restraints. Project 2025, in his telling, represents far more than a collection of conservative policy proposals. It is part of a coordinated effort to transform government, dismantle institutional protections, and reverse social gains achieved during the twentieth century. Particularly troubling to Levy is the movement's apparent determination to consolidate power rather than treat electoral victories as temporary mandates subject to democratic accountability. He described the resulting political environment as one of orchestrated chaos: a continuous stream of actions, announcements, threats, and controversies that overwhelms the public's ability to respond. The consequences are psychological as well as political. People become exhausted. They lose their ability to distinguish immediate emergencies from long-term threats. Eventually, some retreat from political engagement altogether. For a psychotherapist concerned with community well-being, Levy sees that exhaustion as one of authoritarianism's most consequential effects. Related: Build a United Front against Fascism and a Workers Party Why Returning to Normal Is Not Enough If Levy rejected the Republican agenda, he was scarcely more enthusiastic about the Democratic Party establishment. He characterized the Biden administration's approach as an attempt to restore a previous political order rather than confront the systemic conditions that enabled Trump's rise. During the discussion, Levy pointed to the recurring Democratic tendency to avoid confronting past abuses in the name of moving forward. He also criticized Democratic leaders who, in his view, repeatedly prioritize political caution and accommodation over the demands of their own constituents. An audience member pressed him on whether a new generation of Democratic candidates could offer a meaningful alternative, or whether even self-described progressives would eventually accommodate existing power structures. Levy's response returned to a fundamental principle of organizing: the most important question is not simply what politicians promise, but what organized citizens can compel them to do. That distinction shifts the focus from finding an ideal candidate to building movements capable of holding elected officials accountable. It also requires acknowledging that political victories are rarely permanent. America's Long Struggle Between Democracy and Concentrated Power Levy situated the present crisis within a much longer American history. From abolition and Reconstruction to the labor movement and the New Deal, he described a recurring struggle between those seeking to broaden freedom and shared prosperity and those attempting to concentrate wealth, privilege, and political authority. Reconstruction offered an opportunity to remake the country following enslavement and the Civil War, but violent white supremacist resistance and political compromise undermined its promise. Labor organizations fought for economic dignity while corporate interests resisted their advances. The New Deal expanded the federal government's responsibility for economic security, only to face decades of organized opposition. For Levy, these historical examples demonstrate that social progress requires continuous organization and defense. The movements opposing that progress do not disappear after an election. They regroup, adapt, and develop new ways of exercising power. He extended that analysis to contemporary technology billionaires and intellectual figures associated with anti-democratic political ideas, including Peter Thiel and Curtis Yarvin. His concern was that the next generation of right-wing leadership could preserve Trumpism's authoritarian direction while adopting different methods and personalities. The danger, in other words, does not end when Trump leaves office. Related: Don't Obey in Advance: How Ordinary Americans Can Resist Authoritarianism From Passive Consumers to Active Citizens Perhaps Levy's most important challenge was directed not at politicians but at the people attending his session. Modern American society, he argued, has conditioned people to think primarily as isolated individuals and consumers rather than members of a community with shared responsibilities. Economic pressure, cultural habits, and political alienation encourage people to concentrate on private survival. But democracy cannot function when citizens are reduced to spectators. Levy asked participants to consider what they wanted their lives and communities to become—and what they were prepared to contribute beyond their immediate personal interests. One participant expressed a desire simply to live without fear of government interference. Others discussed the difficulty of imagining a better future while political and economic circumstances seemed increasingly unstable. The conversation repeatedly returned to the idea of the common good. What do people owe one another? What responsibilities accompany citizenship? And how can individuals who feel powerless begin acting collectively? These questions transformed the gathering from a conventional political lecture into an invitation to rethink the relationship between personal responsibility and democratic action. Community as an Antidote to Authoritarianism The discussion ultimately turned toward the importance of rebuilding community. One participant observed that meaningful change would require moving from a culture organized around individual advancement toward one in which people again understand themselves as part of a collective. Levy agreed, emphasizing that isolation makes people more vulnerable to fear, exhaustion, and political withdrawal. Community offers something different: companionship, mutual support, shared purpose, and the possibility of effective collective action. He pointed to demonstrations and organizing efforts that bring people together not simply to oppose particular policies but to experience solidarity. Protests, he suggested, can be occasions of connection and even joy, reminding participants that they are not facing political threats alone. This, he explained, was one of the reasons for creating the Left Coast Forum. The gathering was intended not only to expose participants to new ideas but also to help organizers, activists, and community members find one another and develop relationships capable of sustaining future work. Levy stressed that meaningful action need not begin on a grand scale. Writing letters, contacting representatives, supporting organizations, attending demonstrations, contributing resources, and participating in local campaigns are all ways of developing political power. What matters is connecting those individual efforts to broader collective movements. Related: Nina Turner: There Is No Economic Justice Without Racial Justice. The Future Is Something We Must Build Life After Trump did not end with a detailed blueprint for a post-Trump society. That was deliberate. Levy's session was conceived as a conversation in which participants could begin identifying their own priorities and responsibilities rather than passively receiving a prescribed program. The discussion exposed the difficulty of imagining alternatives when so much political energy is consumed by resisting immediate threats. But it also suggested a starting point. A post-Trump agenda must address more than the conduct of one president. It must confront the concentration of economic and political power, defend multiracial democracy, restore the principle of the common good, and create durable organizations capable of demanding accountability. The challenge is not merely to defeat an authoritarian movement or elect a different set of political leaders. It is to build a society in which ordinary people possess the collective power to determine their own future. And that work, Levy reminded his audience, begins long before the next election.

Unofficial Results of the 2026 UAW International Officer Election

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: UAW ／ 著者: Justin Mayhugh ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T21:28:49+00:00

Members of the United Auto Workers have cast their ballots by mail for the union’s top officers for the second time in the union’s 91-year history. Newly elected UAW International Officers will be sworn in on Friday, October 16 and will serve a four- year term. UAW International Officer Election Results: International President: Shawn Fain International Secretary-Treasurer: Brandon Campbell International Vice-President: Laura Dickerson, Kevin Gotinsky, Ryan Hiestand Region 1 Regional Director: LaShawn English Region 1A Regional Director: Mark DePaoli Region 1D Regional Director: Scott Zuckschwerdt Region 2B Regional Director: David Green Region 4 Regional Director: Lucas DeSpain Region 6 Regional Director: Mike Miller Region 8 Regional Director: Tim Smith Region 9 Regional Director: James Lakeman Region 9A Regional Director: Brandon Mancilla

Local 1186 Members Make Plans to Respond to Possible Election Interference

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: UE NEWS Updates | UE ／ 著者: 不明 ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T20:33:42+00:00

As the rising tide of neo-fascist corporatism gradually erodes the foundations of democracy and the Trump administration escalates its attacks on the working class, the United States faces a critical moment in its history. Under this increasingly corrupt, violent, and authoritarian federal government, we have seen the unprecedented consolidation of wealth and political power in our country. Now there is a very real chance that Trump and his cohorts will attempt to interfere with or sabotage the midterm elections in November. Election interference could involve a range of scenarios, including (but not limited to): making it more difficult for people to vote by mail, voter purges, mass disinformation campaigns, stationing armed federal agents at polling places, voter intimidation, illegal seizure of ballots and election materials, and outright refusal to certify election results (or altogether altering them). Organized labor needs to be prepared to address any attempts to disrupt the midterm elections. Members of Local 1186, representing Willy Street Co-op in Madison, WI, have been strategically aligning resources and strengthening connections with other labor and community organizations as part of a collective effort to prepare for these potential threats to democracy. We have been working to circulate and collect pledge cards for labor rights, with escalating actions to which the signer can commit. These actions range from simply voting for pro-labor candidates and phone banking to being a constitutional observer and participating in a national economic blackout in the event that the elections are compromised. The cards were created in conjunction with the South Central Federation of Labor, AFL-CIO (SCFL) and first distributed at the city's annual Labor Fest celebration on Labor Day, where Willy Street Co-op workers, including stewards Adele Horton and Delta Adamson and Vice President Michele McCoy , collected pledges from nearly 100 individuals. In addition to recruiting volunteers for election defense activities and voter protection work, the cards can function as a tool to build conversations around and increase member engagement. Last month, the Western Regional Council passed a resolution on election protection that was introduced by former president of Local 1186 (and current interim Western Regional President) Michael Tomaloff . The delegates resolved that election protection "is not only necessary to protect what exists of true democracy in our government, but is also an opportunity to bring working people together to build an even stronger force for change in our country and the world." Members of Local 1186 have attended labor coalition meetings to come up with a coordinated plan of action and participated in election defense training. Treasurer Beth White and Steward Eva Jach , along with UE International Representative Sean Fulkerson and myself, recently attended a virtual event co-sponsored by Labor for Democracy, Standing for Democracy, and May Day Strong called "Union Training to Defend Our Votes and Elections," which focused on how to facilitate scenario planning in a union to address anticipated national threats at the local level. Regardless of what happens in November, engaging in election defense activities can flex the organizing muscles of a local, build solidarity within the community, and provide educational tools for future struggles. Although voting and partisan politics may not be the most effective pathways to significant social or economic change for American workers, the right to maintain open, free, and fair elections in the United States must be safeguarded. As workers, we all have collective power within our communities. And as members of a militant, progressive labor union, we are uniquely positioned to mobilize our fellow workers for direct action. If you like what you read, please consider subscribing to the UE NEWS — for as little as $5/year you can support great labor journalism and receive the print edition of the UE NEWS four times per year. You can also sign up to receive monthly UE NEWS Bulletins via email, or follow UE on Facebook , Twitter , Instagram and YouTube .

Neukölln, the Berlin District Where the Left Broke Through

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: Jacobin ／ 著者: Frederik Blauwhof ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T20:26:55+00:00

Amid resounding recent victories for the Alternative für Deutschland (AfD), on September 20 Germany’s socialist party Die Linke bucked the trend with a spectacular victory in Berlin. It dominated the campaign with its program of ending cuts, capping rents, and socializing housing companies, leaving other parties to respond to the popular demand that Berliners had confirmed in a 2021 referendum . The monster victory for the AfD in Saxony-Anhalt further galvanized Berlin’s diverse urban working class to express opposition to the fascists. Die Linke’s doubling of its vote in Berlin, winning 25.7 percent support, was no blip. The February 2025 federal election, when Die Linke enjoyed a comeback after years of stagnation, crisis, and splits, already saw a turn to the left in urban centers, among young people, women, migrants, and the Muslim community. At that point already, Die Linke won 20 percent of the vote, with striking gains in inner-city areas of Mitte, Friedrichshain-Kreuzberg, and Neukölln. In the September 20 Berlin state election, it did even better. This victory didn’t just come from the party’s media profile or even its policy agenda. Its result follows a groundswell of coordinated campaigning by local Die Linke branches, whose membership in the German capital has grown from 7,200 members in 2023 to over 19,000 this September. During this campaign, members and sympathizers were visible across the city, on stalls, rallies, and street events, and knocking on doors. Importantly, local activists have been rooted in various struggles and, in mobilizing a vote for Die Linke, could build on already-existing relationships and address local grievances. Across swathes of the inner city, the party has worked to gain trust among parts of the working class that tended toward political cynicism and that had long voted for the Social Democrats (SPD) or Greens without expecting much, or even anything. Take the Rollbergviertel in Neukölln, for example, one of the poorest areas of Berlin. Here apartment blocks belong to public housing corporation Stadt und Land, which has raised service costs and left the buildings to dilapidate and rot while cutting janitors and ignoring complaints. Local Die Linke candidate Philipp Dehne organized a rally in the block with volunteers at the neighborhood canteen Kiezanker, gathered photos of problems, and raised tenants’ demands with Stadt und Land management. Activists knocking on doors could build on this record, while proposing political solutions like a rent cap and the socialization of private housing corporations. They could also address fears of the AfD by referring to successful actions like the one on September 2, when AfD officials planned to take allies from Austria’s far-right Freedom Party (FPÖ) on a tour of the multicultural adjoining main street, the Karl-Marx-Straße. Left-wing groups and Die Linke activists got wind of the plans and followed them in the street chanting “All of Berlin hates the AfD!” forcing them to leave under police escort. The 25 to 30 percentage point gains in blocks like the Rollbergviertel, where Die Linke reached shares up to 55 percent, cannot be explained without reference to such sustained efforts. However, the increasing strength of Die Linke Neukölln and the party’s left wing is also the reason that attacks are now being concentrated on this Berlin district. Their resolve under intense pressure should be an inspiration for the entire party not to give in but to fight for the real change Berliners voted for. Turnaround Die Linke’s activity in Neukölln offers a window into the turnaround in the party’s fortunes. Die Linke was founded in 2007, when the eastern-centered Party of Democratic Socialism merged with a left-wing split from the SPD called the Labor and Social Justice – the Electoral Alternative (WASG). At the time, the Neukölln branch of Die Linke numbered around two hundred members. Operating in a district of what was once West Berlin, the Neukölln branch had little weight in the Berlin party compared to the large ex-PDS branches in the former East Germany, where membership numbered in the thousands and Die Linke could count on a huge share of the vote. It’s important to realize that Die Linke has been in the Berlin government before, albeit as a junior partner. The eastern branches made up the constituencies of the politicians who formed the SPD-PDS/Die Linke governments in Berlin from 2001–11 after the post-reunification fiscal crisis and the Berlin banking scandal of 2001. These governments took measures like the firesale of 70,000 council homes of the public housing corporation GSW and slashing the pay of Berlin public sector workers. Such moves haunt the Berlin party still. The ensuing disillusionment led to the net loss of more than three thousand members between 2001 and 2011 and halved the 22.6 percent vote share the PDS had reached in Berlin, even though it made gains elsewhere in Germany. The Neukölln district party made a point of being critical of the neoliberalism and austerity in which the party had engaged in government. Here the founding of Die Linke in Neukölln involved dozens of revolutionary socialist activists — mainly from Marx21, a reformation of Linksruck — joined a few years later by the Sozialistische Alternative (SAV). The anti-Stalinist Marxist groups in the Berlin WASG brought with them their experience from social struggles against the neoliberal reforms of Gerhard Schröder’s SPD-led government, the anti-Nazi street blockades of the 1990s, and the student strikes against tuition fees, leading the branch into the social movements. The district party set up four local branches ( Basisorganisationen , BOs) which all elected branch organizers. The BOs started organizing regular paper and flyer stalls and regular meetings for prepared discussions on current events, strategy, and theory. Members would and still do prepare their own input or would arrange for an external speaker from a local campaign, strike, or movement. During discussions, a moderator ensures time limits, encourages women and inter-, transgender, and nonbinary people to speak, and can award priority on the speakers list. “Basisorganisationen” as Drivers of Socialist Activism The BOs also plan a wide array of activism. Members coordinate their presence at demonstrations and visits to picket lines, but also educationals, reading groups, and conferences. Throughout the 2010s, they also devoted time to signature drives for referendum campaigns like Deutsche Wohnen & Co Enteignen (resulting in the 2021 popular vote to nationalize the big landlords) or 100% Tempelhofer Feld, alongside Palestine, Greece, and refugee solidarity work. Die Linke gained members by being supportive and effective: politicizing the issues but being careful not to co-opt existing campaigns. In cooperation with the Neukölln board, BOs also mobilize for major direct actions in Germany like blockades of AfD party conferences; climate actions at lignite mines; protests against antiabortionist marches by fundamentalist Christians; and demonstrations against NATO or G7 summits. At the same time, Marx21 comrades less involved in the branch played a leading role in the Ver.di union’s drive at the Vivantes Klinikum Neukölln, culminating in the massive hospital strike of 2021. Other practical-minded Die Linke members focused on organizing tenants in the south of the neighborhood around their issues. Die Linke in Neukölln became a training ground for all-around socialist activism attuned to Marxist theory and regular practical discussions. This alternative model was notably attractive to an anti-capitalist milieu in Berlin that had recently learned to be wary of political parties. Importantly, the branch calls frequent meetings of the district assembly (MVV) every three months or so, where all members can attend and discuss the way forward, while electing a board and delegates to Berlin and national party conferences. In the spirit of full democracy, ample time is devoted to discussions, proposing amendments, and putting motions to a vote. Steady Growth The overall result was a steady growth of the Neukölln branch, from 230 members in 2008 to 600 in 2020, with the percentage of women also rising from 26 to 38 percent. The party’s vote here also rose, from 5.6 percent in the 2009 Berlin elections to 14.3 percent in 2021, surpassing Die Linke’s Berlin average for the first time. These federal elections saw much higher results in Neukölln, up from 13.9 percent in 2009 to 18.2 percent in 2017, a clear indication of discontent of Die Linke voters with the Berlin government. The gains were concentrated in the urban, multicultural north with lesser success in the more conservative suburbs in the south. The Crisis in Die Linke Nationally The late 2010s to early 2020s saw a long crisis in Die Linke, with a conservative, sovereigntist faction around leading member Sahra Wagenknecht eventually splitting away. Die Linke’s federal vote share sunk to 4.9 percent in the 2021 federal elections — almost enough to knock it out of parliament — and it suffered heavy losses also in Neukölln (down to 11.9 percent) and Berlin in general (11.4 percent). Die Linke’s membership shrunk by 20 percent federally and by 10 percent in Berlin between 2017 and 2023. Revolutionary organizations in the branch also experienced ruptures. The Sozialistische Organisation Solidarität (Sol) split from SAV in 2019. A significant number of central, founding members of the Neukölln branch first left the Marx21 network to found Sozialismus von Unten (SVU) and then left Die Linke in the course of 2024, leading up to the expulsion of the Palestinian-German SVU member Ramsis Kilani despite the protest of Die Linke Neukölln. Still, the branch maintained its membership and level of activity. The fact that Wagenknecht split away also opened the door for anti-racists, LGBTQ activists, ecologically minded people, and people worried about Wagenknecht’s Russia-friendly stance. The anti-capitalist foundations of the Neukölln branch, too, helped members focus on the long-term strategic goal of building the branch and the movements rather than despair at the lack of immediate electoral success. Palestine Solidarity After the October 7 attacks and Israel’s turn to all-out genocide, Palestine solidarity became central, also for the many new members in Die Linke Neukölln. The district is home to the biggest Palestinian diaspora in Europe and many others from the entire Middle East and North Africa, as well as mostly young Israeli and non-Israeli Jews. As riot cops raided Neukölln’s Arab shopping streets and went after the Palestine solidarity movement with full force, the government announced blanket bans on demonstrations. Amnesty and Human Rights Watch issued worried reports about worsening democratic rights in Germany. Palestinian school children were humiliated for wearing keffiyehs or the Palestinian flag. One seventeen-year-old reported at a local Die Linke meeting that her teacher ripped her red-green-black wrist bracelet off and threw it away in front of the entire class, claiming, “Palestinians don’t exist.” School protests were banned , like at the Ernst-Abbe-Gymnasium on the Sonnenallee. The Berlin government sanctioned schools to ban signs of Palestinian identity and funded the classroom distribution of the revisionist pamphlet Mythos#Israel1948 by the Zionist group Masiyot. Die Linke Neukölln already had good relations with the Palestinian community, having — unlike much of the German left — long proven its solidarity. The Neukölln council fraction, headed since 2021 by Palestinian-German civil rights lawyer Ahmed Abed, successfully pressured the mayor to retract the Mythos#Israel1948 pamphlet from schools. Since 2024, the Neukölln branch has also continued to intervene in Die Linke nationally to start a campaign to stop weapons exports to Israel and to call out the genocide. In defiance of ever more vicious smear campaigns in the national press and politicians from all other parties, Vedi Emde (now a Die Linke member of the Berlin parliament) organized what turned out to be huge public meetings on Gaza and Berlin’s biggest Palestine solidarity demonstration, with 70,000 people , on September 27, 2025. Comeback At the same time, the national party abruptly found its way back from the abyss. In summer 2024, Friedrich Merz’s Christian Democrats (CDU) broke a previous taboo by seeking majorities together with the AfD to combat migrants and asylum seekers, prompting a huge wave of protests. Die Linke was now able to catch a wave of progressive, anti-fascist indignation, prompting a wave of young, often female and increasingly also migrant members showing up to meetings. Olaf Scholz’s SPD-led coalition government fell in November 2024, leading to snap elections Die Linke was hardly prepared for, in Neukölln or anywhere else. But Die Linke Neukölln saw a chance to put the party’s first ever directly elected candidate in the Bundestag (parliament) in the former West Germany, Ferat Koçak, through a massive door-knocking campaign. The ad hoc campaign group mobilized over two thousand party members and sympathizers, reaching 139,000 doors, and was a wild success with 25.2 percent of the party vote and 30 percent for the individual candidate. By the end of February 2025, Neukölln branch had doubled its membership to over two thousand, winning a thousand mostly young, mostly female, and ever more diverse members over the course of months. After the election, the Neukölln board and local district-level groups were overwhelmed by the growth in new membership and invested huge efforts into contacting them all, organizing welcome and orientation meetings, and resorting the existing structures. They involved new members in preparing meetings and actions, mapped the issues and forms of activity that members were most interested in, and got people started in their group. This year, the branch has grown further to 2,600 members and ten self-organizing BOs. The district party has grown younger as well, with half of the increasingly multiethnic membership under thirty-five and achieved gender parity in 2025. The election campaign was better prepared and managed to integrate the door-knocking campaign with flyer stalls, rallies, and street events. Actions were organized by each local candidate’s volunteer teams and local BO structures and supported by two full-time staffers hired for campaign logistics and coordination. The result was an unprecedented 33 percent of the vote, verging on an absolute majority in the northern half of Neukölln. That is a powerful confirmation of a desire for a real change and a break with the politics of austerity for the sake of militarism, and of official appeasement of the fascists. It is also a vote of trust in an organization that is widely known by now as a mass socialist activist party not afraid to stick to its principles despite sustained smear campaigns. After the Election This September’s election victory in Berlin caught the ruling classes and the media off guard, prompting them to go on the warpath against Die Linke in a way that dwarfs the outrage over the AfD landslides just two weeks prior. Die Linke Neukölln has been a primary target, with Green national coleader Franziska Brantner saying on election night that her party wouldn’t govern with “antisemites from Neukölln.” The fabricated accusations of Jew hate, mixed up with wild conspiracy theories about Neukölln Bundestag member Ferat Koçak’s unfortunate minor contacts with a Lebanese crime boss, further increased pressure on the branch and hurt Ferat’s standing. More attacks of this kind can be expected in the attempt to derail Die Linke and put it on the defensive during coalition negotiations. At the last district assembly this past Tuesday, Die Linke Neukölln made clear that it will not be intimidated and will not give in to the attacks. The membership unanimously passed a motion defending all members caught in the crosshairs, including the newly elected Berlin parliament member Vedi Emde, Neukölln Councilor-elect Basem Said, and the national speaker of the Die Linke Palestine working group, Hannah Bruns, against whom expulsion procedures are underway. However, this past Friday, the Berlin-wide Die Linke leadership chose to cave in to the slanders against the Neukölln branch by approving a joint declaration with the SPD and Greens affirming Israel’s “right to exist” and the International Holocaust Remembrance Alliance definition of antisemitism while banning the Boycott, Divestment, and Sanctions (BDS) movement. The text blandly conflates Israel, Zionism, and Jewish life in Berlin. The parties also agreed to ramp up secret-service surveillance and policing to “recognize and combat antisemitic threats early” while not sparing one word for the violence, virulent racism, and outright pariah status that Palestinians experience in Berlin, nor indeed the countless Jews fed up with being used as a prop for a genocidal apartheid state. This declaration is an act of self-humiliation. There was no objective reason for it, other than to use the situation to discipline the left flank of Die Linke and facilitate a neutered coalition government. The now publicly confirmed threats to exclude Emde, who gained 46.2 percent of the candidate vote alongside 50.2 percent for the party-list vote in her district, prove the point. The leadership is trying to appease the SPD and Greens by forcing us in Neukölln to humiliate ourselves and destroy our solidarity with the Palestinian community. They would rather decimate the party and its roots in communities and movements than risk an escalation of the fight by defending our massively successful branch. Rather than be a Bally Bagayoko — the France Insoumise mayor of Saint-Denis, who used his own body to protect protesting high schoolers from the police — party leaders are throwing the pesky kids under the bus. Oppositional motions on the Berlin board of Die Linke were rejected by a majority this past Friday. But this isn’t what the Neukölln branch, the left wing of the party more widely, or countless new, mostly young members signed up for. We campaigned for real change, to challenge the ruling classes, and for the right to protest. The fight for the soul of the party is on. And with the threat of fascism looming large, we cannot mess this up. The one-ounce scoop of peanut butter served with two pieces of white bread and a one-ounce serving of canned fruit I received at breakfast failed to provide me with sufficient calories for the energy required to complete my task. A Portrait of Eugene Debs for Today’s Left

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: Jacobin ／ 著者: Matthew E. Stanley ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T17:25:32+00:00

You can tell the capitalist system is in trouble when Americans start talking about Eugene Debs. Although the popular image of the great labor and socialist leader has ebbed and flowed since his death a century ago this month, our New Gilded Age has placed him back in the spotlight. Progressive politicians and labor activists, including United Auto Workers President Shawn Fain and union organizer Jaz Brisack, quote his speeches with regularity. Last year, Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez traveled to Debs’s home in Terre Haute, Indiana, to introduce Bernie Sanders, who has long cited the Hoosier firebrand as a political hero. And when Zohran Mamdani kicked off his mayorship of the nation’s largest city, he did so by quoting Debs . Such tributes signify a wider movement. The worldview that Debs championed is more popular now than at any other point since his death, with polls suggesting that Americans, especially young people and people of color, view socialism more positively than they ever have. The Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), the institutional descendant of Debs’s Socialist Party of America, recently surpassed 120,000 members to become the largest socialist organization in US history, and more than 250 members currently serve in elected positions across forty states. In that respect, Shawn Gude’s American Socialist: The Life and Legacy of Eugene V. Debs could hardly be more timely. This charmingly penned and prodigiously researched study both refreshes familiar stories and prods readers to reconsider common assumptions. For instance, Gude devotes more pages than previous biographers to black socialism and Debs’s racial thinking, revealing less a stodgy, dogmatic relic of the (very) Old Left than a flawed yet often versatile rebel who actively and continuously widened his personal understanding of solidarity. In doing so, American Socialist complicates the erroneous notion of Debs as a rigid and unchanging determinist whose outlook failed to reckon with the complexities of race and class. Gude’s goal in American Socialist is not to supplant previous — and still eminently useful — biographies by Ray Ginger, Bernard Brommel, and Nick Salvatore, but to “bring Debs’s story to a new generation and a wider audience.” Gude has indeed crafted a popular biography for our times — a compelling portrait of “one of America’s great critics of plutocracy: a man who clambered into capitalism’s boiler room, gazed at its machinery, and announced it was despotic to the core.” From Respectability to Radicalism Eugene Debs wasn’t born a radical. He arrived at socialism through sobering experience — with corporate power, capitalist courts, and the daily injustices of living and laboring in Gilded Age America. Born in 1855 to Alsatian immigrants in Terre Haute, he joined the railroad at age fourteen as the nation hurtled toward buccaneer capitalism. Thrown out of work during the economic depression of 1873, Debs soon became an organizer in the Brotherhood of Locomotive Firemen, using his rising position to oppose strikes, echo the “law and order” rhetoric of his bosses, and espouse the Brotherhood’s conservative craft union credo of “benevolence, sobriety, industry.” He went so far as to deliver a speech defaming the Great Railroad Strike for its “anarchy and revolution.” At decade’s end, Debs made his first foray into electoral politics, first as a Democratic city clerk, then a state representative. He proved a moderate labor reformer, respectable and accommodating to business. Notably, he broke with his party in an unsuccessful effort to eliminate racial distinctions from state law but remained decidedly antiradical (even sponsoring a pro-militia bill). Imbued with a Great Man view of history and belief in the power of individual fortitude, he then rose quickly within his whites-only union. Unlike many trades leaders, Debs didn’t openly scapegoat black workers. But nor did he use his position to challenge the color line. Rather, he avoided the subject of race entirely out of either indifference or worry that it would repel white workers. Young Debs’s activism was defined by what Gude calls “parlor room reason,” not organized insurgency. But class struggle and the violent obstinance of capital soon wrenched Debs out of complacency. Unrewarded by loyalty to the employer class, Debs led his first strike in 1888 against the Chicago, Burlington and Quincy Railroad. He emerged a changed man, embracing broad federation over craft exclusivism and later eschewing the two-party system. Through a new consciousness that drew from the abolitionists and the Knights of Labor, he became active in the Populist movement and was further stirred by anti-worker violence at Homestead and elsewhere. By the time he founded the American Railway Union (ARU) in 1893 and orchestrated the Pullman Strike the following year, Debs was convinced of the permanent structural conflict between owners and workers. The story of Debs’s first incarceration and radicalization is familiar to readers on the Left, but Gude delivers it with both rigor and style. Like so many revolutionaries, Debs underwent his greatest transformation while in prison. Abandoned by Samuel Gompers and his American Federation of Labor (AFL) craft unions, Debs devoured books by Edward Bellamy and Laurence Gronlund. He read Karl Kautsky and Karl Marx, too. As Gude puts it, the Pullman Strike constituted an almost spiritual awakening, “a baptism . . . out of which a new Debs was born. He was now, and would forever be, a radical working-class agitator.” The Debs Formula Debs soon declared himself a socialist, one committed to unions and not utopian schemes. Believing that a workers’ republic would soon dawn (after all, how could the sharp contradictions and profound inequalities of industrial America persist?), Debs embraced the “political socialism” of organizing a working-class party to contest elections, popularize socialist ideas, and act as the political arm of the labor movement. He learned a new language of Marxism, then distilled it for Oklahoma farmers, New York garment women, Montana copper miners, and Louisiana sharecroppers. Fueled by a mix of radical Christianity and American history, he charged through nonstop cross-country tours spanning five presidential campaigns. As he advocated a big tent that was both ballot- and strike-conscious, the question of how to win immediate reforms with the ultimate goal of winning socialism defined Debs’s crusade for the next quarter century. Debs’s party made socialism a force in American politics for the first time in US history, but political mobilization and governance proved grueling. Intraparty rifts emerged along class, strategic, and regional lines. Debs felt that conservatives, led by Victor Berger, “diluted the revolutionary aims” with their fixation on electability. But unlike his future Industrial Workers of the World (IWW) compatriot Bill Haywood, he couldn’t imagine a democratic revolution occurring in the United States without attaining power through the ballot box. At times, it appeared that the popular Debs was the only personality holding it all together. As an independent party (and not the electoral arm of unions, like the British Labour Party), the Socialist Party (SP) likewise ran afoul of Gompers, who eagerly supported the two capitalist parties. Debs walked what Gude describes as the tightrope of “how to press mainstream organized labor away from exclusionary positions without isolating the Socialist Party.” At times, Debs seemed to believe that, if he only attacked AFL bureaucrats strenuously enough, their rank and file would bolt toward socialist reform. The SP also struggled at times to forge a coherent vision. According to Gude, Debs “never laid out precisely what a democratic socialist economy would look like.” While railroads, steel mills, coal mines, and other large industries would be publicly owned and subject to democratic oversight, Debs never outlined the precise role of workers, cooperatives, small firms, or markets within his envisioned socialist state. Like many fellow socialists, Debs thought it premature or potentially divisive to formulate a specific blueprint for life after capitalism. Although its electoral achievements were remarkable, the party stalled. Debs received nearly a million votes while running from prison in 1920, but that was proportionally fewer than his 1912 tally. Gude details how many working-class voters couldn’t bring themselves to back Debs for a host of reasons, from party loyalty and patronage to simple political viability. “Debs was still a long shot, even if they believed in his ideals,” he reminds, “and, of course, plenty didn’t.” For all of Debs’s energy and magnetism, after two decades the SP seemed pressed hard against an electoral ceiling. Why? Much of the answer lay in the party’s basic lack of resources and machinery. Violent state repression during World War I exacerbated those disadvantages, leading many socialists to shun electoral democracy in favor of revolutionary action. But the answer also lay in the herculean — and still unsettled — task of how to unite the world’s most diverse working class. Something to Offer? Like socialists today, Debs encountered the problem of how to organize workers who were deeply atomized by race, gender, age, ethnicity, language, region, religion, and national origin. The SP’s default position was to maintain that capitalism exploited all workers equally, advocate for “race-neutral” goals, and insist that racism would resolve itself naturally through material struggle. A capacious movement naturally required making space for all kinds of disciples, but socialists adhered to an unofficial white standard, viewing active anti-racism as undesirable either in principle or because it would supposedly fracture their fragile movement. Scholars and activists have spent a century critiquing what Philip S. Foner termed this “Debsian view” of racial evasion. That critique originated with black leftists at the time, who underscored the SP’s shortcomings on the race question (convinced of the party’s unseriousness on race, Hubert Harrison resigned his membership in 1914). During the sectarian battles of the 1920s and ’30s, Communist Party theoreticians weaponized Debs’s (often decontextualized) statements to discredit the SP by exposing its complicity with white supremacy. The result was, at times, a caricature of the Socialists’ alleged racial indifference. Debs and other ARU leaders in prison in Woodstock, Illinois, in 1895. (Railway Times) Historians since have offered greater nuance. Lamenting the SP’s failure “to adopt a specialized program for the Negro people,” Ray Ginger saw the party as immobilized by “Berger’s prejudice,” on the one hand, and “Debs’s oversimplified analysis” on the other. Later critics, especially in the New Left, cited Debs’s more feeble or decontextualized statements (“We have nothing special to offer the Negro, and we cannot make separate appeals to all the races”) as proof of his inadequacy or indifference, but others recognized Debs as moving personally toward anti-racist practice. Nick Salvatore traced how Debs’s racial mindset “changed over the course of his career” from a “one-dimensional” outlook toward, by the end of his life, “a fundamental critique of white American culture itself.” Gude’s account enriches this discussion tremendously by balancing the deficiencies of both Debs and the SP more broadly against myriad external limitations and near-impossible party dynamics. Utilizing new sources and subtle insights into black socialism, American Socialist complicates Ginger’s racist vs. apathetic binary and vitalizes Salvatore’s conclusions. It does so by situating Debs’s transforming views alongside African American politics and SP imperatives. There was little in Debs’s upbringing — one of racial segregation in southern Indiana — to suggest that he would become a racial equalitarian, much less an opponent of racial injustice. That metamorphosis came slowly and through material struggle. As early as the 1890s, Debs leveraged his position as president of the American Railway Union to challenge the union’s whites-only code, perhaps out of personal conviction, but clearly as a matter of union interest. When some delegates defended racial restriction because black workers had been used as strikebreakers (and avowed that Debs himself would be hanged for such an offense in the South), Debs cited that as the best reason to welcome them into the union. His speeches in defense of black membership sometimes reflected popular prejudices (he did not “advocate association with the negro,” for example), and his inclusionary position went down in narrow defeat, but progressive black newspapers praised his efforts. Although Debs as an early socialist trafficked in lazy generalizations and pushed an overly narrow “class-first” logic, such as in his 1903 assertion, “There is no ‘Negro problem’ apart from the general labor problem,” Gude leaves little doubt that the Hoosier firebrand was “a radical on race for his day.” Racial conservatives within the SP certainly “had no difficulty seeing the radicalism of Debs’s overall positions,” the author contends, which sometimes alienated him from other white socialists. And when turn-of-the-century black publications criticized Debs, they did so “invariably from the Right, with an attitude of Booker T. Washington–style accommodationism.” There was more to come. Chronic race riots, especially in his native Indiana, inspired Debs to write more prodigiously on the “race question.” He assailed state SP platforms that included Jim Crow clauses, publicly attacked popular racist literature, and famously refused to speak at venues that excluded African Americans. As his worldview solidified, what Gude calls the “bigoted tics that had previously seeped into his speeches” became less common; he grew more vocal, and his race thinking became more sophisticated. Debs also learned from the black left. Critically, that engagement coincided with the turn against the self-help and entrepreneurial approach of Booker T. Washington, whom Debs allies such as George Washington Woodbey assailed as “a good servant of capitalism.” Influenced by African American delegates William Costley, a California trade unionist, and two Indiana coal miners, John H. Adams and Edward D. McKay, the 1901 SP founding convention adopted a six-paragraph resolution stating that owners sought to foment “race hatred between the white worker and the black” and “betray the negro in his present helpless struggle against disfranchisement and violence.” Pinpointing racism as a national, union-inhibiting dilemma and viewing black support as essential to any workers’ movement worthy of the name, Debs looked South. In a 1903 organizing drive through Dixie, he “hoped to peel off trade union members and former Populists” through class-based interracial appeals that flouted regional norms. Likely inspired by W. E. B. Du Bois’s The Souls of Black Folk , published that same year, Debs addressed the problem of white identity. Like Du Bois, he recognized the irony by which the region’s poor whites, “themselves the foul product of the capitalist system,” were “held in lowest contempt by the master class, yet esteeming themselves immeasurably above the cleanest, most intelligent and self respecting negro.” Walking back his former “color-blind” approach and earlier claims regarding the identical condition of white and black wage earners, Debs acknowledged that black workers were “doubly enslaved.” Debs’s most rapid evolution occurred during the mid- to late 1910s. Influenced by activists of color and pressured by demographic change that saw black workers (and potential voters) begin settling in Northern cities, white socialists as a whole became more attentive to race. It was a matter of both political opportunity and ideological sincerity. Socialists had long dispatched black speakers including Woodbey into black communities while using emancipationist language to liken Debs to John Brown, but they slowly codified that racial progressivism into the party line. By 1918, a new SP platform condemned “hanging and burning; widespread political disfranchisement, and loss of civil rights” in the South and demanded that Dixie comply with the Fourteenth Amendment. Debs continued to drive home the old idea of statutory equality (“The Socialist Party knows neither color, creed, sex, nor race”) but now occasionally jumped into the civil rights fray, even when he had seemingly little to gain electorally. He joined a public campaign to save a black Arkansas teenager named Earl Gilchrist from the death penalty in 1911; four years later, he walked a picket line with black Terre Hauteans to protest D. W. Griffith’s white supremacist blockbuster, The Birth of a Nation . Such efforts drew praise from black leaders including Du Bois and Ida B. Wells, who invited Debs to address her Negro Fellowship League. Meanwhile, Debs befriended black Socialists including Ross D. Brown, Chandler Owen, and A. Philip Randolph, who championed the Hoosier in his radical newspaper, The Messenger . Their growing influence became a part of what Gude describes as Debs’s broader “reassessment on race” predicated on “more immediate measures to combat racial oppression.” Gude details how Debs’s racial education continued until his death and encompassed the cultural elements of white supremacy. In one of his final columns, Debs covered the Osage Indian murders in Oklahoma, deploring the killings and mocking the idea of the “White Man’s Burden.” He exhausted some of the last political energies of his life collaborating with Randolph, a close comrade and future civil rights elder whose Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters, according to Gude, had become a “living embodiment of Eugene Debs’s emancipatory dream.” Debs’s racial views were hardly perfect, his language sometimes problematic, particularly as an early socialist. But as various black reformers noted and as Gude rightly maintains, it is undeniably significant that at the pinnacle of Jim Crow — as social scientists concocted new racial taxonomies, Yankee imperialists proclaimed “survival of the fittest,” and prominent race leaders urged social deference — Debs was the only national politician advocating the “obliteration of the color line.” The Limits of Equality But if Debs evolved toward a deeper understanding of the layered oppressions facing workers of color, why didn’t the Socialist Party adopt an actively anti-racist program? Why didn’t the foremost white economic justice advocates of their day attempt to organize black workers at the point of production, launching organizing drives into the Southern fields to recruit miners and sharecroppers (in the vein of the Communist Party)? Why didn’t the early SP systematically campaign against lynching or racism in housing, education, transportation, or the courts? The answer is manifold, involving a combination of indifference, strategic and ideological missteps, structural constraints, and open hostility. First, as Gude illustrates in detailed and dramatic terms, the party was perpetually fractured between racial progressives and racial reactionaries. Debs’s views represented one such camp, but he never possessed a stranglehold over the entire party or its competing factions (in fact, by 1904 Debs was already retreating from party policymaking). To further confuse the issue, racism/anti-racism didn’t always map neatly onto other right/left divides within the party. For example, SP activist William English Walling helped found the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People (NAACP) but ended up supporting US involvement in World War I. Moreover, even progressives like Debs largely failed, especially in the party’s first decade, to articulate how SP reforms would undermine racism, perhaps seeing a direct confrontation with Jim Crow as what Gude calls “a diversion from Socialists’ fundamental battle.” Debs’s own statements during this time proved contradictory. He spoke to the compounded oppression of the “doubly enslaved” only to then misguidedly state that “when the working class have triumphed in the class struggle . . . the race problem will disappear.” There were also practical intraparty constraints. The Socialists were a small, cash-poor party, and many members saw expensive and potentially dangerous organizing campaigns as infeasible. Debs likewise commanded overwhelmingly white audiences, which made his message of equality noteworthy but also placed functional limits upon it. The party relied on dollars and support from the bona fide white supremacists who were an everyday presence within the party’s big tent (as late as 1912, the peak year of SP dues-paying membership, the convention featured a report that argued racism “is not so much a result of social as of biological evolution”). Banner for the Socialist Party of America’s presidential ticket of 1904: Eugene V. Debs for president and Ben Hanford for vice president. (Bettmann / Getty Images) Perhaps most critically, broader social and cultural conditions were distinct — and more challenging to multiracial organizing — during Debs’s peak influence. In the first two decades of the twentieth century, racially progressive Socialists grappling with the “race problem” in a nation barreling away from racial equality were fighting a rising tide. In most ways, according to Gude, “the space to organize had closed as the noose of Jim Crow tightened.” The great tragedy of early socialism is that Debs’s personal anti-racism never translated into a concrete movement to recruit black workers within the SP. A Growing Man Eugene Debs was, as the abolitionist Horace Greeley once referred to Abe Lincoln, a “growing man.” More than previous biographers, Gude illuminates the ways Debs never stopped learning, sharpening his socialist perspective, or recalibrating and expanding his conception of solidarity. From hard compromises within the ARU to his cofounding of the IWW to pondering the formation of an “American Labor Party,” Debs pursued a never-ending quest of how best to empower workers and win socialism. American Socialist evocatively highlights this ongoing political education. As socialism brought Debs into a global movement, he progressed from a Democrat who supported immigrant exclusion as the solution to labor competition to a staunch internationalist who championed labor organization abroad and insisted that US workers had far more in common with toilers of other countries than with their own elites. Drawing on personal experience, Debs likewise became an ardent critic of the carceral state, deeming prisons a form of “torture” rather than rehabilitation. And although he had always backed women’s suffrage, his gender views and allyships radicalized with time as well. According to Gude, Debs was eternally “finding another injustice to rail against” and “never seemed to see one without immediately dedicating himself to fighting it.” Flexible transformation meant that Debs wore a cast of rotating hats, often at the same time: a humanist whose politics were imbued with Christian symbolism; a world citizen who presented socialism as entirely consistent with American political traditions; and a racial equalitarian whose words and policy proposals often fell short of his ideals even as he moved toward anti-racism — a theme that is one of the book’s most compelling features. Indeed, Gude’s attention to race vis-à-vis earlier biographers evinces how leftists have long grappled with the precise relation between racism and capitalism, including how to undertake class-struggle anti-racism in moments of comparative decline. As in Debs’s time, US workers today — run ragged by rising costs and crushing workloads, disciplined through scarcity discourses and techno-authoritarian surveillance, and isolated through remote and gig labor — appear, at times, almost hopelessly segmented. Owners use religious, generational, and immigration issues to pit blocs of workers against one another. Politicians and media elites leverage various identities to subvert discussions of inequality, labor rights, and material redistribution. Meanwhile, nonreactionary alternatives, including (an easily commodified) liberal “anti-racism,” aggravate the issue by ignoring class politics, absolving capital, and reducing racism to a problem of individual attitudes or cultural representation. Debs’s life, in Gude’s capable hands, exposes these difficulties of multiracial organizing in conditions of anti-worker propagandization and weak structural power for labor. Although he traces Debs’s legacy from the formation of the Congress of Industrial Organizations and the Highlander Folk School to Martin Luther King Jr’s opposition to the Vietnam War, Gude suggests that the “Debsian spirit” has never held more purchase than it does in our current New Gilded Age. Importantly, then, this captivating book isn’t for socialists so much as it is for those who, exposed to the injustices of today, relate to Debs’s expanding consciousness and sense of indignation. It’s for any emerging critic of our wildly undemocratic economy — one that is fixed for the ultrarich while the seemingly powerless majority languish in exploitation and unaffordable and unresponsive systems in housing, education, and health care. It’s for all who recognize that capitalism is failing to deliver on its promise of freedom. Debs’s politics were, at their brightest, tenacious in undercutting what Gude calls “all forms of unaccountable power — rich over poor, men over women, white over Black, colonizer over colonized — as poisonous to human freedom.” A page-turning delight, American Socialist is an engaging and valuable tool for anyone who seeks to win a world where that kind of freedom reigns supreme. This article originally appeared in Jacobin , a democratic socialist magazine publishing long-form essays and analysis on politics, economics, and culture. Subscribe to the print edition for $20 a year.

Trump corruption affordability crisis

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Robert Reich ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T16:55:29+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. In these last weeks before the midterms, Democratic candidates have been gaining ground by talking about two things: the affordability crisis and political corruption. If Democrats regain control of Congress and genuinely want to respond to affordability, they’ll need to do everything possible to root out the corruption. The two are inseparable. In large part, prices are rising faster than paychecks because so much of the economy has become subject to political bribery, Trump Republican pay-to-play schemes, and kickbacks to Trump and his cronies. 1. Trump’s war and the corruption underlying it Trump’s war on Iran is causing Americans to spend almost 50 percent more on gas than before the war, on average. And the diesel fuel needed to transport almost everything else we buy now averages more than $6.00 a gallon, which is also being passed on to consumers. These price increases due to Trump’s war tell only half the story. The other half is the political corruption at the root of the war — enriching Trump’s friends and family. The war has been a huge windfall for Big Tech supplying the military — such as Palantir, a core artificial intelligence and data-integration partner for the U.S. Department of Defense, founded by Trump and JD Vance’s buddy Peter Thiel, and Elon Musk. Musk’s SpaceX reportedly quintupled its connection fees for the Pentagon, from $5,000 per terminal to $25,000 per terminal for terminals integrated into U.S. “kamikaze” (LUCAS) drones deployed in the conflict. Not incidentally, Musk has quietly funneled more than $275 million to Republican candidates in the midterms, making him by far the largest single donor to either party. Meanwhile, investment companies backed by Donald Trump Jr. and Eric Trump have accumulated billions in Pentagon contracts and future contract options, according to the government’s official contract reporting. (Congressional Democrats continue to demand that the Pentagon’s inspector general investigate the approved deals.) Related: AI Backlash Fuels a New American Economic Populism 2. Trump’s tariffs and the corruption beneath them Trump’s tariffs offer another example of higher prices connected to corruption. The tariffs are import taxes that have increased annual household costs by an estimated $1,100, according to The Budget Lab at Yale. Again, though, the price increases tell only half the story. Trump’s tariffs have enabled Trump to give out tariff waivers and carve-outs to industries and companies that do him favors. After Tim Cook, Apple’s CEO, donated $1 million to Trump’s inauguration and promised major domestic investments, Apple got tariff exemptions for imported smartphones, computers, semiconductors, and flat panel displays, all of which saved Apple huge amounts of money. After Elon Musk sank a quarter of a billion dollars into Trump’s 2024 campaign, Tesla got a tariff exemption for electronics. Reyes Holdings, a bottler that ranks among the largest privately held companies in the U.S. and is owned by brothers who have donated millions to Republicans, got a tariff exemption for polyethylene terephthalate, used to make plastic bottles. And so on. 3. Trump’s promotion of AI and the corruption underlying it A third example linking higher prices to corruption is artificial intelligence. Together with the costs of Trump’s war in Iran, AI’s borrowing of over $500 billion so far this year has contributed to sky-high interest rates on mortgages, auto loans, and credit-card overdrafts. And AI data centers are raising electricity prices in areas where data centers are sucking up energy. As with the war and the tariffs, Trump’s support for AI is tied to political payoffs. Tech companies and their executives donated over $300 million to support Trump’s 2024 reelection efforts and MAGA ​Inc, the political action committee aligned with Trump. (This doesn’t include Musk’s more than a quarter billion dollars in donations to Republicans.) According to a CNBC analysis of FEC records , megadonor contributions from AI, crypto, and online gambling executives have overwhelmingly favored Republicans, with 14 of the 20 largest donor groups nearly exclusively backing GOP candidates or outside groups. To see how Trump’s war, his tariffs, and his support for AI interact on prices, consider housing affordability, which is among voters’ top concerns . While Trump’s war and encouragement of AI have contributed to the average 30-year fixed mortgage rate rising to over 7 percent, Trump’s tariffs have caused the prices of lumber and building materials to soar. The National Association of Home Builders estimates that tariffs on lumber and building materials have added $10,900 to the cost of constructing a new U.S. single-family home. The price of copper, a key material for electrical wiring in residential and commercial construction, has risen 40 percent in the past year due to Trump’s tariffs and the demands of data centers — adding even more cost to building a typical home, which requires hundreds of pounds of copper. Related: Hegseth Must Go 4. Trump’s giant tax cut for the rich and the corruption it rests on A final illustration of the close connection between rising prices and corruption is Trump’s tax cuts. Trump’s megadonors in the 2024 campaign demanded another big tax cut as a condition for their investments, which came in the form of Trump’s “Big Beautiful” (Big Ugly) bill. But the tax cuts have come at the expense of healthcare for average Americans. To pay for the tax cuts demanded by their largest donors , Trump and congressional Republicans have slashed Medicaid and failed to renew healthcare tax credits. As a result, millions of Americans are now forced to spend far more for health insurance coverage or do without it altogether. 5. The Democrats’ agenda if they retake control of Congress If Democrats regain control of Congress, they will need to focus not only on alleviating high prices but also on eliminating the causes of those high prices. It will be impossible to do the former without attacking the latter. In addition to ending Trump’s war and his arbitrary tariffs, they must reform campaign financing, and help reduce the influence of big money in politics. One method would be to match public financing with small-dollar donations. They’ll also need to resurrect the systems of accountability Trump has dismantled —the independent civil service, independent inspectors general, the Office of Government Ethics, and the Federal Election Commission. Under Trump and his Republican enablers, the richest Americans and biggest corporations have made out like bandits. Average working Americans are paying dearly for what the bandits have stolen. The grift and the graft must end. Robert Reich's Substack

Gen Z Protests Are Reshaping Asia

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Ruhi Bhasin ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T16:46:48+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. Asia has seen a surge of “Gen Z protests,” led by a generation tired of accepting the status quo and increasingly using social media to drive change in deeply entrenched political systems. In India, protests over the education system helped force the education minister’s resignation in 2026. Protests in Bangladesh and Nepal in 2024 and 2025, respectively, led to government changes. These protests have helped expose fractured political structures in these countries. Governments in several South and Southeast Asian countries have done little to address the economic distress and corruption plaguing them. The Gen Z citizens, who are facing the brunt of these crumbling systems, are increasingly demanding accountability and justice, using social media to fight back, organize, document events, and press their demands. Despite efforts to suppress them, these protests have brought political change. Whether these movements can sustain solidarity and organize beyond immediate demands to pursue lasting reforms remains to be seen. India In India, the Bharatiya Janata Party-led government has been in power for more than 12 years, having first been elected in 2014. Press freedom and other democratic safeguards have come under pressure during its tenure; Sweden’s V-Dem Institute has classified India as an “ electoral autocracy .” As stated by an August 2026 Time report , “Modi has consolidated his grip on all manner of national institutions in recent years: from the media , where pliant owners have repeatedly bent the knee to the administration, to election authorities , which in a recent state poll erased millions of names from voter rolls in a move widely seen as benefiting Modi’s Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP).” India has been facing increasing unemployment among its educated youth , even though the government has consistently maintained these claims to be false . The country’s youth have also been contending with an education system marked by repeated paper leaks. In May 2026, the National Eligibility cum Entrance Test for undergraduate medical admissions was canceled over paper-leak allegations , fueling anger over the examination system. Meanwhile, during the same month, the Supreme Court Chief Justice’s comparison of unemployed youth to “parasites” and “cockroaches” led to the formation of the Cockroach Janta Party (CJP) by Abhijeet Dipke , an Indian student from Boston University who graduated in 2025 with a master’s in public relations. The CJP’s Instagram account amassed 20 million followers within days, according to Tim e. Dipke eventually returned to India on June 6. Anger over the government’s mismanagement of the education system soon moved from social media to the streets as thousands of Gen Z showed up at Jantar Mantar, a popular protest site in India’s capital, New Delhi, with their main demand being the resignation of Education Minister Dharmendra Pradhan. As the protest gained momentum and spread, authorities resorted to force against demonstrators . Young protesters used social media to document events on the ground and responded with “ humor and grit .” For them, the protest wasn’t about any political party but rather about ensuring accountability for a broken education system . They finally succeeded in forcing Pradhan to resign on July 25, 2026. Related: Women Are on the Frontlines of the Climate Crisis—and the Frontlines of the Solution “In the end, the young cockroaches proved so resilient because Modi’s regime of control had no punishment or humiliation to threaten them with beyond what the system already inflicts on them every day. These young men and women had reached a point where they felt they had no voice in the existing order—and nothing left to lose,” explains the Time report. Allegations of links to foreign funding were also made against the movement, but the Ministry of External Affairs said it had “ no information ” to confirm these allegations. In July 2026, after the protest ended, the government introduced a bill to amend the public examinations law, and Modi announced an examination reform task force. Whether these actions succeed in reforming the education system and whether the CJP can keep pushing for their proper implementation remains to be seen. The CJP announced another campaign in September 2026 demanding chief election commissioner Gyanesh Kumar’s resignation over alleged manipulation of voter rolls. Street protests followed in October, with opposition parties and civil society groups participating. The Election Commission denies the allegations . These protests will test the CJP’s ability to sustain pressure and the authorities’ response to demands for electoral reform. The CJP has already split internally , resulting in the formation of the Cockroach Janta Party—Democratic. Nepal In September 2025, Gen Z protests erupted in Nepal after the government banned social media platforms that had not met its registration requirements, according to a Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health article . Gen Z saw this move to ban social media as an attempt to trample their freedom of expression, while the politicians were hoping to use their traditional tactics to also stop hashtags like #NepoKids or #YouthAgainstCorruption targeted against their children and the privileged life they led, in sharp contrast to the hardships being faced by ordinary youths daily. The government further shut down the internet, thinking that would muzzle the protests. However, they drastically underestimated the youth’s power and frustration. While the ban triggered the protests, several larger unresolved issues had been simmering for years, including unemployment, corruption, and nepotism, which strengthened the agitation. “In a country where 56 percent of the population is below the age of thirty, a geriatric leadership whose average age was seventy was woefully out of touch. In the last decade, internet connectivity had rapidly expanded to cover more than half the population. Young Nepalis were online—on Instagram, TikTok, Reddit, Discord—but offline, they saw none of the better future promised by the 2006 revolution against the monarchy. Instead, they saw widening inequality and corruption, and on their social media feeds, they saw the children of elites flaunting their privilege,” writes author Amish Raj Mulmi for the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace. The protesters demanded Prime Minister K.P. Sharma Oli’s resignation. He finally heeded their demands on September 9, 2025. Since democracy was restored in Nepal in 1990, the country has seen political churn, having witnessed “twenty-seven prime ministers, each averaging barely a year in office,” according to Mulmi. The government collapsed after the protests, leading to an interim government, with elections eventually taking place in March 2026. Balendra Shah’s Rastriya Swatantra Party won the election, and Shah became the prime minister. While the Gen Z movement has succeeded in changing the political system, the government’s inexperience is a critical roadblock as it tries to fix a dysfunctional system. It remains to be seen whether it can use the popular mandate to ensure stability. Deadly floods in August 2026 gave Shah’s government an early test of its ability to respond to a national crisis . “While early moves such as arresting former leaders and launching an ambitious reform agenda signal change, the government faces risks of internal fragmentation, personality-driven politics and bureaucratic resistance. Its success will depend on delivering economic opportunities, maintaining public trust, and translating its populist momentum into stable, rules-based governance,” writes Anil Sigdel, a lecturer at the University of Vienna, Austria, in a May 2026 article. The Gen Z revolts in Nepal reflect the dilemma faced by 21st-century democracy, according to the journal Society . “On the one hand, it exposes the exhaustion of a political order sustained by elite capture, corruption, and exclusion. On the other, it demonstrates the enduring vitality of democratic ideals, the capacity of ordinary citizens, and especially the young, to demand accountability, dignity, and change. The uprising of 2025 was not only a fleeting eruption of anger; it was also a generational awakening with decades of unfulfilled promises.” Mulmi argues that earlier generations in Nepal toppled governments without leading to lasting change. Whether the Gen Z movement will result in such reforms remains unclear. Bangladesh Bangladesh saw an uprising primarily led by student protesters in 2024, which was also called the July Uprising.
