The initial protests followed the court’s reinstatement of the quota system. “The protests began in early July as peaceful demands from university students to abolish quotas in civil service jobs—a third of these are reserved for relatives of veterans from Bangladesh’s war for independence from Pakistan in 1971,” according to the BBC . But the protests soon grew into a broader anti-government movement over unemployment and corruption, turning violent amid police crackdowns and clashes between demonstrators and student groups supporting Sheikh Hasina’s government. This was followed by curfews, shutting down the internet, and mass arrests . Related: Covid Crisis Mismanagement Increases Food Insecurity in India and Brazil Eventually, the government had to cave in, and Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina resigned and fled to India on August 5, 2024, after ruling the country for 15 years. The “weeks of bloody protests and brutal action by government forces killed nearly 1,400 people and wounded more than 20,000, according to the country’s International Criminal Tribunal (ICT),” stated Al Jazeera . The protests also prompted allegations of involvement by the United States, China, and Pakistan. BBC Monitoring documented these claims and the responses to them without establishing foreign involvement. In a commentary published by New Eastern Outlook, Brian Berletic argued that the uprising reflected a US-backed effort at regime change. Meanwhile, an interim government led by Nobel Peace Prize laureate Muhammad Yunus took power in Bangladesh after the protests. In October 2025, Yunus’s interim government introduced the nonbinding July National Charter that several major parties signed; Hasina’s banned Awami League was excluded from the process. The signing of the charter was met with protests as agitators “wanted the document to include more provisions for the activists who helped oust the previous government,” according to PBS . While the Yunus-led interim government received support initially, “the enthusiasm and aspiration for transformative change appears to have faded. Instead, the country now faces uncertainty, political fragmentation and instability,” states the Institute of South Asian Studies, adding that the initial uprising “laid bare the flaws of the existing political and socio-economic order by casting them in moral terms and promoting a collective stake in democracy. Through their expression of dissent, protesters sought to demonstrate that democracy is not a singular or monolithic entity confined to those in power.” Eventually, Tarique Rahman was sworn in as Bangladesh’s new prime minister in February 2026. He is already facing pressure to implement the promised changes as people took to the streets in September 2026 amid rising living costs and energy prices. Whether his government can deliver economic reforms remains an open question. Can the Gen Z Protests Lead to Long-Term Changes? In Nepal, the protests paved the way for new political actors to lead the country; in Bangladesh, the long-established Bangladesh Nationalist Party came to power, showing how similar protests can have different political outcomes. Political scientist Arijit Mazumdar contrasts how the two countries’ electoral systems absorbed the protests: “In Bangladesh, elections continued to exist, but confidence in electoral competition had weakened and established parties retained overwhelming organizational advantages. In Nepal, despite significant political instability and extra-constitutional developments, elections remained the broadly accepted mechanism through which political conflict was ultimately resolved. These differences shaped whether protest energy produced rupture without substantial electoral renewal or was absorbed into party competition.” Youth-led protests have also taken place in Indonesia and Madagascar, as well as in other parts of Africa. These protests have showcased the courage and determination of young people. Whether their demands lead to lasting change also depends on political organization and the institutions that carry out reforms. “[G]overning a state requires skills that no revolution alone can teach. … At the same time, examples like Thailand and Sri Lanka demonstrate that youth-led political transformation is not impossible. What distinguishes relatively successful cases is not merely public enthusiasm but organizational continuity. Political movements gradually evolved into structured parties, built alliances with civil society and engaged existing institutions rather than relying exclusively on street mobilization. Sustainable reform required more than revolutionary legitimacy,” writes H.M. Nazmul Alam for Scroll . The youth protests in Sri Lanka in 2022, known as the Aragalaya (struggle), resulted from a major political and economic crisis. The protests helped force Sri Lankan President Gotabaya Rajapaksa’s resignation in July 2022 ; the National People’s Power (NPP), led by President Anura Kumara Dissanayake, formed a government in 2024. Under his rule, some positive economic changes have taken place, but promises of a “system change” take time and vision . Social media has helped young protesters bypass traditional media gatekeepers, amplify their grievances, and build solidarity. But visibility and a change of leaders do not, on their own, remake political institutions. The different outcomes in India, Nepal, and Bangladesh show why the real test of these movements extends beyond the protests. As the article in the journal Society points out , “[without] institutional pathways to translate protests into policy, such uprisings risk being co-opted by populist leaders or dissipating into chaos.” This article was produced by Economy for All , a project of the Independent Media Institute.

Sanctuary: Borders & Belonging in 2026

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: DSA Feed - Global ／ 著者: Rochester Red Star: News from Rochester DSA ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T16:35:44+00:00

by Bleu Cease, Curator Rochester Contemporary Art Center (RoCo) presents Sanctuary: Borders & Belonging in 2026 , an exhibition bringing together national and regional artists, creatives, and documentarians in response to the current political and social landscape surrounding immigration. On view October 2 through November 14, 2026, the exhibition centers the human lives behind political rhetoric, examining how belonging is made and denied, and how care, resilience, and solidarity persist amid increasingly hostile systems and cruel public narratives. * * * Sanctuary is on view through November 14. Sanctuary: Borders & Belonging in 2026 brings together national and regional artists, creatives, documentarians, and community members in response to the political and social realities surrounding immigration and migration. The exhibition amplifies immigrant voices, documents criminalization and detention, examines border politics and architecture, and creates space for people to share the complex experiences that shape belonging. Through testimony and creative resistance, these projects center the human lives behind political rhetoric while also countering simplistic and divisive narratives, insisting on dignity and human rights for all. Together, they ask how belonging is made and denied, and how care, solidarity, and resilience persist in the face The installation honoring Dolores Bustamante, created in collaboration with Erica Bryant and Carly Fox, places portraits of a generous farmworker, grandmother, and community leader alongside 137 letters of support, turning a local campaign for her release into a public shrine honoring the woman at the center and our community’s mutual aid, collective care, and resistance to immigrant detention. Bringing Tom Policano’s photographs from Slim Pickins into conversation with Cher Valentino’s recent paintings inspired by those images, this installation connects the conditions of migrant farmworkers documented in the 1970s to their continued vulnerability today. The pairing reminds us that poverty and unjust labor practices persist, now compounded by immigration enforcement targeting these workers, compressing decades of limited progress into an urgent shared present. Julio Salgado’s project I Am UndocuQueer features heavily alongside the artist’s own personal reflections in the video My Name Is Julio , making visible undocumented queer people’s leading role in the immigrant-rights movement, and confronting fear with pride, humor, joy, and self-representation, even in the face of deportation. Arleene Correa Valencia’s textile artworks transform family photographs, letters, clothing, and culturally specific fabrics into haunting portraits of migration, family separation, memory, and longing; where incomplete or absent figures represent the lives interrupted by borders, policies, and immigration enforcement. The exhibition premiers Sound of Sanctuary , a ten-track album, installed as a listening station in the gallery. Sound of Sanctuary , an immersive listening installation, created by Anaglog (Sean Mulligan) in partnership with RoCo, launches a live-recorded album and brings together five first- and second-generation immigrant artists and five Rochester musicians with the understanding that sanctuary is not a fixed place, but a shared culture of listening, relationship, attention, and care The Community Interview Project: The Immigrant Experience presents nearly 100 intimate recordings with immigrants from Rochester and the surrounding region into the gallery, grounding political debates in the voices and stories of our neighbors, presenting immigration as a diverse collection of lived experiences through which this community and the U.S. as a country have continually been built. (I’m)Migration is an exhibition within an exhibition; a collection resulting from an open call for visual art by immigrants in the Rochester region. It includes more than 60 artworks by immigrants and immigrant collaborators, bringing together stories of arrival, belonging, separation, resilience, cultural practice, and transformation while emphasizing the power of many voices speaking alongside one another. The short advocacy film When They Come For Me: Hope and Fear for Immigrants in the Promised Land by Worldwide Documentaries brings together local immigrant advocates and community perspectives to document the effects of inhumane immigration policies in Rochester, the Finger Lakes region, and Western New York. In Border Wall and several accompanying photographs, Saman and Sasan Oskouei use mirrors, fencing, barbed wire, and steel to fracture the apparent permanence of borders, transforming barriers into boats, doorways, flowers, flags, and passages toward freedom, movement, and envisioning another future. Jim Mott’s ICE Detention Facilities and the American Landscape (Seven Studies) uses paintings based on field observation and digital reconnaissance to consider how the growing for-profit immigration detention system has become embedded within familiar rural and urban communities, connecting the concrete realities of incarceration to the economic decline, infrastructure, and broader culture of the places that host and enable it. Saints of Our Time by Pamela Araya In Resist or Surrender , Annette Dragon’s compassionate photographs show that resistance is not an exceptional act but an ongoing local practice. She honors the ordinary people who gather throughout the region to advocate for immigrants and human rights while holding close the personal absence of her friend who is imprisoned at a nearby detention center. Saints of Our Time by Pamela Araya reimagines Catholic devotional portraiture through three highly visible contemporary figures connected to immigration, Latin American identity, and cultural memory. The three paintings honor Liam Conejo Ramos in Hope , Bad Bunny in Voice , and Renée Good in Testimony , as embodiments of suffering, cultural power, courage, and the ongoing struggle for dignity and belonging. Pamela Dodds’s installation Documenting Border Barriers transforms meticulously rendered drypoint etchings of fences, walls, razor wire, and surveillance systems into an imposing visual and information archive of the architecture of exclusion. It arranges the prints like a wall across the gallery and reveals these structures as deliberate, mostly contemporary acts of political control. Pamela Dodds’s Documenting Border Barriers considers the physical structures that divide nations and restrict movement. Installed outside the gallery, the related work of public art BELONG by Gianna Stewart confronts censorship and erasure by incorporating hundreds of words that have been removed from government websites, publications, and grant applications into a large graphic. This brave artwork makes visible the absurdity and the danger of this attempted erasure, while also reclaiming public space to insist that we all belong. Sanctuary: Borders & Belonging in 2026 continues RoCo’s longstanding support for artists who engage urgent social and political questions. It also reaffirms our commitment to art practices that center testimony, witnessing, imagination, and action. At a time of censorship and fear, the exhibition amplifies artists who, often at great personal or professional risk, speak truth to power and advocate for human rights and a more just, equitable world. As global inequality, climate change, and the politics of fear lead to increasingly barricaded and surveilled borders, and immigrant communities face intensified criminalization, Sanctuary demands solidarity. To listen to one another. To defend one another. And to recognize that our freedom and safety are bound together. Upcoming Events: Film Screening: Ice Breaker / Match Maker . [ Friday, October 16, 6:00pm – 9:00pm] A screening and related gathering exploring migration, connection, and the social realities of immigrant life. Surviving Suppression: How Artists Experience and Overcome Censorship. [Friday, October 23, 2026, 6:30pm – 9:00pm] A panel and conversation examining the pressures artists face when their work addresses politically charged subjects, and the strategies they use to continue speaking through art. Sound of Sanctuary: LIVE . [Friday, November 13, 6:30pm ] A live performance of Sound of Sanctuary which is being launched in partnership with RoCo as an installation and listening station in the main gallery. Sanctuary: Borders & Belonging in 2026 will be on view October 2 – November 14, 2026, at Rochester Contemporary Art Center, [ 137 East Avenue ]. Gallery hours are Wednesday – Sunday, 12:00pm – 5:00pm, with extended hours Fridays until 9:00pm. Suggested admission is $2; admission is free for RoCo members. The post Sanctuary: Borders & Belonging in 2026 first appeared on Rochester Red Star .

Gaza genocide denial

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Norman Solomon ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T15:52:45+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. If you understand that Israel has committed genocide in Gaza, why read an article focusing on the viewpoints of people who vehemently deny it? I wasn’t sure how to explain why, until I remembered something that screenwriter Frédéric Krivine said when I interviewed him in Paris eight years ago. He was the head scriptwriter of the brilliant film series Un Village Français , dramatizing the Nazi occupation of France during World War Two. “People who do horrible things are human beings,” Krivine said. “We have to find a way to talk about them without hiding what they do and without treating them as nonhuman people, nonhuman beings. They are human beings; like us they belong to, we are in, the same species, human species…. It’s humans who kill now everywhere in the world where people are killed. It’s because they are human beings that we have problems – because if they were just extraterrestrial or monsters we could just erase them.” When director Jonathan Glazer gave the acceptance speech for his film The Zone of Interest at the 2024 Academy Awards, these words caused an uproar: “All our choices were made to reflect and confront us in the present. Not to say, ‘Look what they did then,’ rather ‘Look what we do now.’ Our film shows where dehumanization leads at its worst. It shaped all of our past and present. Right now, we stand here as men who refute their Jewishness and the Holocaust being hijacked by an occupation which has led to conflict for so many innocent people. Whether the victims of October the 7th in Israel or the ongoing attack on Gaza, all the victims of this dehumanization. How do we resist?” Quick reactions included a furious open letter that identified its more than 1,000 signers as “Jewish creatives, executives and Hollywood professionals.” The letter declared, “We refute our Jewishness being hijacked for the purpose of drawing a moral equivalence between a Nazi regime that sought to exterminate a race of people, and an Israeli nation that seeks to avert its own extermination…. The use of words like ‘occupation’ to describe an indigenous Jewish people defending a homeland that dates back thousands of years, and has been recognized as a state by the United Nations, distorts history. It gives credence to the modern blood libel that fuels a growing anti-Jewish hatred around the world, in the United States, and in Hollywood. The current climate of growing antisemitism only underscores the need for the Jewish State of Israel, a place which will always take us in, as no state did during the Holocaust depicted in Mr. Glazer’s film.” The Gaza war was then in its fifth month. At the time, citing information from UNICEF, Reuters reported that “over 13,000 children have been killed in Gaza in Israel’s offensive,” and “many kids were suffering from severe malnutrition.” The United Nations issued a statement saying, “This war is a war on children. It is a war on their childhood and their future.” Related: Could Ro Khanna and AOC Reshape the 2028 Democratic Primary? Deaths and injuries mounted swiftly for another 19 months, until an ostensible ceasefire in October 2025. Assaults by Israeli forces have continued at lower levels, with no letup in miserable conditions for 2 million Palestinian people living in Gaza. Given all that has happened, I recently contacted some signers of the open letter to ask whether they still fully believe in its contents and whether their perspective on Israel has changed since then. Ten responded to my questions. Koura Linda, who owns a film production company based in Los Angeles, was appalled by the applause and cheers that Glazer received when he spoke at the Academy Awards. “If anything, I would sign that letter 100 times over today,” Linda told me. Later, she said: “It’s very confusing to me why people are so fixated on Israel specifically…. I don’t understand the fixation on the only piece of the world that belongs to the Jewish people.” Les Glassman, a dentist and documentary filmmaker, spoke with me from his home in Jerusalem. He recalled that The Zone of Interest – which depicts the ordinary everyday life of a Nazi commandant and his wife living next to a concentration camp – was very warmly received when it premiered in Israel. “People loved it,” Glassman said. Later, when Glazer made his Oscar speech, “it was a shock…. It was a stab in the back…. Like treason…. He used the Holocaust against Israel…. I don't know one person who was impressed with his speech. People were the opposite, they were like totally flabbergasted and could not believe what they were hearing. It was like a traitor really.” Glassman has dedicated himself to interviewing elderly Jews who survived the Holocaust. “In nearly all my interviews of Holocaust survivors worldwide which I have been privileged to do,” he said, “the important message imparted by the survivors [is] ‘we have our own Jewish country' as they remember when the world shut its doors and Jews had nowhere to flee or find refuge.” Related: Real History: After 9/11, the “War on Terror” Was a War of Terror Actor and director Geoffrey Cantor told me, “I completely stand by the letter 100 percent.” He had recently voiced an audio book by Eli Sharabi, who was abducted from a kibbutz on October 7 and held prisoner in Gaza tunnels for 491 days. I asked Cantor about the letter’s vehement objection to Glazer’s use of the word “occupation,” referring to Israel’s control of Gaza and the West Bank. “It’s not an occupation,” said Cantor, who described himself as “a liberal politically.” After noting that “war is horrible, it’s always been horrible,” Cantor went on: “Israelis fight war to protect themselves. And it hasn’t been until the establishment of Israel that Jews were able to even engage in a war. Until then, we were just slaughtered. And so, that does something to one’s way of viewing war.” I asked, “What do you make of the outlook of some who say, not only was The Zone of Interest a great film, but it has something to say about the current situation with Israel and Palestinians?” “I think I saw like the trailer,” Cantor said. “I did not see the whole film…. I’m sure the film is beautiful and probably deserved a lot of praise – but then to use that, to say, and Israel’s the worst perpetrator. Ever. In the history of the world. Is so absurd, so wrong. And when it comes from a Jewish person, is so damning… because it feeds the fires. Stokes the flame of righteous indignation against the Jewish people and anybody who supports our only country.” When I asked if it made any difference that Glazer is Jewish, Cantor replied: “When a Jewish voice uses the language that I disagree with about Israel, in such a public forum, it feels a little bit more like a betrayal.” Remembering Glassman’s comment on the phone line from Jerusalem likening Glazer’s speech to “treason,” I asked Cantor if he thought the word applied. “No,” he responded, “just betrayal, not treason. But betrayal.” The interview with Cantor lasted an hour and a half. In contrast, the response was terse from another signer of the open letter, the emeritus rabbi at Sinai Temple in Los Angeles, David Wolpe, who is a consultant to Warner Bros. and Gulfstream Pictures for a feature film adapting his book about King David. Does Rabbi Wolpe still fully believe in the contents of the open letter? “Yes.” Has his perspective on Israel changed since signing it? “No.” Related: How U.S. Torture Went Unpunished After 9/11 Interviewed separately, all of the signers said that they still completely support the open letter. They often returned to the same themes: Glazer’s speech had been a “blood libel” with “false equivalency.” Glazer had used the Holocaust to invert its true meaning while feeding into antisemitism. Hostility to Israel has gained the upper hand in U.S. media and politics. Israel is held to a much higher standard than other countries. Zionists have been increasingly besieged and subjected to hatred. Israel protects Jews from a future Holocaust. Civilian deaths in Gaza were unfortunate but Israel went extraordinarily out of its way to prevent them. Several of the signers indicated that the open letter understates their current abhorrence of Glazer’s Oscar speech. “If anything, I am even more supportive of Israel now amidst the ever-increasing amount of anti-Israel lies, propaganda and brainwashing that is ubiquitous worldwide,” said Jonathan Geffner, a professional ventriloquist. “Unfortunately, our letter is even more relevant today than it was back then,” said Roy Schwartz, a writer and documentary producer. “Blood libels against Israel and Jews have only escalated since. They’re rampant and unchecked, even in politics, civic orgs, and the media. Israel is subject to obsessive focus wildly disproportionate to anything else going on in the world, and it’s held to a standard that no other country is held even close to. Meanwhile, its implacable enemies and other bad players are ignored, excused, or their blatant misinformation accepted uncritically.” Shie Rozow, an Israeli-American composer and music editor living in Los Angeles, has credits including work on 150 feature films as well as popular TV shows. “I actually wish the letter was even more strongly worded,” he said. “The only part of his speech I agree with is that dehumanization is a problem that we must confront. Whether it's in this conflict, or in our politics. And yet again he makes a false equivalency between victims of brutal terrorism that openly and clearly sought to kill anyone it could versus military action that appears to have done more to avoid the killing of civilians than practically any other military has done in history, under unprecedentedly challenging circumstances. This isn't to say the IDF is perfect, far from it. This is not the same IDF I served in. I've seen things that I couldn't imagine happening when I served over 30 years ago. And Israel has the worst, most corrupt and extremist government in its history with senior members who belong nowhere near power.” To the host of “The Unapologetically Jewish Podcast,” Shana Meyerson, “Glazer’s speech is symptomatic of something greater and more nefarious in society: knee-trembling desperation to bend to the rabid masses and support terrorists – the murderers of his own people – in order to pass some loyalty litmus test…. He chose applause over human decency and truth. And the fact that he would actually weaponize the Holocaust to that end, makes his speech even that much more reprehensible.” Among the signers I interviewed, references to Benjamin Netanyahu and Donald Trump were almost always negative. “I have no sympathy for Netanyahu’s approach, nor for Trump’s,” said director and producer Tommaso Cardile. “I would much prefer a moderate, diplomatic Israeli government pursuing a negotiated outcome, and I say that as someone who wants Israel to endure… What concerned me about Mr. Glazer’s speech was not his sincerity but its effect. Whatever he intended, the framing was instantly repurposed by people whose aims he does not share, at a moment when Jewish communities in Europe and the United States were already facing the sharpest rise in threats and harassment in decades. Statements from that stage don't stay in the room they’re made in. I think he underestimated that.” Related: There’s Nothing Moderate About Defending the Status Quo Working on this article, I have strived to be fair to the people I interviewed and to convey the gist of their points of view. I found their basic outlooks to be repugnant. What should not get lost is the profound truth that Jonathan Glazer managed to articulate in the 40 seconds of his Oscar speech that caused so much distress. The director deftly took down a kind of psychological “fourth wall” that routinely separates viewers from film. His speech, like The Zone of Interest , offered the possibility of going beyond “Look what they did then” to “Look what we do now.” For those who heard his speech as abhorrent, Glazer’s invitation to truly examine what we do now – with tax dollars, silence, passivity or active acceptance – was a betrayal of the pretense that our society is not aiding and abetting genocide in the present day. Complacency and indifference prevail about the fate of “the other.” That way, the organized atrocities that ended 81 years ago, which can no longer be prevented, can loom incomparably more important than the organized atrocities that are happening today and might be prevented tomorrow. During the interviews, perhaps the most disturbing responses came after my last question, when I asked whether the signers had any comment on the unequivocal conclusions of Israeli genocide reached in late 2024 by Human Rights Watch , Amnesty International and two Israeli human rights organizations, B’Tselem and Physicians for Human Rights – Israel . All those who replied were dismissive. One response claimed that “Amnesty International and Human Rights Watch have Hamas members in their Gaza operations and are well-documented for their systematic bias against Israel,” and that “B’Tselem is also infamous for its far left, radical, anti-Israel agenda.” Another signer said: “I imagine some of the information is true, and much of it was either outright Hamas-driven lies and propaganda or presented without full knowledge of circumstances or out of context. That's not reliable. Nor are many of the assertions and claims set forth.” Another commented that “Israel gave polio vaccines, if there was a genocide, why are they giving polio vaccines?” Another asserted that the four human rights organizations “have repeatedly been found to be spreading misinformation and are all angled to paint Israel in a negative light.” Another replied, “The devastation and loss of life in Gaza is horrific but it does not come close to reaching the threshold that would warrant or support the label of genocide.” Another sent me a video of a Holocaust survivor living in Israel, filmed at age 101, who said: “If we defend ourselves, then they tell us we’re committing genocide. Genocide is state-organized killing machine and not defense against terrorism.” And, “There is no genocide anywhere committed by us. It’s a figment of imagination of the international media and international politicians.” Related: J Street’s “Pro-Peace” Cover for Arming Israel Another said, “What Hamas did on October 7, 2023 was genocide. Everything since has been war.” Across the United States, denial of Israel’s actual crimes against humanity has diminished during the 30 months since Jonathan Glazer’s speech. But a flinty core of denial still remains. To acknowledge the genocide would be to shatter the identity of Jews who love Israel. For that matter, to acknowledge the genocide would undermine the right-wing Christians United for Israel organization, which claims 10 million members in the United States. And open acknowledgement of the genocide would render almost impossible the reflex of enormous U.S. military aid to Israel that still prevails among a large majority of Congress members. In his 1939 film The Rules of the Game , Jean Renoir says: “The awful thing about life is this: Everyone has their reasons.”

Decline of American Empire

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Tom Engelhardt ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T15:21:41+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. Only recently, after a mere two decades of intermittent conflict (with three years off ), the last U.S. troops finally withdrew from... yes, can you believe it?... Iraq! Don’t be shocked, since we’re talking about a country significantly less than 7,000 miles from our shores. Honestly, that’s next to nothing if you remember that, in my youth, the U.S. was fighting a 20-year-long war in Vietnam (and Laos and Cambodia) about 9,000 miles from Washington, D.C. The conflict in Iraq didn’t even last that long. After all, the U.S. didn’t launch its war there until its invasion of 2003, which, in the great scope of time, was essentially yesterday. And I certainly can’t complain about any of that. After all, this country’s distant wars have always given me something to do. Once upon a time—on April 3, 1968, in fact— I even turned in my draft card at an antiwar demonstration on Boston Common to protest that very war in Vietnam. (In those days, of course, the U.S. Army wasn’t an all-volunteer outfit.) And some weeks later, I wrote a letter refusing a National Defense Foreign Language Fellowship to fund my study of Chinese history at Harvard graduate school. I still have a xerox of that letter, which reads in part: “On the afternoon of April 3rd, on returning to my apartment, I found a letter from Harvard University offering me a National Defense Foreign Language Fellowship to study Chinese language and history at Harvard in the ‘national interest’ for a government which has for years been flagrantly working against that interest. The mistake in a sense had been mine. I had applied for the grant, thoughtlessly, and for that I apologize. Nonetheless, arriving as it did, at the moment it did in my life, it seemed like a bribe, (even if obviously an unconscious one). ‘You made your silly gesture,’ it said, ‘now forget it and take this money.’ ‘There are no strings attached,’ said my friends and perhaps not... “It is, I have been told, innocuous, fairly administered, and helpful to the promotion of learning. I won’t bother to dispute this. But like so many first steps, so many deals we enter where we are assured there are ‘no strings,’ we are later surprised to find ourselves trapped. I look at this first step and it seems so harmless, in fact so inviting; but I look at the results in the field of modern Chinese studies and I say ‘no,’ I will take my chances another way with few regrets.” Related: Trump Is Fighting the Future—and China Is Winning As it happened, within months of writing that letter I had left my studies at Harvard and become a printer—yes, a printer!—at what in that mobilized antiwar moment was known as a “movement printshop.” A mere 58 years later, in an America without a draft, I have nothing (other than this Substack, of course) to turn in—or even turn to— in order to protest my government’s latest war against Iran. Still, don’t you find it strange that, more than half a century ago, my country’s military was fighting in Vietnam (and Laos and Cambodia), while almost six decades later, things have indeed grown so much more logical, as Tehran, the capital of Iran, is a mere 6,300 or so miles from Washington, D.C. Give us another half century, and (if planet Earth is even a livable place anymore), my country (if it still exists) could be fighting a war a mere 3,000 or so miles from its shores and then... but I won’t go on, since I’m sure you can do the math at least as well as I can. (It was never my strong point!) Buy the Book Oh, and in the coming months, in the age of Donald Trump and the “ Donroe Doctrine “ when anything is possible, we could break all the rules of American warfare (with the exception of the invasion of the tiny Caribbean island of Grenada in 1983 and “our” president’s brief militarized leap into Venezuela to seize its leader and his wife as this year began) and invade Cuba , a mere 90 to 100 (instead of 6,000 to 9,000) miles off the coast of Florida. After all, simply destroying endless numbers of boats in the Caribbean Sea and eastern Pacific Ocean is anything but enough! And if you don’t find that distinctly upbeat as a near-future possibility, then what in the world would be? Heading Down, Down, Down On the other side of the coin (yes, I just flipped it!), in 1968, when I wrote that letter, former Vice President Lyndon Johnson, who became president after John F. Kennedy was assassinated, was still “my” president. Today, a mere 58 years later, it’s (gulp!) Donald J. Trump who, unbelievably enough, won his second term in office in 2024 with 49.8% of the national vote—and just in case you’ve forgotten, that was 3.4% more than he got when he won the 2016 election (while losing the popular vote count to Hillary Clinton!), and almost 3% more than his count in 2020 when he lost to Joe Biden (the only candidate in any of the last three elections to get more than half the vote— 51.3% ). And if all of that doesn’t tell you what you need to know about the downhill slope of American politics and history (or perhaps even of politics and history globally), then let me know what might. In short, the single most powerful country on the planet is now ruled by a distinct madman who never won 50% of the vote, which also tells you all too much about this increasingly divided country and endangered world of ours. And if that’s the path we’re on (as it seems to be) on this distinctly overheating planet of ours in which wars still rage from Ukraine to Gaza to Somalia, pouring fossil fuels into the atmosphere (as will the future spread of AI data centers ), try for a moment to imagine the nature (and acts) of a president (if that even still proves to be his— and yes, it will probably still be a HIM as in His Imperial Majesty—title) in whatever passes for the United States of America 20 or 30 years from now, when we may be fighting our wars so much closer to home (or even right in this country). Phew, that sentence exhausted me! Imagine going back in history and telling George Washington, Abraham Lincoln, or Franklin Delano Roosevelt (all of whom were wartime presidents) about this Trumpian wartime moment and planet of ours. Or maybe just return to 1956, when my parents—my father having been in the Air Force in World War II—voted for Democratic candidate Adlai Stevenson and yet Dwight D. Eisenhower, the former Supreme Allied Commander in Europe in World War II, won his second term in office and that seemed like bad news to us then. Of course, our present world—if someone had even been able to describe it to us then—would have been inconceivable, if not the most ridiculous science fiction plot ever. (If somehow Eisenhower or Johnson were to return and win a presidential election now, it would be a hallelujah moment, which tells you just how far we’ve come in the last nearly three-quarters of a century.) Related: Donald Trump and a World at the Boiling Point Once upon a time, nothing could have convinced me that Donald Trump—had you described him to me then (assuming he’s even describable in the normal sense of the term)—could possibly have been elected president or that this country would functionally go to war ( economically at least ) with our neighbor Canada. Had you made up such a plot, you would then have been categorized as a raging idiot. I would have simply laughed you out of the room. That’s our future? Give me a break! Whatever my country’s problems and disturbing acts were once upon a time, the present moment would then have been the single lousiest joke imaginable. And yet here we are in that once inconceivable future on a planet slowly going down the tubes—and it’s hard even to imagine how to protest it all, especially “our” president. Is there anything left that could be turned in to protest this hell on earth we all too literally find ourselves in? And don’t forget to add one more all-American thing: imagine that you had told me on that April 3rd so long ago that, 58 years later, our country, led by a maniac named Donald Trump, would be fighting yet another war thousands of miles away. Or what if you had said that this world of ours more generally would still be engulfed in war from Ukraine to the Middle East to—thank you again, Washington!— Somalia ? And what if you had asked me what we should make of a planetary power that, for endless decades, has fought wars in distant lands, but whose president now seems all too intent on bringing war ever closer to home? Given the history of the United States in the last century or so, that—and he—couldn’t be more striking symbols of decline on a planet that itself is going down in a big-time fashion. The madness of Donald Trump as president of the United States (twice!), though seldom thought of that way, should undoubtedly be seen as a sign of the once greatest imperial power going down, down, down in a big-time way and potentially taking so much else down with it. I wonder what letter I would write today, if I were in my early twenties and facing just such a world? Tom's Substack

In Russia’s Election, Nothing Happened, but Things Got Worse

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: Jacobin ／ 著者: Maria Chiara Franceschelli ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T15:20:35+00:00

Between September 18 and 20, Russians voted for a new State Duma, along with several regional parliaments and governors. According to the Central Election Commission, turnout reached 59.8 percent, up eight points since 2021. The result was the one everyone expected. Vladimir Putin’s United Russia took almost 58 percent of the party-list vote and 349 of the Duma’s 450 seats, up from 310 in the outgoing chamber. Forty-one of its new MPs are veterans of the invasion of Ukraine. The Communist Party (KPRF) came a distant second with thirty-seven seats, followed by the Liberal Democratic Party (LDPR) with twenty-three, New People with nineteen, A Just Russia with seventeen, and Rodina with one. Four independents complete the total. Voters received two ballots: one for a nationwide party list (225 seats) and one for a candidate in their local single-member district (the other 225). Yabloko, the only registered party running on an openly antiwar platform, was removed from the party-list ballot after the Supreme Court canceled its federal slate in August following a lawsuit brought by Rodina. Yabloko could thus be voted for only on the district ballot and only where one of its individual candidates had survived registration challenges, i.e., in roughly a hundred districts. Outside those districts, and on the national party list everywhere, Yabloko was not an option. Yabloko has never carried much weight in parliament, but this time was the only institutional party openly opposed to the war, with a program calling for an immediate end to the offensive and a return to negotiations. The Kremlin had two options. It could exclude Yabloko, as it has routinely done with inconvenient candidates, or let it run and collect a negligible vote — proof, as it were, that opposition to the war commands little support, while stressing United Russia’s supremacy as well as offering a façade of pluralism. At first, it seemed the authorities had chosen this path: the Central Election Commission registered Yabloko’s list. Then, Rodina filed its complaint, and Yabloko was eventually struck from the ballot. Besides the ruling United Russia, all the other nine parties belong to the “systemic opposition”: a well-rooted paradox of Russian politics by which some parties enjoy enough room for maneuver to criticize specific government policies, while ultimately remaining loyal to Putin and not representing a substantial threat to the Kremlin. In this scenario, the only notable development was New People (Novye Lyudi), founded in 2020 by entrepreneur Alexey Nechayev. Having no clear ideology, the party’s stances can be linked to a vaguely liberal center right. Over the last year, it has gained popularity thanks to its opposition to the internet shutdowns and restrictions imposed by the Kremlin, has surpassed the KPRF as the second-most-popular party (although polling in authoritarian systems, especially in wartime, must be treated with caution), and won in eighteen out of twenty-seven of the main polling stations abroad. However, this data is not nearly enough to predict any groundbreaking change, also because New People’s vague brand could be easily co-opted by the Kremlin and made another pillar of Putin’s stability. Election observers were also absent. There was no mission from the Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe’s Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights (ODIHR), since Russia did not send the necessary invitations. Russia’s own independent monitoring groups, meanwhile, have been worn down by years of legal and informal pressure, to the point where they could no longer meaningfully operate. Nobody was surprised by United Russia’s landslide, which even exceeded the government pollsters’ own figures by a few points. The real question these elections raise is not about the outcome, which has been known in advance for more than twenty years. Instead, it is about what an election even means in a country moving decisively toward totalitarian rule. Power Built on a Vacuum The stability of Putin’s rule has always rested on the systematic depoliticization of Russian society: a lasting detachment of citizens from political life and its rituals. This detachment has a history. In the Soviet Union, political life and participation were organized from above, and there was little room for bottom-up engagement. When that scaffolding collapsed, nothing took its place. The economic and political crisis of the 1990s, Boris Yeltsin’s coup against parliament in 1993, and the rapid consolidation of an oligarchic order meant that the transition to a working participatory democracy never happened. Mechanisms of democratic participation from below never took root, either in terms of free and open electoral competition or the everyday infrastructure of collective organizing. This does not mean that Russia lacks grassroots organizations, activists, and movements. It has them, and they have paid dearly for their existence under mounting repression. It means that this kind of social dynamic never became the norm. Putin’s system found fertile ground in that vacuum, building an authoritarian order that starts from economic elites and extends through the military and security apparatus down to the courts and local administration. Over the last twenty years, the structural gap in resources between the state and those who tried, or wished, to build a genuinely participatory democracy could never be bridged. Depoliticization, then, works on two levels. It operates through formal channels of participation such as elections, but also at a cognitive level. An important manifestation of structural depoliticization, as researchers at the Public Sociology Laboratory have shown in a series of studies, is the alienation of Russian citizens from the very act of articulating their own political positions. From Demobilization to Mobilization Since the full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, however, something within the regime has been shifting, not as a change of direction but as an exponential increase. Putin’s system was authoritarian; it is increasingly taking on the features of a totalitarian order, closing down remaining spaces for expression, participation, and dissent. In this sense, depoliticization is now paired with mobilization encouraged from above. For most of Putin’s rule, the goal was to keep people away from politics, including the polling booth. A low, predictable turnout suited a system that asked nothing of its citizens but acquiescence. Now Russia is shifting toward potential disorder, with Ukraine reacting to the invasion and drones bombing infrastructure and houses in western Russia, shortages at gas stations, a looming economic crisis, internet restrictions, rising death tolls at the front lines, and the prospect of a new military mobilization. In this context, consensus and unity are more needed than passivity. Voting is actively encouraged — with employees in factories and firms being formally obliged to vote and even taken to the polling booths — not for the sake of democracy but to mobilize the population in support of the further concentration of power and the consolidation of the political system itself. Local administrations, state agencies, and state-linked institutions are all put to work bringing people to the ballot box. The role of elections has thus been inverted. They are no longer a moment of (managed) democratic participation that the Kremlin prefers citizens to sit out but a mechanism for mobilizing consent to the centralization of power. Although internet voting and the involvement of the occupied territories of Crimea, Sevastopol, and parts of Luhansk, Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia, and Kherson surely play a role, the rise in turnout, from 51.7 to 59.8 percent, should be read also in this light. In that sense, these elections tell us little that is new in electoral terms. And yet, at a delicate moment, marked by growing economic difficulties driven by the war and Ukrainian missile strikes and by a risk of social unrest, Putin’s answer is once again repression but this time accompanied by a demand for active participation and performative consent, in the manner of the totalitarian regimes of the 1930s. The Russian Feud This discursive effort is accompanied by classic techniques from the Putinist repertoire. The first is the figure of the necessary leader. Putin presents himself as the only one who can defend the country from external attack: in this case, “the West’s war on Russia.” Many Russians now perceive this confrontation as inevitable , almost messianic, something from which only a great leader can save them. The second is passing the buck to the regions. The costs of the war, as so often, are made to fall on regional administrations. The management of the fuel crisis, for instance, has been offloaded onto governors. This is not new: the Kremlin did the same during the pandemic, shifting the responsibility for COVID-19-related restrictive measures onto regional governors. This mechanism protects the image of the center, which must continue to be seen as the guarantor of stability, and spares it direct responsibility for unpopular measures. This is part of what makes the system so resilient. Putin personally still hovers above the fray. His authority is real, but the responsibility for what the state actually does, its merits and its shortcomings, is spread widely across institutions and levels of government. Combined with the heavy security theater on display, especially in Moscow, this diffusion of responsibility helps explain why the system appears so immovable. The regions have also borne the human cost of the war most unevenly, and military recruitment has doubled as an instrument for managing (to put it mildly) those with the least political and economic power or those the authorities find most inconvenient . There is also a more mundane, logistical side to these elections. Elections and the reshuffling of posts that follows allow careers and responsibilities within the state and legislative apparatus to be redistributed without anyone having to be fired. The war itself “works” in this sense as a continuous transfer of public money to military elites and as an occasion to redraw relations among institutions. The forty-one veterans now sitting in the Duma for United Russia are one visible sign of this. Wartime Support What do we really know about popular support for Putin and the war? Ethnographic work carried out inside Russia suggests that most people do not hold a clear, consistent political opinion about the war, which is one reason why conventional opinion polling tells us so little. People move back and forth between supporting and criticizing it, depending on personal and situational factors rather than any coherent ideology. Opinions on Russia’s war can be influenced by personal ties with relatives and friends from and in Ukraine, the mobilization of a family member to the front and the tragic implications that this often carries, personal economic impact, fear of drone attacks, and other factors that strongly affect one’s personal life. In fact, it is also important to remember that Putin has always strived to keep his war far from Russia’s civilian population and instead to push a romanticized, heroic, and epic image of the “special military operation” and its “patriots.” Over the last months, this has become increasingly difficult to sustain. However, thanks also to a far-reaching propaganda effort, many see the war less as a political event they could influence than as something more like a natural disaster arriving from the outside. Without a settled view of the war or a broader political outlook to anchor it, people tend to slide into a kind of relativism. Opinion about the war, in other words, is scattered and situational rather than systematic. This is partly a product of depoliticization. It is also because people do not reason the way sociologists do in their work: they do not necessarily organize their views into positions that can be neatly measured and aggregated. For years, much of the debate on Russia turned on a recurrent question: Was support for Putin genuine or merely passive? It was a reasonable thing to ask, and it once seemed to hold the key to the regime’s future. Today it has become a matter mostly for specialists. The Putin system has sealed itself off and narrowed the space for participation so far that the quality of consent, whether sincere or alienated, no longer has much bearing on the course of events. What shapes the situation now is simpler and starker: there is no longer any opening through which that consent, or its absence, could make itself felt. This also goes for parliamentary debate, such as KPRF leader Gennady Zyuganov’s postelectoral criticism of the Kremlin’s war efforts in Ukraine and his forecast of a gloomy defeat if Putin maintains his line. However, the KPRF has historically supported the annexation of Donetsk and Luhansk, as well as the invasion of Ukraine. The KPRF’s 2026 election program never names Ukraine, yet it takes the war for granted: it speaks of securing victory at the front, uses standard Kremlin language about “neofascists” and “Banderites” in Kyiv, and promises more for the army and veterans. It offers no position on the war’s aims, an end to it, or peace talks. It sounds more like performative criticism of domestic policies amid a situation — the war — that is already taken for granted. Very Few Options These elections were a problem for the Russian opposition, too. The opposition in exile is increasingly disconnected from dissent inside the country, which is small and mutilated, and the networks of organization and collective action that once existed have been cut and harshly repressed. So, what options did people have if they did not want to support United Russia or wanted to vote against the war? With Yabloko gone, four remained, and none of them offered much. The first was a boycott. Staying home would lower official turnout but not change the result: Russia abolished the minimum turnout requirement for Duma elections back in 2007. Nor does a boycott send a clear message, since there is no way to tell someone abstaining in protest apart from someone who simply stayed home. The second was tactical voting, in the tradition of Alexei Navalny’s “Smart Voting” now upheld by his widow and heir of his movement, Yulia Navalnaya. Its impact on the party lists is minimal, but it can matter somewhat more in single-member districts, which elect half the Duma. There are no runoffs: whoever gets the most votes wins the seat. The logic is to back the strongest challenger to the Putinist candidate rather than splitting protest votes among several names, whether by reading the polls or following the recommendations published by Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation (FBK). The challenger in question is almost always a candidate of the tolerated “systemic” opposition. The tactic had its biggest successes in the 2019 Moscow City Duma election, where the supported candidates took twenty of forty-five seats, and in the 2020 local elections in Tomsk and Novosibirsk. By the 2021 Duma vote, the blocking of FBK’s app and website and the rollout of electronic voting had strongly blunted its impact. The third was to take one’s ballot home. Duma election law does not forbid it, and while it changes nothing about the distribution of seats, it can expose fraud. Precinct commissions must record the number of ballots issued but not returned in their final protocols. If a voter took a ballot home and the relevant line in the protocol reads zero, the commission most likely filled the gap with an extra vote for someone. The fourth was to spoil the ballot. This too leaves the result untouched, but a mass protest is easier to see this way than through abstention. It is also risky. In previous elections, some voters were arrested after writing antiwar or anti-Putin messages on theirs (although theoretically, votes are anonymous). A ballot left blank, however, is easy to fill in during a fraudulent count. None of these strategies amounts to a real opening for opposition, not even a symbolic one, or the kind of small-scale guerrilla activism that dissidents relied on in the first years of the war. The choices were few, and each of them could at best leave a trace in a precinct protocol. This is what distinguishes the 2026 elections from those that came before. For two decades, Russian elections were managed events that the regime preferred citizens to watch from a distance. Now they have become a mere rally reminiscent of a totalitarian regime, in which the opposition cannot even score symbolic wins. This article originally appeared in Jacobin , a democratic socialist magazine publishing long-form essays and analysis on politics, economics, and culture.
