The Impossible Will Take a Little While: The Power of Persistence

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Paul Rogat Loeb ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T13:58:24+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. We live in a time fraught with bad news: assaults on democracy, climate disasters, crippling inequality, and an endless proliferation of lies. The pace of the daily outrages reminds me of a moment from Art Spiegelman’s Maus . The Spiegelman character asks his therapist, a concentration camp survivor, what it felt like to be in the camps. His therapist thinks, and then yells “Boo!” as loudly as he can, and Spiegelman jumps back. “It felt a little like that,” his therapist says. “But always! From the moment you got to the gate to the very end.” Though Americans aren’t living through the Holocaust, each day can bring more political horrors, more attacks on communities and lives. Exhaustion and despair are understandable, so we need to learn from people who’ve acted in the most difficult circumstances imaginable, and persisted with courage, heart, and even joy. Those in power—who want that power to go unchallenged—insist that our actions don’t matter. Related: The Power of Everyday Activism: How Ordinary People Become Leaders But as Czech playwright, dissident, and later president Václav Havel wrote, “Hope is not prognostication. It is an orientation of the spirit, an orientation of the heart.” Havel wrote those words under a Communist dictatorship that was three years away from being unexpectedly overthrown in a nonviolent revolution. Sometimes the seemingly impossible happens unexpectedly fast—but only if we continue acting on what we believe even when times get hard. Engagement can carry costs. Havel was imprisoned for four years, Nelson Mandela for twenty-seven. ICE resisters have been indicted, imprisoned, or killed. But if we look at life through a historical lens, we find that the proverbial rock can be rolled, if not to the top of the mountain, then at least to successive plateaus. And attacks on human dignity can be successfully resisted with enough courage and persistence. History also shows that even seemingly miraculous advances are the result of small steps taken by many people, often over a long period. And that the actions of any of us can give hope and sustenance to others. Courageous action can echo far beyond particular causes, countries, and historical times. Those who inspired Gandhi included Leo Tolstoy and Henry David Thoreau. Key US civil rights leaders traveled to India to learn from Gandhi’s compatriots. The US civil rights movement inspired those who challenged South African apartheid and the Eastern European dictatorships. Young activists from Nepal, Peru, and Madagascar have recently inspired each other in rising up against corrupt and dictatorial governments. When previously apolitical Chicago residents helped resist ICE immigration raids, they paved the way for similar responses in cities like Minneapolis. And Minneapolis residents credited both cities like Chicago and Norwegian forebears whose nonviolent resistance successfully defied the Nazis in World War II. I created this wholly updated book, The Impossible Will Take a Little While, to help ordinary citizens continue fighting for democracy and a more humane world, despite all the obstacles, setbacks, and legitimate reasons for fear. Here are brief capsules from five of the essays: Lightsticks, Ramen, and K-Pop: How South Koreans Stopped a Coup Howard Zinn talks about “the quick collapse of systems of power that seemed invincible.” In December 2024, as Korean peace activist Jungmin Choi describes, South Korean President Yoon Suk Yeol imposed martial law, ordering soldiers to storm the National Assembly and drag members out so they couldn’t vote to end it. But 16,000 ordinary South Koreans pushed past the troops and surrounded the building to protect the legislators as other Assembly members arrived and climbed the walls to enter. Soldiers resisted by refusing to interfere with the civilians, staying in their military buses, withholding flight approvals for military helicopters, citing bureaucratic rules to refuse to distribute ammunition. One group of soldiers even detoured to a convenience store to eat ramen noodles. The legislators finally got the necessary quorum and voted to negate Yoon’s directive. Over the next ten days, more than a million people gathered in front of the building, waving lightsticks, singing K-pop songs, and successfully demanding Yoon’s impeachment. Many spoke of the 1980 Gwangju protests as inspiration for their actions. Courage here was collective, and contagious, and President Yoon ended up impeached, tried, and jailed for life. Climate Hope It’s increasingly easy to become fatalistic about climate change. The ten hottest years in nearly two hundred years of recordkeeping have all occurred in the past decade. Disasters continue to escalate. Yet as environmental writer Bill McKibben describes, renewables have now become the world’s cheapest energy sources. By 2025, renewable sources produced more electricity globally than coal. By 2031, solar alone is projected to produce more than gas. Costs for batteries to store the new energy have dropped 95 percent in fifteen years. In my favorite story of McKibben’s, Pakistani homeowners and storekeepers began buying dirt-cheap solar panels from China and saving money by simply laying them on the ground to collect the sun. The panels deployed in less than a single year produced more electricity than a third of the country’s existing production nationwide, displacing costly diesel fuel for lights, water pumps, and cell and computer charging. A key driver was when “the German Green Party leveraged its parliamentary power to win a big government subsidy for rooftop solar power, creating a demand that led China, which was then building one coal plant after another for its own use, to start manufacturing solar panels in bulk for export to Europe.” American state and federal subsidies for renewables helped as well—even as Trump has revoked the latter. Challenging the Pipeline Psychologist Mary Pipher, describes how helpless she felt when she learned that the Keystone XL pipeline, designed to carry Canadian tar sands oil, would pass through Nebraska, where she lives, with potential spills jeopardizing the state’s ancient aquifer. Keystone was greased by more political money than the state had ever seen, so everyone assumed it was a done deal. Then Pipher took the radical action of hosting a potluck to figure out what to do. The movement she and a handful of friends helped launch grew quickly to include students, small-town ministers and business owners, and Republican ranchers and farmers. A Republican rancher named Randy became a symbol of the cause. Activists carried life-size cutouts of him with the message, “I Stand with Randy,” and his statement, “We will not be bullied.” When a TransCanada ad flashed on the jumbotron at University of Nebraska’s first football game of the season, eighty-five thousand fans booed. Obama eventually vetoed the pipeline’s expansion, the pipeline owner gave up, and a planned massive expansion of tar sands extraction never ocurred. Grief and Witness On the eve of COVID, the 33-year-old husband of two-time National Book Award winner Jesmyn Ward died from a sudden respiratory illness. His last words were: “Can’t. Breathe.” Ward takes us through her raging grief as she tries to stay strong for their children while finishing her novel about a young enslaved woman whose loved ones are ripped away from her. Four months after her husband’s death, cellphone videos showed George Floyd saying “I can’t breathe,” pleading for his life, while a policeman kept pressing his knee into Floyd’s neck. And protestors around the world began chanting her husband’s final words. Ward assumed Floyd’s death would simply pass, as had so many other police killings of Black men, women, and children. But “I can’t breathe” became the slogan of the Black Lives Matter movement, and people worldwide responded with a river of human solidarity that restored Ward’s hope. “The people marched, and I had never known that there could be rivers such as this, and as protesters chanted and stomped, as they grimaced and shouted and groaned, tears burned my eyes. They glazed my face.” “Even in a pandemic, even in grief,” she writes, “I found myself commanded to amplify the voices of the dead that sing to me, from their boat to my boat, on the sea of time.” The ripples of that powerful river of solidarity continue despite an administration that works to bury even the possibility of confronting America’s deep racial wounds. And the power of courage and witness continues. Only Justice Can Stop a Curse In “Only Justice Can Stop a Curse,” Alice Walker uses an ancient curse collected by Zora Neale Hurston to grapple with the politics of bitterness—the temptation to conclude that humans might actually deserve the destruction we’re capable of reaping. “Let the Earth marinate in poisons,” Walker thinks. “Let the bombs cover the ground like rain. Let the heated atmosphere go mad.” Might it be best, she wonders, if the ills we’ve created were indeed to end our tenure on this planet? But she cannot ignore the gifts of the earth, like the taste of fresh peaches and the courage of those who act for justice. And that expands her spirit, making her larger than her rage. Never complacent, never blind to human-constructed horror, Walker nonetheless chooses life. Walker’s answer isn’t sentimentality, but it’s an alternative to getting trapped in helpless bitterness. In the words of legendary organizer Heather Booth, it’s putting love at the center—honoring the richness of life and our bonds with our fellow human beings, no matter how difficult things get. And those connections can give us the strength to continue. Adapted from the wholly updated edition of The Impossible Will Take a Little While: Perseverance and Hope in Troubled Times. Basic Books, October 2026. theimpossible.org This appeared first in The Nation.

All Aboard Eugene Debs’s Red Special

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: Jacobin ／ 著者: Shawn Gude ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T13:31:31+00:00

The specially chartered locomotive pulled out of Chicago on a late August day in 1908, heading west, a sleeper car, dining car, and baggage car loaded with socialist pamphlets in tow. Red flags poked out, bunting and streamers festooning the sides. The passengers were in high spirits. Socialist Party presidential candidate Eugene V. Debs joked that he and his balding brother, Theo, were the resident “long hairs”: “We have three long hairs between us.” Neighbor Stephen Marion Reynolds, whose new biography of Debs was serving as campaign literature, pinned a red flag to his pajamas and explained, “Something might happen in the night, and they would find me dead or unconscious. How would they know I wasn’t a Republican?” As the train raced across the fields of Iowa — flat, flat, flat — a newspaper reporter asked Debs what he’d enjoyed most on the trip so far. “Oh, associating with this brace of despots, I suppose,” Gene quipped. Debs’s speaking trip was a cavalcade of railroad depots and opera halls, scheduled speeches and impromptu whistle-stops. His September 2 itinerary read like this: Des Moines, Iowa: Depart at 6 a.m. Creston, 9:40–10:10 Corning, 10:50 to 11:20 Clarinda, 12:20 to 1 p.m. Burlington Junction, Missouri, 1:45 to 2:15 p.m. Kansas City, MO: 3:30 p.m. (speech that evening) He spoke at each stop, plus at least six other unscheduled ones. Sometimes he mounted a platform outside the Red Special — as Debs’s campaign train was known — speaking as Theo and others distributed campaign literature in the crowd. Other times, he simply rained down words from the caboose. He attracted converts, critics, and the merely curious. At the Corning, Iowa, stop, a woman, sneering, asked a man clad in overalls whether he’d accepted a socialist book. The man — half-sheepish, half-unapologetic — showed he had. Briefly, Debs considered cutting south to speak in the minefields of Birmingham, Alabama, where members of the United Mine Workers were engaged in a daring interracial strike. He ultimately judged the detour cost-prohibitive given the cramped budget. The Red Special continued speeding west. (The landmark strike was soon crushed by the government, wiping out the local union and, with it, the rare organization able to challenge Jim Crow.) It was Debs’s third presidential bid in a row. His Socialists would again try to beat the vast structural odds by reaching the toiling masses rally by rally, pamphlet by pamphlet. By sheer exertion. By relying on the “Jimmie and Jennie Higginses,” as the selfless rank-and-file members of the Socialist Party were known. People like Sarah Cluff of Provo, Utah, and E. Z. Ernst of Olathe, Kansas. Eugene Debs, on the presidential stump: “We know that economic conditions determine man’s conduct toward man, and that so long as he must fight him for a job or a fortune, he cannot love his neighbor.” Cluff was in her early fifties, an English immigrant who had toiled at a lace factory as a young girl and raised three children in poverty. Opinionated and resolute (she’d successfully divorce her husband a few years later on grounds of cruelty), Cluff was part of the volunteer army that sold Appeal to Reason , the massive socialist paper out of small-town Kansas that was proving a linchpin of Debs’s campaign. As a younger woman, Cluff had been mystified about “why it was so hard to get enough to subsist on.” Then she’d studied socialism. It was “the only light,” she told the Appeal , “that can dispel the darkness for working people.” Ernst, also in his fifties, was a farmer and artisan in northeast Kansas who’d once led a sixty-acre socialist commune. An 1896 illustration in the paper Ernst edited at the time showed him with short hair and a long tangle of muttonchops and a beard, as if while clearing farmland the underbrush had leapt onto his face and taken up residence. By the 1908 election, he’d been a radical agitator for decades, passing out literature, selling subscriptions, and knitting together town and countryside, so crucial to Socialist success in the rural Midwest. Sarah Cluff and E. Z. Ernst weren’t outliers. Socialist Party membership had doubled since the last presidential election to 40,000, and a staggering four thousand volunteer speakers were available to work the soapbox. In Milwaukee, party members would venture out every Sunday morning, arms chock-full of literature and buttons, brains swimming with answers to questions they’d receive at the doorstep. They could take heart that the headline-grabbing Red Special was barreling across the country at that very moment. The Red Special experienced some hiccups once it reached the Pacific Coast. The campaign was already running low on money, threatening an embarrassingly early end to the trip. Debs turned to average party members, whose nickels and dimes filled up the campaign coffers. The show would go on. Supporters contributed more than just money. They would pile onto the iron caravan at each stop, their enthusiasm infectious, sometimes bearing gifts. One day in Oregon, Theo described the bounty to his daughter: “The baggage car is half full of the finest fruit I have ever seen. At every station our supply is increased: pears, plums, seedless raisin grapes, and such bunches, strawberries, watermelons, and today a comrade handed in a magnificent string of fresh mountain trout.” They wolfed it down for dinner that evening, joined by Socialist rank-and-filers, who could pay two cents a mile (and fifty cents a meal) to ride for several stops. Eugene Debs, photographed circa 1912. (Bain News Service / Library of Congress) Along the road, Debs saw the unmistakable sign of recession: workers on the “tramp.” One day, shortly after leaving San Bernardino, California, the train stopped when a tramp was found on board attempting to snag a ride. Normally, the man would have been tossed off the train. But this was the Red Special. He was instead ushered up “to ride with a presidential candidate on the plush,” Debs recounted with a laugh. “Of all the workers who are tramping,” Debs told a reporter, “we have not seen one of the type usually used for caricatures in the comic sections of the Sunday newspaper editions. All we have encountered since leaving Chicago on this campaign train have been fine young fellows, the same kind that have helped make this country what it is today, but have been thrown out of work by the trend of the times.” And what use was an economic system that regularly made millions beg for a job? As Debs crucified capitalism, its leading champions mounted a defense. Earlier that summer, President Theodore Roosevelt had used a memorial ceremony near his twenty-three-room summer home in Long Island to charge Debs and his compatriots with promoting “equality of reward” rather than “equality of opportunity.” Socialists would sweep away a system that valued “energy, foresight, intelligence, thrift, courage” in favor of one that allowed the undeserving to “rob his neighbor of what that neighbor has earned.” (The dedication ceremony was for a monument to an early English settler named John Underhill, the famed leader of a colonial militia that massacred American Indians.) Debs couldn’t conceal his scorn, unloading on this “well-groomed president of the ruling parasites” in an Appeal article. What “equal opportunity” was there for the unemployed workers teeming the breadlines? What “equal opportunity” was there for the child laborers “fed alive to Mammon”? It was capitalism, not socialism, Debs growled, that had a skewed system of rewards — bequeathing “colossal private fortunes” for owning rather than toiling. Debs was more gracious with Lincoln Steffens, the Progressive journalist, who interviewed Debs in Milwaukee shortly after Roosevelt’s address. Sympathetic but skeptical, Steffens pushed Debs on the nuts and bolts of his socialist vision. Debs explained that in the “cooperative commonwealth,” as he called it, everyone able to work would have a job. No one would be terrorized by the “fear of starvation.” Workers would be “paid according to their social use” rather than the profit-minded calculations of the free market. “Skill and ability would count” in determining remuneration, but workers performing undesirable jobs (say, sanitation) would also get better pay or shorter hours. Labor-saving technology would be used to reduce drudgery. Pay wouldn’t be equal, but nobody would pile up money like a Rockefeller (or a Roosevelt). Wasn’t Debs worried, Steffens asked, that people would lack an incentive to work? He wasn’t. Human beings had an innate need to be active, he explained, and they were motivated by a range of concerns other than making the most money possible. And “a society that produced for use and not for profits would have plenty of work to have done.” Plus, with much shorter hours, all workers would have ample leisure time, when they could “enjoy healthily all the good things of the earth.” What about individuality? Wouldn’t socialism “level men down”? Here, Milwaukee Socialist Victor Berger, who joined Debs for the interview, jumped in with characteristic gusto: “The capitalist system — doesn’t it level most men down now? Yes, it takes all the individuality, all the courage, self-respect, liberty, and beauty out of the great mass of men to produce a few” economic titans. Debs agreed. Theodore and Eugene Debs during the 1908 presidential campaign. He slapped down the stereotype that socialists fomented class hatred (“There are classes now and we say so. Why not? It’s true”) and that they wanted to seize personal property. “Socialism does not abolish private property,” he explained, as he’d surely explained so many times before, “except in the means of production. We want all men to have all they produce, all; we are for [personal] property; it is Capitalism that is against it.” Nor was Debs worried about running a socialist economy. He cited capitalism’s recurring depressions and panics as “proof that capitalists cannot handle industry, business, nor even money.” He noted one of the contradictions at the heart of capitalism, what economists call the “overproduction problem”: employers want to keep labor costs as low as possible, but this means that workers don’t make enough money to buy the goods they produce. And so, Debs explained, “The surplus grows, a load on the market, till the crash comes, production halts, men are discharged, prices fall, and — there’s your panic.” Debs thought the solution was simple: socialize the economy, remove the profit incentive, distribute the economic pie more equally, all fixed. Even in the Steffens interview, he never laid out precisely what a democratic socialist economy would look like. Yes, the railroads and steel mills and banks and other large industries would be publicly owned, managed in some capacity by a democratically accountable government. But what role would their workers play in directing these enterprises? How would socialists ensure that an expansive state didn’t become another haughty boss? And what of smaller firms? Would they be run as worker cooperatives? What role might markets play? Like many socialists of the era, Debs seemed to think it was preemptive, even undemocratic, to sketch out a postcapitalist blueprint. That was for the people themselves to decide (and the socialist intellectuals to agonize over). He certainly underestimated the difficulty of managing a modern economy. And yet this fuzziness about the nitty-gritty turned off fewer people than we might imagine. Catholic priests fulminating against “ungodly” socialism and major-party newspapers smearing “violent” socialists probably dissuaded more workers from voting for Debs than the charge that he lacked a fully fleshed-out alternative. Many Americans had lived through a Civil War that had extinguished an entire economic system in the South. Many remembered a time before industrial capitalism. The Socialists’ claim that they would harness the economic trends and technologies of the day and simply transfer them to democratic, public hands seemed less far-fetched to early twentieth-century ears than twenty-first-century ones. Or as Debs ended the interview, soaring back into the heavens: We know that economic conditions determine man’s conduct toward man, and that so long as he must fight him for a job or a fortune, he cannot love his neighbor. Christianity is impossible under capitalism. . . . When things are rearranged so that I can help my fellow man best by helping myself, by developing all my skill and strength and character to the full, why, then, I shall love him more than ever; and if we compete it will be as artists do, and all good men, in skill, productiveness, and good works. By the end of September, Debs had traveled nine thousand miles and delivered 187 speeches over twenty-five days in every western state and territory, to an estimated 275,000 people. Exhausting. So exhausting, in fact, that occasionally, when it was an especially small town and Gene’s throat felt especially aflame, Theo would fill in for his brother unannounced, the doppelganger convincing the crowd that this balding man gesticulating wildly before them was the famed Eugene V. Debs. And still the East Coast lay ahead. There were some highlights out east: Gene’s wife, Kate, joined him for this leg of the journey, and in Trenton, Ohio, a scrum of children dashed through the rain after school to greet the Red Special — “one of the most beautiful compliments paid us on the entire trip,” Debs told the kids, choking up. “This train,” he explained, “represents an effort to make it possible for all children everywhere to have time for play, and time to go to school, and the opportunity to grow up.” The band struck up a tune, and they were off. Debs’s train rumbled through nearly every state in the Upper Midwest and Northeast, clattering textile towns and cacophonous manufacturing cities. He marched in front of a parade stretching ten full blocks to Boston’s Faneuil Hall. He sold out New York City’s Hippodrome Theatre, with 7,500 paying admission and 2,500 more turned away. After working himself into a sweat — a photograph from the event shows Debs in a three-piece suit speaking from the wooden floor of the hall, his sea-blue eyes flaring like a tropical storm, his hands in a flurry so fierce the shutter could only register a blur — Debs climbed into an open-roof car and headed down to the Lower East Side. By that point in the journey, Debs admitted, “I was little more than a portable corpse.” The newspapers reported that he was so fatigued it “was painful to witness,” and Debs told those assembled at Hamilton Fish Park he wouldn’t be giving a speech because “I am not able to do so.” Kate was watching from a box at the Hippodrome — accompanying New York Socialist Morris Hillquit and his wife, Vera — with what must have been a mix of pride and dismay. What would be left of her Gene after the cause had gotten through with him? Her husband pushed on. Turning back west, continuing to fill speaking engagements along the way, the Red Special made an impromptu stop at Harpers Ferry, West Virginia, the site of John Brown’s doomed antislavery rebellion. Standing at a monument marking the spot where Brown was captured, Debs delivered brief remarks: Brown “dared the whole world and gave up his life for freedom. What more can a man do? . . . [Ralph Waldo] Emerson has said: ‘The time will come when John Brown will have made the gallows as glorious as Jesus Christ made the cross.’” Cheers and tears. William Lowery, a former slave who owned the scuffed-up tin candlestick that lit John Brown’s fort, gifted Debs the timeworn object. Theo found it the “most thrilling and dramatic” moment “of the entire trip.” The Socialist Party was making an explicit appeal to black voters that year despite their minor electoral heft. (Ninety percent of African Americans still lived in the Jim Crow South.) A party pamphlet cast Debs, rather hyperbolically, as “the John Brown and William Lloyd Garrison and Frederick Douglass and Abraham Lincoln of the new emancipation all rolled into one.” Jobs and political rights for all — Debs’s “new emancipation” would rip out the roots of racial oppression (although the pamphlet omitted mention of any immediate civil rights measures). The party dispatched several black speakers, including the Reverend George Washington Woodbey, who traversed the Upper Midwest and the East Coast, culminating in a two-hour, eve-of-the-election address on a cold night in the increasingly black New York City neighborhood of Harlem. Other slices of the working class also received targeted appeals. Denmark’s A. C. Meyer, a Socialist member of parliament, crossed the Atlantic to campaign for Debs, often making his overtures in his native tongue at Scandinavian venues like the soaring five-story brick of Minneapolis’s Dania Hall. A poster for Debs’s 1912 presidential campaign. (Socialist Party of America) It had been an inspired campaign. Debs had crisscrossed the nation twice, traveled over 20,000 miles of tracks, forced his raspy voice to deliver addresses sixty-five days straight in “one of the most aggressive speaking campaigns ever undertaken by a presidential candidate,” according to one biographer. He had heard passing train drivers sound their whistles in solidarity, seen buggies queue for miles along the tracks and throngs so eager to hear his words they’d block the rails to make the Red Special stop. At his final campaign address, the Chicago crowd cheered him for twenty-seven minutes straight. The results of Debs’s 1908 Red Special campaign were disappointing: a mere 20,000 votes more than in 1904. Good for just 3 percent of the national vote, despite higher shares in strongholds like the Mountain West, the industrial Midwest, and the Great Plains. His Red Special, the campaign’s great expenditure at $35,000, had been funded by 15,000 contributions and $14,000 in ticket sales. J. P. Morgan and Andrew Carnegie alone had donated millions to the GOP. But all was not lost. Far from it. By Debs’s next presidential bid four years later, party membership had tripled to roughly 120,000; some 1,200 Socialists held elected office, including eighty mayors in thirty-six states; and strikes were cascading through the country, many led by Socialists. Maybe, just maybe, Eugene Debs’s “cooperative commonwealth” was still on the horizon. This article originally appeared in Jacobin , a democratic socialist magazine publishing long-form essays and analysis on politics, economics, and culture. Mary Church Terrell, Reproductive Loss, and the Meaning of Motherhood

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: AAIHS ／ 著者: Elizabeth Gonzalez ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T09:29:06+00:00

Most Dangerous Branch

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: LA Progressive ／ 著者: Sharon Kyle, Dick Price ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T05:53:38+00:00

The opinions expressed here are solely the author's and do not reflect the opinions or beliefs of the LA Progressive. Alexander Hamilton famously described the judiciary as the “least dangerous” branch of government. Unlike Congress, it controlled no purse. Unlike the president, it commanded no army. Its principal power was judgment. More than two centuries later, that description is being put to the test. In a special Left Coast Forum discussion presented by LA Progressive, USC law professor Jody Armour joined constitutional scholars Marjorie Cohn and Stephen Rohde to examine a much larger question than whether the current Supreme Court is conservative: Is the Roberts Court fundamentally changing the constitutional balance of power? That distinction matters. Courts have always issued decisions that large portions of the country strongly oppose. But Cohn and Rohde argue that something more consequential is happening today. Across cases involving presidential power, voting rights, independent federal agencies, immigration, guns, campaign finance and reproductive rights, they see a Court steadily transferring power away from Congress, administrative agencies and ordinary citizens—and toward the presidency and the Supreme Court itself. The Rise of the Unitary Executive Cohn began with one of the most consequential ideas underlying the current constitutional struggle: the unitary executive theory. The Constitution divides governmental authority among three branches designed to check one another. But the expansive version of unitary executive theory gives the president extraordinary control over the executive branch, including agencies Congress deliberately attempted to insulate from political pressure. That theory became particularly significant with the Supreme Court's 2024 presidential immunity decision. Cohn emphasized the extraordinary scope of the ruling: presidents receive absolute immunity from criminal prosecution for exercises of their core constitutional authority and presumptive immunity for other official acts. Rohde sees a fundamental contradiction. Conservative justices frequently invoke the intentions of the nation's founders through “originalism.” Yet, he argued, those same justices are enabling precisely the concentration of governmental power that the framers feared. George Washington warned against concentrating legislative, executive and judicial authority in the same hands because such accumulation was the essence of tyranny. For Rohde, the danger becomes particularly acute when one political movement exercises enormous influence over all three branches. When Precedent Becomes Optional The panel also examined the Court's treatment of stare decisis—the principle that courts generally adhere to previous decisions. Precedent is not absolute. Brown v. Board of Education , after all, required the Supreme Court to reject earlier constitutional doctrine supporting racial segregation. But Rohde argued that the Roberts Court increasingly treats precedent not as a stabilizing principle but as an obstacle that can be discarded when it conflicts with the majority's ideological objectives. The most dramatic example is Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization , which overturned Roe v. Wade and eliminated the constitutional right to abortion that Americans had relied upon for nearly half a century. The same conflict is appearing in disputes over independent federal agencies, where the Court has questioned longstanding restrictions on a president's ability to fire officials Congress intended to operate with some political independence. The consequence reaches far beyond individual commissioners. If presidents can remove officials simply because they disagree with their decisions, agencies intended to rely upon professional expertise can increasingly become extensions of presidential political power. Related: Build a United Front against Fascism and a Workers Party Voting Rights and the Courthouse Door Cohn focused particular attention on voting rights. The Voting Rights Act of 1965 was one of the central achievements of the civil rights movement. Yet the Roberts Court has steadily weakened its protections. The problem is not limited to what rights remain on paper. Rohde emphasized another, less familiar doctrine: standing. Before courts decide whether someone's constitutional rights have been violated, plaintiffs must establish that they have the legal right to bring the case at all. A restrictive approach to standing can therefore prevent courts from ever reaching the constitutional question. “The right can be difficult to enforce,” Armour observed, “if the people harmed cannot get through the courthouse door.” That seemingly technical doctrine can have enormous political consequences. Governing Through the Shadow Docket Another transformation receives considerably less public attention: the Supreme Court's growing use of its emergency, or so-called shadow, docket. Traditional Supreme Court cases normally involve extensive briefing, oral argument and written opinions explaining the justices' reasoning. Emergency decisions can happen much differently. Cohn explained that the Court increasingly makes enormously consequential decisions through abbreviated proceedings, sometimes without oral argument and with little explanation. Although these orders may technically be temporary, they determine which government policies remain in effect while litigation proceeds. That means temporary decisions can change people's lives long before the Court ever issues a final opinion. The emergency docket has become particularly important in disputes involving immigration, federal workers, executive authority, election rules and government programs. Related: UCLA 9/11 Symposium Sparks Battle Over Academic Freedom Is the Supreme Court the Most Dangerous Branch? By the end of the discussion, Armour returned to Hamilton's famous characterization. Has the “least dangerous branch” become the most dangerous? Cohn offered an important qualification. The Supreme Court may not itself be the most dangerous branch. Instead, she suggested, it may be enabling the most dangerous branch by removing constitutional restraints on presidential power. Rohde described the presidency and Supreme Court as “the most dangerous twins” in the present constitutional crisis. Their concern ultimately reaches beyond any single Supreme Court decision. It is about whether the constitutional system of separated powers remains capable of restraining government itself. “We the People” Yet the discussion did not end with nine justices. Armour asked what ordinary people can possibly do when Supreme Court justices hold lifetime appointments. Cohn's answer was succinct: organize, litigate, pressure Congress, write letters, protest and participate politically. Rohde went further. “The Constitution begins with the words ‘We the People,’” he reminded the audience. Throughout American history, constitutional change has not come exclusively from courtrooms. Abolition, labor rights, women's suffrage and civil rights required movements outside the courts that eventually transformed what government institutions were willing—or forced—to do. Related: The 2026 Midterms May Not End on Election Day Litigation remains important, Rohde argued, but lawsuits alone cannot protect constitutional democracy. Citizens must understand what the Court is doing, challenge elected officials about judicial appointments and constitutional reform, organize in their communities, demonstrate and vote. That may ultimately be the most important lesson of the conversation. The Constitution is not merely a document interpreted by nine people in Washington. It is a political compact whose survival ultimately depends upon millions of people insisting that its promises—and its limits on governmental power—still matter.

UAW President Shawn Fain Wins Mandate in Reelection Victory

フォルダ: 00_毎日確認 ／ 媒体: DSA Feed - Global ／ 著者: Detroit DSA ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T03:02:52+00:00

Kentucky Auto Workers joined the crowd in Detroit for the rally that kicked off the Stand-Up Strike September 15. Photo: Jim West, jimwestphoto.com . By Luis Feliz Leon and Jane Slaughter United Auto Workers President Shawn Fain has won a second four-year term, with an overwhelming lead in member balloting for the union’s top officers. The election was a referendum on the new fighting direction Fain has taken the union by leading 2023’s Stand-Up Strike against the Big 3 automakers, other bold contract fights, and new organizing campaigns. It marks the second time members have voted for their top officers in the union’s 91 years. UAW members voted to switch to a one-member-one-vote system in December 2021. Fain received 68 percent of the vote, against five opponents. His nearest competitor, incumbent vice president Rich Boyer, had 13 percent. Fain ran as part of the United UAW slate. His slate-mate for secretary-treasurer, Brandon Campbell, also garnered 68 percent, against two rivals including incumbent Margaret Mock. The slate’s candidates for the three vice president spots appeared on track to be elected with 19–25 percent each in a field of 11 candidates. (VP candidates needed 16.7 percent to win.) The slate also won all the regional director races that it contested. The sweep means that the slate will hold all the seats on the union’s International Executive Board except for one regional director. Fain won a majority of the vote in most locals, from assembly plants to legal services, and a plurality in almost all the rest. Boyer, who heads the union’s Stellantis division, won just two of the union’s dozens of big locals: Local 140, Warren Truck, his home local, and Local 1700 at Sterling Heights Assembly Plant, both near Detroit. Turnout was low and similar to the 2022 election, with a little over 10 percent of the union’s 400,000 active members and 600,000 retirees returning their mail ballots. (Retirees vote on top officers in the UAW.) While some expected Fain’s strongest support to come from the higher education and legal aid locals on the East Coast, turnout there was down by more than half from 2022, with fewer than 2,000 ballots mailed in. Turnout was up in Local 4811 at the University of California, which went 75 percent for Fain, but still only 2,500 of 60,000 members there voted. As in the last election, it was members in the big manufacturing locals that were key to Fain’s victory. Perennially online candidate Brian Keller had 7 percent of the presidential vote. As the ballots were counted he took to Facebook Live to swear at members for 30 minutes and call them “morons” for not voting for him. MOTIVATORS In phone interviews with a dozen members, auto workers cited pensions and post-retirement health care as their key motivators for backing Fain. They also raised concerns about tackling automation and job security. But the biggest issue was the better contracts the UAW has won since Fain took office. “Infighting” between Fain and his opponents on the outgoing International Executive Board — Rich Boyer, Margaret Mock, and Mike Booth — came up, but people drew widely different conclusions, with many repeating the phrase Fain “isn’t perfect.” Jeep worker Megan Fagan in Toledo said some members have checked out due to the internal drama, reinforcing the cynical view that not much has changed. For most, though, disagreements at headquarters were not of interest or on their radar. They looked at their pay stubs and voted accordingly. Boyer, Mock, and Booth came together to form the Integrity Slate but failed to offer a counter to Fain’s focus on fighting the companies and launching new organizing drives. Boyer’s main pitches in his campaign statement in the UAW magazine were to lower union dues and reduce the staff at union headquarters. “Fain and his slate are the only ones that have a real program for the future of the union,” said historian Nelson Lichtenstein, author of Walter Reuther: The Most Dangerous Man in Detroit . “You’d think the other people would have come up with something aside from they want to reduce dues, which means basically cripple the new organizing.” “The members chose Fain, despite the Monitor’s best efforts to create turbulence, because we’re ready to fight for retirement health care and protection from A.I. layoffs in 2028,” said Gary Chynoweth, vice chair of the reform network UAW Member Action , in a statement. “Our work now is to keep helping members step up as leaders in a union that’s ready to fight.” KEEP ON TRACK UAW Local 551 member Candyla Brown is one of 5,000 workers at Ford’s assembly plant in Chicago. She said she backed the United UAW slate to keep the union on track in 2028, to win pensions, post-retirement health care, and pay bumps that keep up with inflation. To win these demands, “we need strong leadership,” she said. “We had a good experience with the 2023 strike, and I’ve been watching the new organizing efforts,” she said. “I think the UAW has come a long way since Fain got into office.” She said organizing in the South is a bright spot. Volkswagen workers in Tennessee voted to join the UAW in 2024, becoming the first unionized foreign-owned auto assembly plant in the South. At Daimler Truck in North Carolina, Jon Greene, president of Local 3520, said Daimler has staffed up its labor relations personnel in preparation for another showdown, and corporate HR has been closely following the election. “We tripped them up with the last contract,” said Greene. “It wasn’t any more of that friendly bargaining that had been happening over the years with International, so we caught them off guard.” RENEWED MANDATE With a renewed mandate, the pressure is on Fain’s team to organize members to step up when contracts expire at the Big 3 automakers on May 1, 2028. In 2023, the companies were caught off guard, accustomed to a sedate strike, if any, and modest union demands. When the union launched a spreading strike against all three companies at once, with more plants walking out week by week, company bargainers were blindsided. They gave up more in that one contract than they had in the previous four contracts combined. They will likely be better prepared for May Day 2028. Unions may face another obstacle in 2028 — in an election year, Democratic politicians traditionally bring heavy pressure not to rock the boat, fearing blame from voters. In 2023, though, a poll found 78 percent of Americans supported the UAW’s strike. Some expensive union demands were left on the table last time: pensions and retiree health insurance for workers hired after the big concessions of 2007–2009, and protections against mandatory overtime and automation. Flush with victory in fall 2023, Fain urged other unions to line up their contracts with the Big 3’s, for a united assault on corporate coffers. Domanick Moss, of Local 1853 at General Motors’ Spring Hill, Tennessee, assembly plant, said he backed United UAW because it built a broad coalition and set a goal of preparing for a potential May Day 2028 general strike. “I think that we have a solid ground laid in terms of how we’ve been connecting with other unions,” Moss said. “If enough of us go on strike, we could get more attention on universal health care. I would like to see Medicare for All and the PRO Act passed. I would like to see consequences for corporations doing rampant automation.” (The PRO Act would make it easier for workers to organize and secure contracts.) GOVERNMENT HANDS OFF UAW The election results are a sharp rebuke to the government-appointed monitor , Neil Barofsky, whose original mandate was to oversee clean-up of the endemic corruption that sent 13 union officials to jail. Barofsky developed a strong antagonism to Fain when the UAW Executive Board passed a resolution calling for a ceasefire in Gaza in late 2023, which the monitor said could be seen as “antisemitic.” Fain resisted Barofsky’s attempts to reverse that stance. Ever since, Barofsky has issued a series of reports criticizing Fain and siding with his opponents on the Board. Some UAW members who followed the drama drew cynical conclusions, but apparently most voters found Fain’s actions spoke louder than the monitor’s words. Barofsky’s mandate is set to end in early 2027, but the consent decree keeps open the possibility of extending the monitorship. If Barofsky’s previous behavior is any indication, he may push to stay in place longer. Fain’s opponents made much of the monitor’s reports, which accused Fain of misdeeds including retaliating against Boyer and Mock, after he relieved them of some of their major responsibilities. Opponents spent most of their energy attacking him personally and detailing their own grievances, rather than talking about the direction of the union. Their campaigns did not have much reach. Fain, on the other hand, reminded members of the union’s achievements and tried to keep the disagreements from looking personal. Despite encouraging leadership at the top, the UAW has a long way to go to become a member-driven union that consistently takes on the boss. While Fain has charted a new direction, much of the union bears the scars of decades of concessions, or is still stuck in the old, company-friendly ways of operating. That includes seeing the union structure as a career ladder, a way to get off the factory floor. Years of one-party rule means that the bench of members able and willing to push the union in a new direction is still shallow. That has the potential to change in locals where the International has brought big resources for contract campaigns and strikes. The UAW Member Action network is slowly working, through education and trainings, to build up the bench. After this election, it looks like the UAW will continue to be back in the fight. {A longer version of this article appeared in Labor Notes and Jacobin. ] UAW President Shawn Fain Wins Mandate in Reelection Victory was originally published in The Detroit Socialist on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.
