The African revolutionary Amílcar Cabral once asked his counterparts throughout the world at late-night strategy sessions: How did they wage successful armed struggle? The post Letters from the American Empire: October 12, 2026 appeared first on MR Online .

Beyond Polarity: For a Marxist Theory of the World Order

フォルダ: 06_左派ニュース・社会主義戦略 ／ 媒体: [:en]THE TRICONTINENTAL[:es]TRICONTINENTAL[:] ／ 著者: 不明 ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T08:39:25+00:00

Collage by Tricontinental: Institute for Social Research drawing from a Dymaxion map projection and Islamic, Mesoamerican, Ethiopian, and Chinese maps made between the twelfth and seventeenth centuries. Images sourced from: Muhammad al-Idrisi, Tabula Rogeriana, 1154, 1929 reconstruction and transliteration by Konrad Miller via Wikimedia Commons; Matteo Ricci and Li Zhizao, Kunyu Wanguo Quantu (坤輿萬國全圖), 1602, Library of Congress Geography and Map Division via Wikimedia Commons; Códice Xólotl, Lámina 1, Tetzcoco, Mexico, c. 1541 via Wikimedia Commons; Tigrayan circular map from the Māshafā Aksum (Book of Aksum), pre-eighteenth century, Bibliothèque nationale de France, Collection Antoine d’Abbadie, via Wikimedia Commons; and Justinkunimune, Blank World Map, Dymaxion Projection , 2023, Wikimedia Commons, CC0 1.0. The maps in this dossier trace how the hegemonic world order has organised and represented the world, from colonialism, the post-World War II international order, and Cold War bipolarity to internationalised capital, the North–South divide, and US unipolarity. More than representations, they distil concepts imperial and colonial powers have used to exercise power. Every dot of ink is a choice: what sits at the centre, which countries appear at the top, and which distortions are considered acceptable. The cover gathers cartographic traditions with different centres, orientations, and cosmologies, asking how, beyond poles and blocs, we might understand the unequal social relations that constitute the world order itself. The Mercator projection was developed in 1569 for European navigation during the colonial period. During imperial expansion, it became the standard map projection, still used today. This map, which shows the spheres of influence of the ‘great powers’ and their ‘possessions’, was created by H.E.C. Robinson in 1908. Sourced from the National Library of Australia via Wikimedia Commons. The language of ‘unipolarity’ and ‘multipolarity’ – whether global power is concentrated in one dominant state or distributed among several major states – has become almost unavoidable in political debates. US and allied policymakers have often described the post-Cold War international system as a ‘liberal international order’ increasingly threatened by ‘revisionist powers’ such as China and Russia. Many governments and movements in the Global South, by contrast, welcome what they see as the emergence of a multipolar world in which Washington can no longer dictate the terms of international life. This aspiration is understandable. Any weakening of the United States’ capacity to impose blockades and unilateral sanctions, organise coups, and wage wars can open political space for sovereign development and for class struggles to advance. ‘Multipolarity’ may function as a slogan of resistance to US primacy and offer a rough description of how certain forms of state power are distributed. Its political appeal, however, should not be confused with explanatory power. The number of powerful states tells us little about the social relations that produce their power, the class interests organised within them, or the imperialist hierarchies that connect them. Multipolarity does not, by itself, provide a Marxist account of the world; when treated as such, it can mislead us into believing that a wider distribution of state power is inherently progressive. 1 Venezuela’s former President Hugo Chávez, who had every reason to welcome a world of many centres, was dissatisfied enough with the term ‘multipolarity’ to coin ‘pluripolarity’ against it – insisting that what mattered was not the number of centres but whether popular subjects were the agents of transformation. 2 The need to specify this political content was itself revealing: the number of poles alone tells us nothing about the class forces or popular subjects shaping the resulting order. A Marxist analysis must therefore replace the arithmetic of poles with the analysis of a chain: an unevenly developed system of social formations, each internally constituted by class struggle and externally bound to the others by relations of exploitation and dependence. The question is not how many poles there are but how the chain is held together and where it is most susceptible to being broken. The problem, then, lies in the image of the pole itself. It asks us to picture the international system as a set of sovereign states treated as its decisive units, each possessing a measurable quantity of power. Depending on how that power is distributed, the system is described as unipolar, bipolar, or multipolar. This vocabulary tells us how many great powers count as geopolitical poles. It does not tell us what kind of social order each state reproduces, how its power is produced, which class interests shape that power, or through which transnational relations it is exercised. It photographs the chessboard while hiding the social relations that made the board, carved the pieces, and decided who is permitted to play. Polarity presents itself as a form of measurement. Measured by gross domestic product (GDP) at purchasing power parity (PPP) – which adjusts for differences in price levels between countries – the world appears economically plural: no single economy commands anything resembling the dominance that the word ‘unipolarity’ implies. Measured by military expenditure and the alliance systems organised around it, however, that same world appears far more concentrated: in 2022, the United States alone accounted for about 53.5% of global military spending, while the US-led military bloc accounted for 74.3%. The United States also maintained at least 902 foreign military bases – by far the world’s largest network. 3 Measured in a third way – by control over critical markets and infrastructures that function as chokepoints in contemporary production and exchange – the world appears neither plural nor bipolar but close to monopolistic. Yet this concentration does not map neatly onto states. Four firms hold 56% of the world’s commercial seed market and 61% of the global pesticide market. One of them is Bayer, which absorbed Monsanto and its patent portfolio in 2018. 4 Three firms – Eli Lilly, Novo Nordisk, and Sanofi – control more than 90% of the global insulin market by value and around 95% of the market in low- and middle-income countries. 5 Palantir, a single US firm, supplies the data platform on which the US Army runs its operations and US immigration authorities track human beings. 6 The same concentration appears in finance and energy. The dollar accounts for more than four-fifths of global trade finance – a predominance that far exceeds the US share of global output or trade. This is a form of monetary command that a ranking of national economic weight cannot capture. 7 In energy, ownership of a resource can likewise diverge from control over the circuits through which it reaches the market. This divergence is visible in neighbouring Venezuela and Guyana. Venezuela has owned its oil outright since 1976, yet US aggression – whether military (such as the 3 January bombing), economic (over 1,000 sanctions and other unilateral coercive measures), or otherwise – continues to shape the conditions under which Venezuelan oil can be sold internationally. In neighbouring Guyana, the 2016 production-sharing agreement governing the offshore Stabroek Block restricts the government’s ability to alter its terms without the prior written consent of the ExxonMobil-led consortium. 8 Three measurements, three different worlds, one reality. The disagreement between those who see unipolarity and those who see multipolarity is therefore not simply a disagreement about the facts. It is also a disagreement about which relations constitute power – and it is precisely this question, which must be settled before any counting can begin, that the vocabulary of poles leaves unanswered. Polarity also inherits what is often called a Westphalian representation of power: a world composed primarily of sovereign territorial states treated as internally coherent units. This obscures how territorial states, capitalist markets, chartered colonial companies, and cross-border circuits of capital accumulation developed together. The Dutch East India Company, founded in 1602, possessed extensive commercial and quasi-sovereign powers – including the authority to wage war, conclude treaties, and build forts – decades before the 1648 Peace of Westphalia. 9 The history of the modern capitalist world system is therefore not one in which fully formed sovereign states appeared first and corporations only later crossed their borders. Capitalist power combined territorial, commercial, and imperial forms from the beginning. The 1941 Atlantic Charter set out an Anglo-American vision for the post-war order, affirming the right of ‘all peoples to choose the form of government under which they will live’ while Britain still ruled a vast colonial empire. Map created by MacDonald Gill in 1942. Sourced from the PJ Mode Collection of Persuasive Cartography, Cornell University Library via Wikimedia Commons. The US Genealogy of Polarity The modern vocabulary of polarity emerged from the institutional and political world of post-1945 US political science. It had older antecedents in European discussions of the balance of power, as we shall see, but it was in the United States, amid the behavioural revolution, systems theory, Cold War strategy, and the rapid expansion of university-based national-security research, that ‘polarity’ came to be treated as a formal property of the international system. The central figure in the consolidation of polarity as a theory of international structure was Kenneth Waltz, the principal architect of neorealism. Rather than beginning with the internal character of states, neorealism sought to explain their behaviour through the constraints of an anarchic international system and the distribution of capabilities among its leading states. Waltz built on the work of Morton Kaplan, whose System and Process in International Politics helped translate the diplomatic history of balances of power and alliances into abstract, rule-governed models. 10 Kaplan organised international politics into six models – the balance of power, loose bipolar, tight bipolar, universal, hierarchical, and unit-veto systems – giving a systems-theory scaffolding to what had previously been studied largely through diplomatic history. Both Kaplan and Waltz studied at Columbia University under the international relations scholar William T. R. Fox, who coined the term ‘superpower’ in 1944. 11 At a time when US political science increasingly sought to model political behaviour in formal, scientific terms, Kaplan and then Waltz drew on general systems theory to analyse international politics. The histories of imperialism, revolution, and class struggle were displaced by abstract interactions among formally comparable units. Class did not have to be explicitly refuted; it disappeared from the object of study itself. Waltz gave this systems approach its canonical neorealist form. In ‘The Stability of a Bipolar World’ and more systematically in Theory of International Politics , Waltz argued that the international system was anarchic – meaning that no central authority stood above sovereign states. Within this system, states were treated as functionally similar despite differences in their political and social systems and distinguished principally by the distribution of capabilities among them. 12 The domestic orders of these states could differ profoundly, but Waltz’s structural theory abstracted from those differences, and his models transcended ideology. The United States and the Soviet Union could therefore be treated as functionally comparable units occupying two poles, and their conflict could be analysed primarily through the structure of bipolarity rather than through the antagonism between their social systems. Whether capitalism or socialism organised production in each country was treated as an internal difference rather than as something that constituted the international system itself. This abstraction was not innocent. It converted a historically specific Cold War conjuncture into a general theory of international politics. Within this framework, anticolonial revolution, competing social systems, unequal exchange, and US imperialism became analytically subordinate to the number and relative weight of the strongest states. The resulting framework closely reflected the concerns of a US national-security establishment focused above all on strategic stability and the management of rivalry. During the Cold War, Waltz argued that bipolarity was more stable than multipolarity because the two superpowers depended less upon allies and could assess one another’s capabilities more directly. As the USSR disintegrated, this same conceptual vocabulary was repurposed to describe US primacy. In his 1990 Henry M. Jackson Memorial Lecture, Charles Krauthammer argued that the apparent plurality of world power concealed the predominance of a single superpower: the United States. 13 The concept soon migrated from political commentary into realist scholarship. William Wohlforth’s ‘The Stability of a Unipolar World’ (1999) argued that the scale of US preponderance made counterbalancing difficult and major-power war less likely. 14 In the same year, Samuel Huntington described the emerging order as ‘uni-multipolar’: one superpower operating alongside several major powers whose cooperation it often required. 15 Although these authors disagreed over the number of poles and the distribution of capabilities among them, they shared the same underlying conception of the international system: states as comparable units whose relative capabilities determined its structure. As US predominance has eroded in some areas while remaining entrenched in others, the language of polarity has returned to the centre of debate. The categories inherited from Kaplan and Waltz are now used to ask whether China, Russia, India, Brazil, and other states constitute independent poles. The debate commonly turns on indicators such as GDP, military expenditure, population, technological capacity, and diplomatic reach. Yet ‘multipolarity’ is often used less to identify a coherent structure of world power than to describe the relative decline of US power. What often remains unquestioned across these successive descriptions – bipolarity, the unipolar moment, and now multipolarity – is the assumption that territorial states are the basic containers of power and that world order can be understood by ranking them. The limits of this approach become clearer when we try to interpret the war in Ukraine, the expansion of BRICS+, or the different positions and policies of China, Russia, India, and Brazil. These states cannot be treated as equivalent units merely because they resist, bargain with, or operate outside particular US initiatives. Their ruling blocs, forms of property, relations to international finance, popular pressures, and positions within the imperialist chain differ sharply. Those differences help explain why governments that cooperate within BRICS+ or another multilateral institution may diverge sharply on other questions and why the same state may resist US pressure in foreign policy while reproducing exploitation domestically and relations of dependency internationally. Judged by its own criteria, however, the predictive record of this framework after the Cold War has been uneven at best. Structural realism was never merely descriptive: its proponents used it to make concrete predictions about how the post-Cold War order would develop. In 1993, Waltz argued that balance-of-power pressures would push Germany and Japan towards full great-power status, including the acquisition of nuclear weapons. 16 Working from similar realist premises, Mearsheimer argued in 1990 that if US and Soviet forces withdrew from Europe and the Cold War alliance system dissolved, Europe would return to a multipolar order more prone to instability. 17 Neither prediction was borne out. Germany and Japan did not acquire nuclear weapons. The United States remained militarily embedded in Europe. NATO expanded. A Marxist explanation for this sequence of events points instead to relations that structural realism largely sets aside. In Germany and Japan, dominant class fractions were integrated into US-centred circuits of accumulation while their state apparatuses were reshaped within the wider US-led order. 18 This structure did not fail to determine outcomes; the structure was never the one Waltz described. John Mearsheimer’s work illustrates both the strength and the limits of this tradition. His rejection of the language of ‘democracy promotion’, used to justify NATO’s eastward expansion, can illuminate the strategic pressures surrounding the war in Ukraine more clearly than accounts framed primarily in terms of liberal values. However, by treating great powers principally as functionally similar security-seeking states, realism cannot adequately explain the class forces that shape Russian, Ukrainian, European, and US policy. Nor can it explain the material interests organised through energy, finance, arms production, and property relations or the unequal imperial structures within which strategic choices are made. A critique of liberal ideology is necessary, but without a social analysis of the states and class forces involved it remains incomplete. Paradoxically, the language that now announces the end of US predominance remains rooted in a conceptual framework developed within the US national security academy during the Cold War. The social and class relations that helped shape the post-Cold War order are precisely those which this framework tends not to register. This map depicts the two antagonistic blocs of the Cold War and the geopolitical logic of bipolarity. Robert M. Chapin, Two Worlds 1950, 1950. Source: Time, 2 January 1950, via the PJ Mode Collection of Persuasive Cartography, Cornell University Library via Wikimedia Commons. The Westphalian Model and the World Market The theory of polarity rests on what is often called a Westphalian representation of the international order: a world composed of territorially bounded states, each sovereign within its borders and formally equal to the others, interacting in the absence of any higher authority. The term refers to the Peace of Westphalia, the agreements concluded at Münster and Osnabrück in 1648 that ended the Thirty Years’ War – a devastating conflict in Central Europe shaped by religious divisions, dynastic rivalry, and struggles over political authority within the Holy Roman Empire – and were later treated in international relations theory as the birth of the modern sovereign-state system. Yet the 1648 treaties of Münster and Osnabrück did not suddenly invent sovereignty, nor did they produce a clean division between domestic hierarchy and international anarchy. Rather, they settled the war within a layered dynastic and imperial order in which political authority remained overlapping, unequal, and socially heterogeneous. Historians and international relations scholars have therefore challenged the ‘Westphalian myth’ – the idea that the 1648 settlements created the modern system of sovereign states. 19 The importance of Westphalia lies less in what the treaties immediately created than in what they later came to symbolise in international theory: the supposed birth of a world composed of territorially bounded sovereign states. For Waltz, Westphalia served as a useful historical boundary. In Theory of International Politics he argued that from Westphalia onward no more than eight major states had at any one time competed for mastery over the world system. Waltz used this early European example to demonstrate the structural problem of multipolarity, largely setting aside the personalities of individual rulers and the details of diplomatic manoeuvring. A second route to thinking about multipolarity emerged from Henry Kissinger’s study of the Congress of Vienna. His 1954 Harvard dissertation, later reworked and published in 1957 as A World Restored , examined how Castlereagh and Metternich used legitimacy, restraint, and equilibrium to stabilise the post-Napoleonic settlement. 20 Kissinger asked how diplomacy could manage a multipolar order. Waltz asked how the number and relative capabilities of great powers constrained diplomacy regardless of who governed them. Together, these approaches established the two dominant US conceptions of multipolarity: one centred on diplomatic management and the other on systemic structure. Within both accounts of interstate order, economic relations remain secondary to relations among sovereign states. What recedes from view is the historical development of mercantile, industrial, and financial capital; the formation of national and international markets; and the legal and political institutions governing trade, property, labour, and investment. Waltz acknowledged that large corporations ‘and other nonstate actors are difficult to regulate and may rival some states in influence’. 21 Even though the ‘importance’ of nonstate actors is ‘obvious’, he argued, it does not make the ‘state-centric conception of international politics’ obsolete. The issue, then, is not whether Waltz recognised non-state actors but whether they can be treated as secondary forces operating within a system whose basic structure is already defined by states. From a Marxist standpoint, they cannot: the capabilities attributed to states are themselves produced through relations of property, production, finance, empire, and class struggle, which cross territorial boundaries. For Marxists, this omission matters because modern territorial states did not first develop as sealed political units and only later enter capitalist economic relations. The modern state system and the capitalist world market developed together. The consolidation of European sovereignty was bound up with enslavement, dispossession, and colonial rule abroad. 22 The formal equality of sovereign states in international law has long coexisted with deep material hierarchy across the world system: colonies denied sovereignty, formally independent debtor states, occupied territories, protectorates, and states whose policy space is constrained by creditors, trade rules, sanctions, and military threats. Modern territorial sovereignty itself was produced over centuries through war-making, colonial conquest, capitalist property relations, and class struggle. The pole metaphor converts these relations into attributes of individual states. Financial power appears as something the United States possesses rather than as a set of relationships reproduced through the international role of the dollar, central and commercial banks, debt contracts, payment systems, corporate accounting, and the constraints these arrangements place on other states. Contemporary production is organised through global value chains in which corporate control, manufacturing, logistics, data, intellectual property, and the location of profits are geographically dispersed. A corporation may own few factories yet control thousands of suppliers through standards, patents, purchasing contracts, and digital platforms. Workers across the Global South can be integrated into the same transnational production networks while remaining divided by borders, wage regimes, migration controls, and differential exposure to environmental harm. Lead firms and financial owners tend to capture disproportionate shares of value and profit while suppliers, workers, and states bear much of the risk. A polarity framework has no convincing way to capture this kind of power. If a US-based technology firm organises production in East Asia, extracts data globally, holds intellectual property in one jurisdiction, books profits in another, depends on minerals from Africa and Latin America, and relies on US courts, diplomacy, export controls, and military protection, to which pole does its power belong? Calling it simply ‘American’ misses the transnational organisation of accumulation. Treating it as ‘stateless’ misses the state-backed legal and coercive systems that make its property claims enforceable. The Marxist point is therefore not that corporations have replaced states. Capital requires states to define and enforce property rights, discipline labour, absorb losses, build infrastructure, police borders, and defend access to markets and resources. Multinationals operate across borders yet remain dependent on territorial authority. They exploit differences among national tax, labour, and regulatory regimes while relying on powerful home states and bargaining with host states. The relation is neither a world of sovereign billiard balls nor a borderless empire of corporations. It is a differentiated unity of states and capitals – distinct forms of power that are nevertheless deeply interdependent. 23 This relational understanding of power has deep roots in Marxist theories of imperialism. Vladimir Lenin’s theory of imperialism already points beyond a simple map of great powers. Lenin defined imperialism through five connected features: the concentration of production and capital into monopolies; the merging of bank and industrial capital into finance capital; the growing importance of the export of capital; the formation of international monopolist capitalist associations; and the territorial division of the world among the major capitalist powers. For Lenin, the division of the world among great powers mattered not simply because several powerful states existed but because this division was bound up with capitalist accumulation and uneven development. A contemporary theory must extend this insight to global production, dollar-centred finance, digital monopolies, the expansion of extraction into new ecological frontiers, and the legal architecture of intellectual property and investment – not retreat to counting poles. At the Second Congress of the Communist International in 1920, the debates over the Theses on the National and Colonial Question – including Lenin’s draft theses and the supplementary theses presented by the Indian revolutionary M. N. Roy – asked how communists should distinguish the oppressed from oppressor nations, analyse the class composition of anticolonial movements, relate workers and peasants to the national bourgeoisie, and combine temporary alliances with the political independence of the proletariat. In this framework, interstate relations, national liberation, and internal class struggle formed interconnected parts of a single strategic problem. Mao Zedong’s On Protracted War (1938) offers a masterful example of this method in the context of China’s war of resistance against Japan. Mao’s analysis began from the concrete balance of forces: China’s social and political conditions and vast geography, Japan’s military and economic strengths and weaknesses, and the changing international situation. The Vietnamese revolution likewise defeated French colonialism and US imperialism by combining an analysis of domestic classes, the social and political contradictions inside the United States, and the possibilities created by relations and divisions among states with different social systems and political alignments. Cuba’s revolutionary diplomacy – from its defence of sovereignty to its internationalist activity – has repeatedly demonstrated the same capacity to work within interstate contradictions without reducing politics to relations among states. This map was created in 1956 as an advertisement by a major US bank to show its expansion overseas, especially in Latin America, Africa, and Asia, highlighting international financial circuits and capital accumulation. Sourced from an advertisement by the First National City Bank of New York in the article ‘How Bankers “on the Scene” Make the Difference Overseas’, the PJ Mode Collection of Persuasive Cartography, Cornell University Library via Wikimedia Commons. States, Internationalised Capital, and the Imperialist Chain Nicos Poulantzas developed Lenin’s account of imperialism in a direction that is particularly useful for understanding the relationship between national states and internationalised capital. Although Poulantzas did not produce a separate theory of geopolitics, his writings on the capitalist state, social classes, and the internationalisation of capital provide important elements for such a theory. In State, Power, Socialism (1978), he described the state as a ‘ specific material condensation of a relationship of forces among classes and class fractions’. 24 For Poulantzas, the state does not stand outside of class struggle: the balance of forces among classes and competing sections within them takes material form in its institutions and policies. The state is neither a neutral actor nor simply the instrument of a unified capitalist class. Capital itself is divided into competing fractions – such as industrial, commercial, and financial capital – whose interests do not always coincide. The state consequently has a degree of ‘relative autonomy’ from any one fraction, allowing it to organise what Poulantzas called a ‘power bloc’: the alliance of dominant classes and class fractions whose competing interests are held together under the leadership of a hegemonic fraction. Foreign policy should therefore be understood neither as an automatic expression of capitalist interests nor as the pursuit of a timeless national interest. It is shaped by the changing balance of class forces expressed through the state. In Classes in Contemporary Capitalism (1975), Poulantzas applied this approach to the internationalisation of capital. The expansion of multinational corporations, he argued, did not abolish national states or generate a single borderless capitalist state. International capital still had to be reproduced within concrete social formations and through national states, which guarantee property, regulate labour, provide infrastructure, manage crises, and organise political consent. The internationalisation of capital therefore takes place not outside or above national states but through changes within them. Foreign capital becomes embedded in domestic production, ruling-class alliances, administrative institutions, and state strategies. Imperial relations are thus reproduced not only through external pressure between states but also through the class relations and institutions within the states they connect. Poulantzas developed the category of the ‘internal bourgeoisie’ to describe fractions of the domestic bourgeoisie whose accumulation remained nationally grounded but had become entangled with foreign capital. In post-war Europe, he argued, important sections of the bourgeoisie were neither autonomous national capitalists nor passive agents of the United States. Their capital remained rooted in their own social formations, but its reproduction had become intertwined with US corporations and international circuits of accumulation dominated by US capital. These bourgeoisies could bargain with US capital and sometimes conflict with it while remaining structurally dependent on the wider imperial order. Imperial hierarchy was consequently reproduced not only through pressure exerted by one state on another but also within the power blocs of subordinate states themselves. 25 This brings us back to the Leninist conception of the imperialist chain, now understood through Poulantzas’s theory of the state. Its links are not identical sovereign states distinguished only by how much power they possess. Each link is a social formation with its own combination of classes, institutions, political struggles, and forms of dependence. The chain is marked by uneven development: states occupy different positions within global production, finance, military power, and technological control. But these positions cannot be determined by ranking states according to national capabilities alone because the relations of imperialism also run through the internal class structure of every state. A Poulantzian analysis would therefore ask questions that the theory of polarity does not. Which class fractions constitute a state’s power bloc? Which fraction exercises hegemony within it? How is foreign capital embedded in domestic production and state institutions? Which corporations depend upon which states to protect their ownership, markets, technologies, and supply chains? How do popular struggles alter the balance of forces within the state? Such questions do not make interstate conflict irrelevant – they explain its social content. Poulantzas helps us move beyond the false choice between a world of sovereign billiard balls and a borderless empire of multinational corporations. Capital is internationalised, but political power remains unevenly organised through a plurality of national states. States are nationally constituted, but their internal class relations are also shaped by international capital and imperial hierarchy. Geopolitics is not an autonomous contest among territorial states but the international organisation of class power through a differentiated system of states. This also helps us understand the power of transnational corporations. Global supply chains and international finance allow large monopoly firms to operate across jurisdictions, but their existence and expansion depend decisively upon states. US digital and technology monopolies rely on public research, procurement, subsidies, courts, patents, export controls, diplomatic pressure, intelligence systems, and military power. Other monopoly capitals likewise depend upon the states in which their ownership and strategic command are anchored. At the imperialist stage, the integration of state and monopoly capital does not end when finance crosses borders; it is reproduced internationally through an unequal system in which capital operates across national boundaries while retaining privileged relationships with particular state apparatuses. The erosion of particular forms of sovereign power under the pressure of international finance must therefore not be confused with the disappearance of the state. Even a subordinate capitalist state remains a central apparatus through which the dominant classes organise exploitation, secure property, discipline labour, manage social conflict, and reproduce their power. Integration into the imperialist chain changes the balance of forces within the state and may compel its ruling bloc to cede policy space, but it also makes the struggle against imperialist subordination part of the class struggle within that state. The relationship between class power and national sovereignty is consequently concrete rather than abstract. Sovereignty exercised by a capitalist ruling class is not popular sovereignty, yet breaking or loosening mechanisms of imperial command can enlarge the terrain on which workers and other popular forces struggle. The 1980 Brandt Line, overlaid onto a Gall-Peters map projection, depicts the division between a wealthier industrialised North and a poorer, largely formerly colonised South. Sourced from the cover of North-South: A Programme for Survival, the report of the Independent Commission on International Development Issues chaired by Willy Brandt, Pan Books, 1980. A Contradictory World Order Neither unipolarity nor multipolarity adequately captures the present world order. US predominance has eroded, but it has not simply disappeared. The United States remains the world’s largest military spender, sits at the centre of an unparalleled system of alliances and overseas military bases, and benefits from the dollar’s central place in reserves, trade, debt, and payments. 26 Its power is institutional and relational, not merely an aggregate of national resources. At the same time, China has become a major industrial and technological centre. Russia retains formidable military capabilities and vast natural resources. States across Asia, Africa, and Latin America have widened their room for manoeuvre. The expansion of BRICS+ reflects real demands for representation, development finance, and freedom from Western tutelage in the Global South. Yet these tendencies do not add up to several equivalent poles. Military command, industrial capacity, finance, energy, technology, natural resources, and political legitimacy are distributed in different patterns. A state may be militarily protected by the United States, industrially tied to China, dependent on Gulf energy, exposed to private creditors, and responsive to a domestic popular movement – all at once. Interdependence does not abolish hierarchy, and rivalry does not end integration. US and Chinese capital may compete over technology while firms in both countries remain connected through production and markets. The semiconductor industry illustrates this layered geography: intellectual property, chip design, fabrication, lithography (the specialised technology used to print microscopic circuits onto chips), mineral extraction, assembly, and final markets are concentrated in different territories and controlled by different combinations of corporations and states. No single national ranking captures this structure, even though state power remains decisive through subsidies, export controls, sanctions, infrastructure, and military alliances. Sanctions and so-called ‘de-risking’ policies designed to reduce strategic dependence on rival economies disrupt or redirect some economic circuits while finance and commodity exchange bind others together. Meanwhile, the climate crisis, care work, migration, and social reproduction create material pressures that no great-power ledger measures. These different distributions of power did not emerge independently of one another: they were reshaped through a connected transformation of global capitalism. From the 1970s onward, US-based capital increasingly relocated labour-intensive production abroad while retaining or strengthening control over functions such as design, intellectual property, standards, branding, finance, and the legal and coercive infrastructures that sustained them. The industrialisation of several US-aligned East and Southeast Asian economies – including the ‘Asian Tigers’ as well as Malaysia, Thailand, and others – and the financialisation of the North American and British economies were two aspects of the same process. What appears in one register as US decline – the loss of manufacturing employment – and in another as continuing US structural power – the dollar, legal and financial institutions, export controls, and military bases – can therefore be understood as related outcomes of the same restructuring. As strategic control over production became increasingly separated from the physical location of manufacturing, major conflicts began to emerge not only over territory but also at the points where this control is exercised, such as export controls on lithography and chip design, sanctions on payment systems, the extraterritorial application of domestic law, and struggles over technical standards and data. A ranking of great powers cannot readily capture these conflicts because it converts the relations that produce them into attributes of individual states. A Marxist theory of geopolitics should begin not with the number of powerful states but with the historically developed totality of capitalist social relations. It would ask how surplus is produced, appropriated, and transferred; how ownership and control are organised across commodity chains; how finance disciplines states and households; how race, caste, gender, and citizenship segment the global working class; and how ecological costs are displaced onto dominated regions. It would examine the changing balance of forces within each state, locate that state within the unequal relations of the imperialist chain, trace how particular blocs of capital remain nationally anchored while accumulating transnationally, and examine how state institutions pursue the conditions for accumulation, political legitimacy, and security under different historical circumstances. Applied to the present world order, this method refuses to treat China, Russia, India, or Brazil as socially or politically equivalent simply because they participate in BRICS+ or favour a less US-dominated order. Rather, it asks in each case how each state emerged historically, which classes and class fractions make up its power bloc, how different forms of property and planning shape accumulation, how labour and peasant struggles constrain it, how domestic capital relates to international finance, and whether its external actions reproduce or weaken imperial hierarchy. Only such an analysis can explain why states align in some areas and diverge in others rather than inferring their politics from their position on a geopolitical map. Such a theory would also distinguish among political struggles and projects too often collapsed into the category of ‘great-power competition’. These include conflicts among fractions of capital, projects of sovereign development, socialist experiments, national liberation movements, and struggles against transnational corporations by workers, peasants, women, Indigenous peoples, and other impacted sectors. Their political content cannot be deduced from whether they oppose Washington, nor can every assertion of sovereignty be treated as emancipatory. The relevant questions are concrete: does a project weaken mechanisms of imperial domination? Does it expand popular control over production and social wealth? Does it reduce dependency and hierarchy, or does it reproduce exploitation under a new flag? A state can increase its strategic autonomy without transforming its domestic relations of exploitation. Conversely, greater sovereign control over finance, resources, technology, or infrastructure can create conditions for popular forces to pursue more fundamental transformations. Neither outcome can be deduced solely from a state’s opposition to the United States. This map, created by the US government, divides the world into the geographic ‘areas of responsibility’ assigned to US military commands. Sourced from the United States National Imagery and Mapping Agency, The World with Commanders’ Areas of Responsibility, 1999, via the Library of Congress Geography and Map Division and Wikimedia Commons. For this reason, struggles over national sovereignty cannot be dismissed as distractions from class struggle. In states subordinated within the imperialist system, control over finance, resources, food, technology, infrastructure, and foreign policy can be indispensable to any popular programme. A loosening of links in the imperialist chain may be valuable even when initiated by a capitalist state because it can weaken external constraints and alter the conditions of domestic struggle. However, its political meaning depends on which class forces lead it, who gains control over sovereign capacities that are recovered, and whether popular organisation can convert state autonomy into social transformation. Multipolarity, then, has a limited but real political meaning. A less concentrated interstate order can make unilateral coercion more difficult and create openings for the Global South. Those openings can assist national liberation projects and advance class struggles within capitalist states. However, alignment with other states against US primacy does not alter a state’s class character or abolish its exploitative social order. There is no automatic path from several centres of state power to equality among peoples. A world of competing capitalist powers can remain profoundly imperial, exploitative, and ecologically catastrophic. The task is not to choose the correct number of poles. The image of a balance treats power as a set of comparable weights whose distribution can be rearranged. A chain, by contrast, is composed of unequal and interlocking relations. It is transformed not simply by rearranging its links but by breaking the relations that bind them at the points where those relations are weakest. The decisive question is therefore not where a state is strongest but where imperial relations are most vulnerable to rupture. For Marxists, the horizon cannot be a better balance among ruling states alone. It must be the international power of working peoples: popular sovereignty against imperialism, cooperation against zero-sum rivalry, social ownership against monopoly, ecological repair against endless accumulation, and internationalism rooted in popular struggle. On 4 September 2026, the UN General Assembly voted 164 to 1 to promote equal-area projections (shown in red) that more accurately represent the relative size of continents, challenging distortions such as Mercator’s enlargement of northern landmasses (shown in blue). The only opposing vote was from the United States. Mercator and Equal Earth projection comparison by Tricontinental: Institute for Social Research. The Land to Those Who Work It: Land Reform and Socialist Agriculture in the German Democratic Republic

フォルダ: 06_左派ニュース・社会主義戦略 ／ 媒体: [:en]THE TRICONTINENTAL[:es]TRICONTINENTAL[:] ／ 著者: 不明 ／ 公開日時: 2026-10-13T08:39:25+00:00

About Studies on the DDR The German Democratic Republic (Deutsche Demokratische Republik, DDR) was a socialist state founded in East Germany in 1949 as a democratic, anti-fascist reaction to the Second World War and the subsequent restoration of monopoly capitalism in West Germany. The DDR represented a new Germany – one in which the land was redistributed, the means of production socialised, agricultural production organised into cooperatives, and significant advances made in gender equality, education, healthcare, and other areas of society. The DDR also cultivated friendly and close-knit economic relationships with other socialist states and exercised political and material solidarity with countries and movements fighting for their independence in Latin America, Asia, and Africa. In this series, we use the German acronym DDR instead of the English acronym GDR specifically because it has become a positive point of reference in many parts of the world and especially for countries in the Global South. After the reunification of Germany in 1990, the DDR’s economy was dismantled and treated as a prototype for the shock therapy austerity measures that were soon imposed on other countries – not just the former socialist states. At the same time, the DDR was politically, judicially, and morally delegitimised. The publications in this series are a rejection of the narrative propagated by enemies of socialism, both new and old, that the demise of the DDR – and other socialist projects – proves the inevitable failure of any socialist policy and economy. Through the series Studies on the DDR, the Internationale Forschungsstelle DDR (International Research Centre DDR), and Tricontinental: Institute for Social Research seek to stimulate a renewed engagement with the history, achievements, and principles of the DDR. After all, millions of people around the world are still fighting for advancements that were once a given in the socialist system of the DDR but were lost with its downfall. 1. Introduction The state of contemporary global agriculture and food production urgently begs for alternatives. If the global food supply were distributed fairly and equally, everyone would have enough to eat. Yet, in 2025, 645 million people – roughly one out of thirteen – worldwide went hungry. 1 Prices are further inflated through financial speculation on food, raw materials, and land, perversely ensuring that profits are reaped not only from basic goods needed for survival but also from growing rates of hunger as rising commodity prices themselves become targets of speculative trading. 2 While just over one-third of the world’s food continues to be produced by small farmers, 3 massive agricultural corporations such as the ABCD Group – Archer Daniels Midland, Bunge, Cargill, and Louis Dreyfus – dominate global food cultivation, processing, marketing, and distribution. 4 The result is that companies like those in the ABCD Group have a near monopoly over advanced agricultural technology and increasing control over globally centralised production and processing chains. This concentration of land ownership and the means of food production reinforces patterns established during the colonial era, driving monocultures, one-sided trade relations, and land evictions. These dynamics, in turn, exacerbate small-scale subsistence farming, extreme exploitation of agricultural workers, poverty, hunger, environmental destruction, and rural maldevelopment. While the development of capitalist relations in agricultural technologies and science has unleashed significant potential for food production, this has clearly not been used to satisfy people’s most basic need for food. Decades after the dissolution of the DDR, we look back at its achievements – and what is to be learned from its shortcomings – for answers. In the DDR’s mere forty-five years of existence, the conditions in rural areas changed fundamentally. This change took place in three stages: The first stage (1945–1948) was during the shift in the balance of power in East Germany after the Second World War, which marked the decisive starting point for the DDR’s agricultural transformation. During this period, land reform was implemented that broke up the centuries-old land ownership structures in the countryside. This process of democratisation created the first framework for new relations of production based on cooperation. The second stage (1952–1960) was characterised by the Genossenschaftsbewegung (cooperative movement), which was able to resolve the contradictions between modern technology and small-scale production in the interests of farmers and agricultural workers without ruining, displacing, or subordinating them to the interests of big business, as occurs under capitalism. By the same token, agricultural cooperatives also provided the rural population with previously unattained social and cultural rights and opportunities. The third and final stage (1970s–1980s), built on the foundation established during the previous periods, saw the development of large agricultural production networks and deepening cooperative relationships between the various stages of production, from primary production to processing and distribution. The DDR’s agricultural reform was among the most successful and comprehensive programmes in the country’s short history, transforming the reality of the peasantry from feudal-like working and living conditions (including economic and political subordination and cultural deprivation) into one marked by confidence, democracy, cultural vibrance, and highly productive industrial agriculture. Every country and region has its own history and conditions that must be carefully analysed and for which unique solutions must be found. Nevertheless, the decisions made in the DDR’s agricultural sector offer practical insights for an alternative to capitalist market competition and profit-seeking. This study aims to contribute to contemporary debates on the prospects of socialist agriculture by examining the historical process that fundamentally changed the social structures and ways of working and thinking in the East German countryside. It is not possible to cover all aspects of the DDR’s agriculture in this study; important questions around forestry, ecology, agriscience, internationalism, and the inner workings of the Democratic Farmers’ Party (Demokratische Bauernpartei Deutschlands, DBD) could only be touched upon in passing and certainly deserve to be explored further in the future. This study is structured chronologically, following the concrete historical development of agriculture in Germany: first the centuries-old feudal yoke imposed on Germany’s peasantry, then the period of land reform and, finally, the transition to a cooperative organisation of agriculture and large-scale industrial agricultural production. 2. The Prussian Path to Capitalist Agriculture and the Rise of Fascism German agriculture transitioned from feudal to bourgeois-capitalist conditions during the nineteenth century, relatively late compared to other European countries like England and France. 5 Even then, it only occurred very gradually and without a radical overthrow of the feudal aristocracy, who retained their hold over the land. The unification of Germany into a modern bourgeois nation-state was brought about by the political manoeuvring and warmongering of the Prussian ruling class, led by Otto von Bismarck and Kaiser Wilhelm I. During this period – referred to in Marxist literature as the ‘Prussian path to capitalism’ – the emergence of the capitalist mode of production was tightly bound to relations of feudal subordination. The living and legal conditions of the various peasant working masses changed, but their economic and social dependency on the landed nobility remained. A class of ‘free’ small and medium peasants 6 emerged, most of whom were heavily indebted to the landlords. Servants and sharecroppers who used to be landless farmhands for the lord of the manor now crystallised as a new class of agricultural workers. The feudal landlord himself became the owner of a sort of proto-agribusiness. Village life continued to be shaped by the large landowners, who maintained an economically and politically dominant role well into the twentieth century that was upheld by laws, customs, traditions, and new capitalist dependency relations. Backward, almost medieval-era conditions thus prevailed in the countryside. Feudal laws, which had ostensibly been abolished in 1919 after the fall of the German monarchy, remained a de facto reality for agricultural workers and servants who were prohibited from organising politically or in trade unions such as in the German Agricultural Workers’ Association (Deutscher Landarbeiterverband, founded in 1909). Excerpts from the Prussian Servants’ Regulations, which was established in the early 1800s, reveal how labourers were still deprived of basic rights: Paragraph 77: ‘If the servants provoke the lord’s anger through improper behaviour and are treated by him in return with insulting words or petty abuse, the servants may not seek legal redress’. Paragraph 79: ‘Except in cases where the life and health of the servant is in present and unavoidable danger due to maltreatment by the lordship, he is not permitted to actively resist the lord’. Paragraph 74: ‘Without prior knowledge and permission of the lordship, servants may not leave the house, even for their own affairs’. Paragraph 76: ‘The servants are to accept the orders of the lordship and their reprimands with deference and modesty’. 7 From the early nineteenth to the twentieth century, Germany’s economic base underwent a fundamental shift from an agro-industrial to an industrial economy. The population nearly tripled, from around 18 million in 1800 to over 52 million in 1900. 8 While most Germans lived in the countryside in the early nineteenth century, from 1871 to 1910 – in the wake of the urban migration that accompanied capitalist development – the percentage of the population living in cities (defined at the time as having more than 2,000 residents) rose from 36% to 60%. 9 Meanwhile, from 1882 to 1907, the share of workers engaged in agriculture and forestry dropped from 42.5% to 28.6%. A large class of landless agricultural workers soon outnumbered the owners and tenants of farms in rural areas. In 1907, large estates (greater than 100 hectares) employed an average of 51 labourers, and one-third of the 9.5 million people working in agriculture were wage labourers. 10 Agricultural wage labourers were concentrated in the areas east of the Elbe River, where large agricultural enterprises were most widespread. Foreign seasonal workers, especially from the Polish regions, were particularly exploited. In the mid-nineteenth century, there was a significant concentration of land ownership: less than 1% of landowners held more than 40% of arable land, while around 80% of farmers owned less than 12%, consisting of individual plots under 7.5 hectares. 11 Conditions in the German countryside developed unevenly throughout the nineteenth century because of the country’s territorial fragmentation into many small states. West of the Rhine River, serfdom and indentured servitude had been swept away with the French Revolution of 1789. In central and southern Germany, between the Rhine and the Elbe Rivers, small and medium-scale farmers worked parcels of land with leases to large landowners. But when serfdom was abolished by law in the areas east of the Elbe (which would later make up much of the DDR’s territory), peasants were forced to compensate their former masters. Ultimately, to be emancipated from feudal obligations and duties, peasants paid a total of 1.8 billion marks and had to give 425,169 hectares of land to the Junkers , Prussia’s landed nobility. 12 This intensified the already greatly unequal distribution of arable land and gave rise to the large, landed estates that shaped the East German countryside prior to 1945. Furthermore, the Junkers east of the Elbe were a central pillar of the reactionary, militaristic Prussian state and alongside influential industrial monopolists, would later become the facilitators of Hitler’s fascism. As historian Siegfried Kuntsche points out, the aristocratic Junker class used the key positions it retained in Germany’s military and state bureaucracy to openly support reactionary forces during the Weimar era (1919–1933), with ‘representatives of several long-established noble families join[ing] the Nazi movement’ as far back as the early 1920s. 13 In the region of Mecklenburg, one of the heartlands of the Junkers, a quarter of all landowners became members of the Nazi party. 14 Half of the generals in the high command of the fascist Wehrmacht (armed forces) came from nobility. 15 During the Second World War, Junkers used their already dominant role in village social life to promote the theft of Eastern European land, pitting peasants against their counterparts in the East and diverting attention from the Junkers’ own history of appropriating peasant land. Under Hitler (1933–1945), German fascism pursued a policy of the complete subjugation of peasants and the rural population to enable its imperialist war policy. Organisations and unions of agricultural labourers were banned. Everyone involved in the production, processing, and trade of agricultural produce was forced to become a member of the Nazi Reich Food Estate, or Reichsnährstand , under the command of large landowners. A total delivery obligation was enforced during the war, and peasants’ savings and credit balances were confiscated to pay for the cost of war. The fascist state compensated for the rapid decline in crop yields during the war years by imposing delivery quotas on produce from occupied countries in Eastern Europe and enforcing a merciless starvation policy against local populations. Only through the barbaric exploitation of millions of forced labourers were the fascists able to keep German agriculture afloat. Nazi Germany’s wartime policies created a major setback for the country’s agricultural development. As early as the mid-1930s, the war economy was prioritised over agriculture. ‘If necessary, we will be able to cope without butter, but never without cannons’, as Nazi propaganda minister Joseph Goebbels put it in 1936. 16 The damage incurred by the war left machinery and large tracts of land in ruin and livestock decimated. This was especially true in East Germany, where the fascists sought to ensure that nothing could be left behind for the Red Army and proceeded to systematically slaughter livestock and raze the soil – what Gerhard Grüneberg, the Socialist Unity Party of Germany (SED) agriculture secretary from 1960 to 1981, described as the continuation of the Nazis’ ‘criminal scorched-earth policy’. 17 3. Land Reform: Junkers’ Lands in Peasants’ Hands Post-War Destruction and Hunger East Germany was the main European front for the final battles of the Second World War, leaving large areas of agricultural land, facilities, and processing plants in ruin; means of transport practically non-existent; and production capacities set back by decades. As the Red Army advanced on German territory, many Junkers fled to West Germany, often taking their livestock, technical equipment, and other private possessions with them and thus further worsening the supply situation. Swine livestock was reduced to 20% of its pre-war levels, cattle to 66%, and poultry to 25%. 18 As a result of all these factors, the domestic food industry collapsed completely in 1945. Image 1. Farmers remove debris and stones from a field in the East German village of Podelzig, which was almost destroyed in the final days of the Second World War, 1945. After the war, Germany was divided into four military occupation zones. The Eastern Zone (known as the Soviet Occupation Zone, SOZ) was under the control of the Soviet Military Administration, and the Western Zones were controlled by the United States, the United Kingdom, and France. Berlin, which was located in the SOZ, was similarly divided among the four allied powers. In the SOZ, the German Administration for Agriculture and Forestry (Deutsche Verwaltung für Land- und Forstwirtschaft) was set up to deal with the urgent task of securing the first post-war harvest. On 18 June 1945, the Soviet Military Administration abolished the Nazis’ total delivery obligation by passing Order No. 40, which established delivery targets for grain, potatoes, vegetables, and oilseeds, followed shortly thereafter by Order No. 18, on 29 July 1945, which put an end to the compulsory delivery of livestock products. These were among the first measures implemented that promoted a democratic agricultural policy and helped spark the peasantry’s interest in increasing production. Soon thereafter, a two-price system was established: farmers were paid a lower price for produce that they were required to sell to the state to ensure that there was sufficient food for the population and a higher price for surplus produce, which they could sell freely. Administrative bodies, trade unions, and newly permitted parties – which were united in the anti-fascist democratic bloc – called on the population to help with the harvest. Engineers and industrial workers formed crews to repair agricultural machinery as townspeople went into the fields to help with the harvest and peasant committees were formed in the villages to coordinate the work, combat hoarding, and ensure that the population received their food rations. The Land Reform Programme The Soviet Occupation Zone During the war, German communists in exile in the Soviet Union developed a land reform programme for the post-war period. Edwin Hoernle was one of the main architects of this programme. Hoernle, an agricultural expert and communist parliamentarian during the Weimar era, returned from exile in May 1945 and took over as president of the Administration for Agriculture and Forestry (the main administrative position for agriculture and forestry in the newly created German Economic Commission). The meticulous planning and viability of this programme garnered support among the Communist Party of Germany (KPD) and its base, which in June 1945 issued a public appeal for democratic land reform under the slogan Junkerland in Bauernhand! (‘Junkers’ lands in peasants’ hands!’) and campaigned for a swift deliberation and implementation by the four-party bloc. On 22 August 1945, the KPD’s central committee adopted a concrete proposal to implement a land reform programme by the end of the year. Its central points were: To expropriate, without compensation, large private estates (over 100 hectares in size). To expropriate, without compensation, all property belonging to Nazi functionaries and war criminals, regardless of size. To create a state land fund consisting of the expropriated property as well as state-owned assets. To allocate the largest part of the land fund as private property in small, individually owned plots (five hectares in the case of high- and medium-quality soil and eight to ten hectares in the case of poor- and very poor-quality soil). To distribute livestock and basic production equipment to ‘new farmers’, i.e. former agricultural workers, landless peasants, or refugees. To transfer tractors and large agricultural machinery as well as workshops and agricultural processing plants to peasants’ mutual aid committees for collective use. 19 Image 2. On 11 September 1945, three municipalities near Dresden, East Germany, expropriated and divided up the former Royal Manor. The sign (front left) reads ‘Free farmer on free soil’, and the banner (upper right) reads ‘The master’s time is up’. The land reform initiative was promoted in a variety of settings, from the press to churches and party committees, and found increasing support among the population. Hoernle and the KPD argued that compensating the large estate owners for their expropriated land would be irresponsible given the supply shortages and general economic destitution. As they pointed out, by no means would large estate owners be reduced to beggars if their estates were expropriated without compensation, as they still retained their private belongings and accumulated wealth. Furthermore, this was considered an appropriate punishment for war crimes and an act of historical justice. 20 Since the Christian Democratic Union of Germany and Liberal Democratic Party of Germany – both members of the anti-fascist democratic bloc alongside the Social Democratic Party of Germany and the Communist Party of Germany – opposed the proposal for expropriation without compensation, there was a series of lengthy negotiations before the legal ordinances calling for expropriation without compensation were ultimately passed by the five state governments within the Soviet Occupation Zone in early September 1945. Image 3. A 1945 poster from the KPD’s central committee which reads: ‘Away with Junker rule: Junkers’ lands in peasants’ hands’. The Western Zone Popular pressure and the approval of land reform in East Germany in early September 1945 prompted the US and British military administrations in the West to draw up legal proposals for land reform in their zones. Yet the legal provisions they drafted offered politicians and large landowners enough leeway to ultimately prevent redistribution measures or to delay them until the balance of political power had shifted in their favour. The bourgeois parties forming in West Germany at this time sought to ensure that the old private capitalist ownership relations were preserved, despite popular referenda demanding the democratisation of the economy through the nationalisation of industry and other measures. The West German region of Hessen is an example of this desire for change. Here, the social democrats and communists held a majority in the state government and drafted a state constitution that, in Article 41, outlined plans to socialise key industries and place the banking sector under public administration. Though a public referendum in December 1946 showed that 72% of voters were in favour of Article 41, the US military administration nevertheless opposed it, and US General Lucius Clay prohibited its implementation. The land reform laws being drafted in West Germany were indicative of the rural population’s desire for change. Ultimately, however, the rural population was left disappointed, as the few measures enacted in the West applied to only 5% of the total arable land. In the DDR, meanwhile, 3.2 million hectares were expropriated and redistributed – fourteen times more than in West Germany. 21 The Political and Economic Background of Land Reform Neither the Soviet Military Administration nor the German communists pursued the goal of socialist construction in East Germany in the immediate aftermath of the war. Rather, from 1945 to 1952, their main objective was to foster an anti-fascist, democratic transformation of a united Germany; eliminate German imperialism and militarism; and ensure that post-war Germany would become a neutral demilitarised zone in the international rivalry between the capitalist and socialist states. 22 As such, land reform – i.e., the parcellation of land as private property – cannot be seen as a policy of the DDR’s socialist construction, which was first initiated in 1952, seven years after the war but as an effort to address pressing practical issues in the post-war era. Among them was the need to rebuild the collapsed food supply in East Germany by promoting small-scale agriculture, which motivated farmers to produce more and offered them the opportunity for self-sufficiency. Edwin Hoernle summarised the main reasons for land reform under these specific conditions: The great agrarian reform is above all a decisive means for the complete eradication of Prussian militarism, whose main supporter was the Junker caste. Economically, the handing over of the land to the self-employed peasant is also urgently needed. In light of the current ruin of agriculture and the enormous lack of technical equipment, only proverbial peasant diligence, the fanaticism with which the smallholder farmer clings to his land, can bring about a rapid increase in agricultural production. 23 Nonetheless, land reform was a decisive step that later enabled the eventual transition to socialism. The democratisation of the countryside and the encouragement of cooperation among the peasantry laid the socioeconomic foundations for the eventual creation of socialist relations in agriculture. This policy was first and foremost the fulfilment of the peasants’ demand for the redistribution of land and an end to feudal oppression. Walter Ulbricht, the leader of the Socialist Unity Party of Germany (SED), which emerged from a merger between the Communist Party of Germany (KPD) and Social Democratic Party of Germany (SPD) in April 1946, summed up this connection at a party conference in 1947: To be frank, some people asked us at the beginning of the land reform: What kind of socialists are you? You’re not even in favour of the ‘collectivisation of farmland’. We responded to them: That might suit you, gentlemen, keeping the large estates together so that they could later be easily returned to their former owners! No, our course is different: we are acting to ensure that the large estates are completely divided up, so that the large landowners in Germany can never again reclaim this land. 24 In the short term, the structures created by land reform were able to significantly improve the food supply, both for the individual farmers and the whole country. However, it was clear from the outset that the small-scale farming facilitated by land reform would not be able to meet the increasing demands of food production in the long term, as it faced challenges resulting from the size of the allotted agricultural land, the limited availability of equipment, and other factors. Image 4. Members of a land reform commission in the DDR measure a parcel of land in September 1945. Image 5. Young people help dismantle an old estate house to obtain building materials for new farmhouses. The Implementation and Impact of Land Reform In East German public village assemblies in September 1945, agricultural workers, landless and smallholder peasants, and refugees 25 elected members from their ranks to land reform commissions that were tasked with coordinating the implementation of the reform. The commissions determined which land was to be expropriated; documented and secured an inventory of associated buildings and stocks; drew up lists of smallholder and landless peasants, agricultural workers, and refugees; and assessed land allocation applications submitted by individual peasants. Redistribution plans were discussed and ratified in public meetings in which all applicants for land allocation participated. In order to avoid favouritism, decisions regarding the allocation of plots, stock, and inventory were made by drawing lots. The land reform commissions were also responsible for creating the first peasants’ mutual aid committees, established in the third quarter of 1945. These committees ensured that agricultural machinery was used efficiently and that mutual aid was organised among new farmers, from providing each other with advice on managing pests to lending helping hands during the harvest. Solidarity was to replace competition or obedience in the countryside. More than 52,000 people 26 participated in the 9,500 land reform commissions, 38% of whom were agricultural workers, 35% of whom were smallholder and tenant farmers, and 12% of whom were resettled refugees. The remaining 15% were industrial workers and village craftsmen. The responsibility for implementing land reform was thus placed in the hands of the social forces that had the most vested interest in the reorganisation of land ownership. The executive boards of the commissions, consisting of five to seven members, were usually chaired by the district’s mayor. By the end of 1945, most of the land marked for individual reallocation had been redistributed, and the landed nobility was generally expelled from the estates and villages over which they had reigned. Since the implementation of the land reform was overseen by the local population, the commissions came to act as schools of democracy. For instance, the handover of the title deeds for allocated land became a day of celebration in the villages. Local party member Hermann Wesemann’s account of a public meeting in Torisdorf, Schönberg county, paints a vivid picture of how land reform was implemented at the village level: Torisdorf was an estate of around 400 hectares. It belonged to Junker Axel Bunger, a staunch militarist who let ‘his people’ address him as ‘Lord Captain’. There were some class-conscious workers in the village who helped us prepare a [monthly] village meeting. … Everyone was there, including the resettled refugees. Some agricultural workers nervously tried to give the impression that they had just come across the meeting by chance. They held buckets of water or milk cans in their hands and stood at the back. This was no doubt because of [their fear of] ‘Lord Captain’, who kept tabs on everything in the village. …Opinions went back and forth for a while until, finally, everyone agreed to divide up the estate. A land commission was formed and was to be headed by an agricultural worker. But now the landowner had to be officially informed of the meeting’s decision. When we went to see him, he was already shouting and swearing at us. I told him briefly about the decision and asked him to hand over the keys and all estate documents to the land commission immediately, to stay in his room until further notice and to refrain from any interference. He initially tried to intimidate us, declaring that the meeting did not have the authority and that he would not recognise oral decrees at all. In response to the question of authority, I asked him whether he thought this was a question of political power. For in this regard, he was too late: it had already been decided in favour of the working people. He and his peers had been outplayed. We kept him under constant watch until the estate was divided up, which happened soon afterwards, and prevented him from sending communications. 27 Torisdorf was among the 3.3 million hectares of land – around one-third of the agricultural and forestry land – and 14,000 buildings that were expropriated without compensation in the Soviet Occupation Zone as part of the land reform. Of the 3.3 million hectares of expropriated land, 76% consisted of 7,160 private estates (farms with more than 100 hectares of arable land); 4% of 4,537 farms owned by active Nazis and war criminals with less than 100 hectares of land; and the remaining 20% of state property and Nazi party property. 28 Two-thirds of this land (2.2 million hectares) was transferred to a central land fund, which was then distributed to individual applicants as identified and listed by the land reform commissions. The expropriated land was distributed to small farmers, agricultural workers, peasants, and refugees as privately owned plots and could be passed down within a family on the condition that the recipient family members use the land. It could not be sold, leased, or mortgaged. Forty-three percent of the expropriated land was distributed to agricultural workers and landless peasants, while 35% went to refugees. 29 As a result of the land reform, the share of agricultural land cultivated by smallholder farmers doubled. Various measures were implemented to improve the quality of life of the new farmers and support nascent businesses. These measures included flexible delivery quotas, loans, and the provision of seed and livestock. Efforts were made to promote mutual cooperation and assistance during harvest times, as well as a special construction programme initiated by the SMA which led to the building of approximately 95,000 houses, 104,300 stables, and 38,470 barns by 1953. 30 Due to the shortage of labour, agricultural machinery, and draft animals, it was important and necessary to ensure cooperation between the peasants and the local authorities, organisations, and businesses. For example, the urban population was mobilised to help new farmers harvest their yields. The remaining expropriated land (1.1 million hectares) became state property, resulting in the creation of 500 state-owned estates. 31 Initially, these estates served as supply farms for the Red Army as part of the post-war reparations to the Soviet Union. Later, they were converted into Publicly Owned Estates (Volkseigene Güter, VEG), which became important mechanisms to support other agricultural enterprises, both publicly and privately owned, such as farms for seed and animal breeding, training and education, and experimental and research purposes. They also supported economically struggling farmers in an advisory capacity and played a pioneering role in the introduction of new farming methods. Data from Klaus Schmidt, Landwirtschaft in der DDR [Agriculture in the DDR] (Clenze: AgriMedia, 2009), 16. * Beginning in the early twentieth century, non-profit companies bought large estates and divided them into plots for lease to sharecroppers. ** Individuals and organisations associated with the Nazi party. Data from Klaus Schmidt, Landwirtschaft in der DDR [Agriculture in the DDR] (Clenze: AgriMedia, 2009), 16. This means, for example, that in 1939 only 1.1% of all farms had more than 100 hectares of land. However, these few farms accounted for 28.3% of the total agricultural land. Land reform not only greatly increased the number of small and medium-sized farmers but also enormously increased their share of the total utilised agricultural land.
