Data from a Collective of authors, Früchte des Bündnisses – Werden und Wachsen der sozialistischen Landwirtschaft der DDR [Fruits of the Alliance – The Development and Growth of Socialist Agriculture in the DDR] (Berlin: Dietz Verlag, 1985), 33. In early 1946, East Germans went to the polls to elect local commissions for the Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association (Vereinigung der gegenseitigen Bauernhilfe, VdgB), thereby turning the organically grown peasant mutual aid committees into official administrative bodies. The Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association was tasked with establishing machine-lending stations (Maschinen-Ausleih-Stationen, MASs), where peasants could rent agricultural machinery and equipment, and with setting up businesses that processed agricultural products and built repair workshops and cattle rearing centres. By 1950, there were 32,621 state-owned and 617,886 privately owned agricultural farms of at least one hectare, the latter of which cultivated around 88% of all agricultural land in the DDR (5.7 million hectares). 32 In 1948, 40,000 farms were run independently by women farmers, a result both of land reform and of the significant number of soldiers who died during the war. 33 With an average size of 9.3 hectares, privately owned farms were divided as follows: Data from Klaus Schmidt, Landwirtschaft in der DDR [Agriculture in the DDR] (Clenze: AgriMedia, 2009), 131. At the time, roughly two of the eight million people employed in the DDR worked in agriculture. A quarter were agricultural workers (workers with contracts); another quarter were farmers (self-employed owner or tenant of a farm), and half consisted virtually entirely of family workers (the family of the owner or tenant working without contract, social security, or income tax), which were almost exclusively women. 34 Of the approximately 500,000 farmers, 210,000 were new farmers. 35 In sum, the implementation of land reform in East Germany marked a historic turning point in the democratisation of agrarian relations and radically improved the living conditions of the rural population. With the economic and political power of the landed nobility finally broken, peasants and agricultural workers increasingly developed into self-confident and socially influential classes. They began to actively shape society through new democratic structures such as the Land Reform Commission, the Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association, and the Democratic Farmers’ Party of Germany 36 Cooperation and participation in decision-making processes became central features of peasants’ lives, bringing the question of power to the fore in the countryside. This was a new beginning, uncharted territory for a peasantry that had known only centuries of feudal oppression and then twelve years of fascist warmongering. Image 6. A celebration of the 100th tractor built in the Brandenburg tractor factory in October 1949. The sign (right) reads: ‘Fighting for peace is fighting for Germany’s future’; the mural (left) reads: ‘Peace nourishes – war destroys’. 4. Building Cooperatives: ‘From Me to We’ In Germany, cooperatives and cooperative farmers’ associations existed prior to the DDR in the form of agricultural enterprises that offered bank loans and organised purchases and sales of agricultural products (seed, fertiliser, etc.). However, since Junkers and large-scale farmers held leading positions in the associations, they were able to use them to strengthen their dominance over small and medium-sized farms. By 1950, because of the land reform programme, these organisational units had been restructured and integrated into the Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association as the Farmers’ Trade Cooperative (Bäuerliche Handelsgenossenschaft), replacing a logic of subjugation with one of solidarity. On the production side, the first Agricultural Production Cooperative (LPG) was founded in 1952. From the outset, LPGs were conceived not merely as institutions for the collective production of agricultural goods, but as broader social organisations tasked with economic, social, and cultural functions. The socialist character of cooperative production established itself through the LPG’s stated purpose, operations, and, above all, its integration within the DDR’s political and economic structures. Yet establishing this socialist character and continuing the fundamental transformation in the countryside proved to be a complex process. For the DDR’s agricultural policies to meet the material needs of the population, especially in a context facing post-war ruin and an economy short of labour power, the question of scale and efficiency were key. Bruno Kiesler, head of the SED Central Committee’s Agriculture Department, explained how ‘democratic land reform was a strategically correct decision, though its initiators were aware that large-scale production was ultimately superior to a fragmented, small-scale economy in agriculture’. In order to ‘take the rural masses along this path’, he continued, rather than imposing the idea of large-scale production on them from above, the peasants ‘first had to find their own footing and gain trust in the political leadership. Through their daily working of the land, they gradually came to recognise that communal, cooperative work was more effective’. 37 The economic pressure to overcome small-scale agricultural production increased at the beginning of the 1950s with the heightened demand for agricultural products, both as raw materials for industry and as foodstuffs for the population as post-war scarcity gave way to peacetime demands. At the same time, there were signs of stagnation in agricultural production and labour productivity, and the difficult economic situation led to farm closures, especially among new farmers. 38 Furthermore, parcelled agricultural production on small private farms hindered the rational distribution of labour and made it difficult to efficiently use agricultural machinery, which was already scarce. Manual labour remained predominant. In 1950, the 514 machine-tractor stations (Maschinen-Traktoren-Station, MTSs) in the DDR had just 10,834 tractors and 675 trucks for the entire country. 39 By 1952, before the cooperative movement began, some 30,000 new farmers had abandoned their farms. 40 While the MAS – the precursor of the MTS – serviced individual farmers and offered small equipment, the MTS sought both to extend beyond individual equipment rentals, providing large agricultural machinery and organising its deployment in the most efficient way across more and larger LPGs. Image 7. Courtyard of an MTS in Obhausen, Saxony-Anhalt, DDR (1950). The socialist historian Kurt Gossweiler described the increasing necessity to make a strategic decision about the political system under which to develop the agricultural sector: Faced with the urgent need to increase the agricultural yield, the DDR’s leadership had to decide how to open the way to large-scale agriculture: either along capitalist or socialist lines. For this reason, the discussion about the construction of socialism in the DDR was primarily a discussion about whether the formation of agricultural production cooperatives would be approved. 41 A policy oriented toward cooperative agricultural production would have a profound impact on labour and property relations in rural life. It was a far-reaching decision that could not be resolved until the national question in Germany had been settled. In 1949, when the DDR was founded as an anti-fascist, democratic state in reaction to the founding of the Federal Republic of Germany (Bundesrepublik Deutschland, BRD), the Soviet Union, as well as the communists in West and East Germany, continued to pursue the strategic goal of a unified, non-aligned German republic in which the Four Ds of the 1945 Potsdam Agreement would be implemented. 42 Ultimately, the SED and the political leadership of the Soviet Union recognised the necessity to shift strategies. At its 1952 party congress, the SED decided to begin the construction of socialism through the formation of LPGs. Walter Ulbricht, general secretary of the SED, stressed the need for farmers to join cooperatives on a voluntary basis, emphasising ‘the principle of absolute voluntariness in the organisation of such cooperatives and to point out the inadmissibility of applying any kind of coercion towards the peasants in this regard’. Furthermore, Ulbricht continued, ‘Comrade Lenin himself had stressed that communal agriculture cannot be introduced by decrees and laws’. 43 Image 8. An SED agitation poster from the 1950s that reads: ‘The future belongs to socialism. Individual farmers, join the LPG’. Building Socialism One LPG at a Time Though the need to develop cooperatives and the structures for large-scale agricultural production was clear, in many respects the subjective and objective conditions to carry out such a project had not yet developed. How would the DDR bring about a shift in peasants’ way of thinking, working, and living? In some cases, it was peasants and agricultural workers who pushed for a form of collective production. Such peasants had in fact urged the SED to support the founding of cooperatives even before the party’s decision to do so in July 1952, and this approach is what led to the creation of the first LPG in Thuringia in the same year. They were in favour of LPGs because they either saw them as a necessary response to the difficult economic situation on their own land or because they knew of cooperative agriculture in the Soviet Union and were convinced of the need for socialism. Image 9. In January 1953, nine farmers founded an agricultural production cooperative in the municipality of Blochwitz (Grossenhain district). A farmer named Godzina signs a document to register herself as a member of the cooperative. Yet, for the vast majority of peasants – especially among long-established medium and large-scale farmers – the thought of giving up individual farming was still inconceivable in 1952. Many were dismissive, while others took a wait-and-see approach. An unprecedented reorientation was necessary to promote a more collective way of thinking and working, which came to be known by the slogan Vom Ich, zum Wir (‘From Me to We’). SED agriculture secretary Gerhard Grüneberg summarised some of the major difficulties in this process: Cooperative work makes it necessary to become a member of a collective. Previously, the peasant alone decided how to organise his work. Now he 44 receives instructions or, if he becomes head of a brigade, for example, he must organise the work for many people… [and] learn to overcome certain prejudices and respect other cooperative members as his equals. 45 In the initial phase, new and smallholder farmers who managed economically weak farms of all sizes were the main driving force behind the cooperative movement. 46 Large-scale farmers were excluded from LPG membership until 1955 in order to ensure that those peasants with closer links to the working class played the decisive role in the cooperatives from the outset. 47 Generally, smallholder farmers, agricultural workers, and new farmers were easily recruited to join LPGs because they faced the greatest economic difficulties and were less attached to their land. Image 10. On 19 May 1954, the Freie Erde (Free Soil) LPG in Magdeburg-Salbke builds a large farmyard. The cooperative’s farmers laid the foundation stone together with workers from neighbouring industrial enterprises. The sign reads: ‘How we work today is how we will live tomorrow!’ Image 11. The LPG construction brigade builds a feed house on 19 October 1955. Between April and October of that year, the construction brigade completed three new stables. By 1960, the brigade had built farrowing pens, and combines for pigs, cattle, and poultry. All these major construction projects were completed with the help of volunteer construction workers. The nascent LPGs faced unfavourable conditions of production in the early years, such as degraded soil, no experience in organising large-scale collective production, and a shortage of labour and lack of available barns, stables, and other farm buildings. The process was made even more difficult by the fact that farmers often joined the cooperatives one by one. Integrating new members and new areas of production were a constant challenge. Crop rotations and the organisation of production had to be adjusted frequently. On average, it took five to ten years to establish solid structures and continuous operating procedures in LPGs. 48 Despite these and other challenges, by 1952 the first cooperatives were already farming an average of 114 hectares of agricultural land. 49 This meant that the material prerequisites for large-scale production – such as the new agricultural machinery and technology and funds needed to construct farm buildings – had to be secured as quickly as possible. Even though the manufacturing of agricultural machinery accelerated in 1952, there was still a lack of sufficient technical equipment and training for cooperative farmers. All of this put further strains on the national economy. In order to facilitate a gradual and accessible transition to cooperatives, the state introduced active development incentives and subsidised newly established LPGs through measures such as the reduction of tax arrears and outstanding debts, the provision of free technical expertise, the priority allocation of quality seeds and mineral fertilisers, and the provision of loans on favourable terms. 50 For the same reason, three different types of LPGs were created, which introduced a gradual approach away from the private property model of the previous system to the collective approach of LPGs. From type I to III, each stage introduced a different extent of socialising the means of production, requiring participation in communal work, and regulating income distribution: In Type I LPGs, arable land, machinery, equipment, and draught animals became collective property while productive and breeding livestock remained the property of the farmers. In Type II LPGs, tractors, draught animals, machinery, and equipment were also transferred to the LPG. Type III LPGs operated as fully collective and independent enterprises. Here, except for private household farming, the farmers’ entire homestead (arable land, grassland, woodland, livestock, tractors, machinery, equipment, farm buildings) was transferred to the cooperative. In all three types, the land that peasants brought into the LPG remained their own property rather than state property and was taken into account when wages were distributed. 51 The higher the tier, the less the land contributed by the farmers was taken into account for remuneration. In 1949, the machine-lending stations, which had been organised by the Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association to share existing machines between farmers, were transformed into state-owned machine-tractor stations (MTSs). This change reflected the growing supply of tractors and agricultural machinery. The MTSs played a leading role in forming and stabilising cooperatives: while larger technical equipment, trucks, and machine systems in the MTSs could be borrowed according to a tariff system that favoured small over large-scale farmers and cooperatives over individual farms. Furthermore, since the technical base of the MTSs and their advisory service on crop production and soil conditions were continually expanded by the state, they effectively determined the direction of development towards large-scale industrial-style agricultural production. In addition to meeting the economic needs of the peasantry, the MTSs played a central role in the political and cultural development in the villages. Cultural annex buildings were attached to the MTSs, where further training for farmers, agrochemical and zootechnical consultations, film screenings, theatre trips, music and dance groups, libraries and sports clubs, and much more were organised by the MTSs. These cultural houses became an essential hub for agricultural development, social and cultural interaction, and democratisation of society. Furthermore, MTSs served as support hubs and cooperative recruitment bases for the working class in rural areas, connecting mechanics, tractor drivers, and other workers with peasants who were involved in cooperatives. Image 12. Villagers browsing through the library in the community of Krien, in Mecklenburg-Vorpommern, DDR. Integrating Cooperatives into the Planned Economy Though the shift toward cooperative agriculture was initiated with the founding of the first LPG in 1952, the first LPG law was only passed in 1959. Incorporating the experiences of the past seven years and the aspirations of those yet to come, this law and later versions included a comprehensive definition of the social role and task of cooperatives. The 1982 version of the law stated the following: §1 Principles (1) Agricultural production cooperatives (hereinafter referred to as LPGs) are voluntary associations of farmers, gardeners, and other citizens that promote joint socialist production to meet the material, cultural, and food supply needs of the population, as well as provide industries with raw materials. LPGs ensure a significant increase in output and high efficiency in agricultural production by constantly improving the ratio of input to output. (2) In LPGs, cooperative farmers organise their joint work and social relations according to the principles of cooperative democracy and socialist business management, such as equality, comradely cooperation, and mutual aid. Farmers jointly decide over cooperative property and share in the economic gains of their LPG according to cooperative principles of distribution. 52 Six years into the transition to socialist agriculture, the SED’s Fifth Party Congress in 1958 set the goal of achieving self-sufficiency in agricultural production as well as adequate access to raw materials for the agricultural industry by 1962. Apart from products that could not be grown in the DDR due to climate and environmental conditions (e.g. tropical fruits, coffee, cocoa, etc.), food sovereignty was thereafter largely achieved. 53 In order to plan and develop the national economy, cooperative agricultural production had to be integrated into the DDR’s centrally planned economic system. The primary instrument for planning agricultural production was the Plan of State Production Volume of Agricultural Products, which was worked out through a complex process of assessments and discussions. First, the demand for agricultural goods and products was determined at a central level by the State Planning Commission, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, and the Central State Office for the Registration and Purchase of Agricultural Products. This included priorities for the development of production as well as cultural and social progress. The objectives laid out in this draft plan were then discussed at the district and municipal levels as well as in the individual LPGs and the other socialist agricultural enterprises before being presented again at the central level and then adopted by the People’s Chamber of the DDR. Only then, based on the annual plan, did individual LPGs draw up their final operating plans, working closely with the municipal councils. The plan’s implementation was then monitored by the municipality’s council or mayor. Each task in the LPGs was assigned a value on a scale of 0.8 to 1.6 work units, a conversion key that served to classify these according to their importance, difficulty, and the qualifications required to complete them. Complicated work was remunerated with more units than simpler work, with members completing a range of tasks in a given period, each assigned a specific work-unit value. A work unit corresponded to a monetary value which was calculated from the total sum of the generated funds available for distribution to the members and the number of work units performed in the LPGs. A minimum value of seven marks (the official currency of the DDR) per work unit was guaranteed by the state. Since the exact value of earned work units could only be determined at the end of the year, a system was used in which approximately 70% of the planned value of work units was paid in advance. 54 Solveig Leo, a long-standing chairwoman of an LPG, recalled the process of assigning value to work units and the debates that arose: In the evening, they [the LPG farmers] usually assessed the day’s performance and recorded the work units on a blackboard in the LPG: 1.1 or 1.2 work units or so per day for each name. This meant that every cooperative farmer was able to see how many work units his colleague had been awarded. And, of course, there were debates. For instance, if someone disagreed and thought they had worked much more diligently than what the calculation showed, they asked: ‘Why do I have only 1.1 and he has 1.3?’. Then the chief brigadier had to explain why. 55 Image 13. LPG bookkeeping was a difficult and often unpopular but necessary task. An agronomist from the Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association (left) trains a farmer (right) who took on this work for an LPG in Neubredersdorf, DDR, while some of the other members check the calculations (October 1954). When an LPG laid out its production plan, it allocated it to various funds in coordination with the municipal council. These funds were divided into a consumption reserve and an investment reserve with various distinctions, such as the essential fund (which went towards farm buildings, machinery, equipment with a long service life, membership initiation fees from cooperative members, etc.); working capital; investment; remuneration; cultural; and social expenditure. As was the case in other sectors in the DDR, in order to promote production, competitions were organised within and among LPGs alongside annual plans and everyday brigade work. The aim was to mobilise additional resources in labour time, draw public attention to the importance of agriculture, and make villagers aware of the collective results of all the work in the agricultural sector, including the work of the LPGs. Author Thomas Kupfermann describes how much public attention these competitions attracted: The mechanised harvesting of crops on a large scale… was known as the ‘battle of the harvest’. The hard-working men – and women – who drove their combine harvesters across the large LPG fields and took their machines to the next job on the roads… were the ‘harvest captains’. Every year, the harvest battle raged, and the media accompanied it with headlines and success stories… The cooperatives participated in district competitions, which were also reported on, and at the end of the harvest battle there were awards for the winners and their pictures were featured in the ‘Streets of the Best’ [blackboards where the best workers were displayed]. 56 Image 14. Colleagues from the Institute for Agroeconomics at the German Academy of Agricultural Sciences in Berlin support the senior accountant of the Schönemark LPG, Anne-Margret Mielke, as she prepares the monthly cash audit. Image 15. Thirty-two female farmers from the Vorwärts LPG in Groß Beuchow, DDR, harvested 100 tonnes of potatoes, which were immediately taken to nearby shops (September 1961). Image 16. 1961, the Friendship Milking Brigade of an LPG in Neßdorf produced more than 80,000 kilograms of milk, exceeding the target production plan for that year. Image 17. Members of the Priborn LPG discuss high crop yields and the efficient use of technology at their annual general assembly (January 1973). Class Struggle and Policy Disputes The progress of the cooperative movement in the DDR was shaped both by internal conditions and by the political conditions across Europe in the 1950s. In the DDR and Eastern Europe, the development of socialism was still in its infancy. At the same time, the Cold War waged by the US and its Western allies against the socialist camp intensified. There were many attempts to exploit uncertainties and contradictions in socialist countries and their political leadership, especially following the death of Joseph Stalin in March 1953. The violent political crises and anti-government protests in the DDR in 1953, Poland in 1956, and Hungary that same year can be understood in this context. As late as March 1952, Heinrich von Brentano, then chairman of the CDU/CSU parliamentary group in West Germany’s Bundestag, openly stated his intentions to regain control of East Germany : ‘We will do anything and everything – and I say explicitly: anything and everything – to get the Soviet Occupation Zone back’. 57 In particular, the open border between West and East Berlin offered the West a wide range of opportunities for disruptive actions, including espionage, sabotage, and establishing terrorist organisations. For instance, the West Berlin radio station, Rundfunk im amerikanischen Sektor (Broadcasting in the American Sector), used manipulative, negative reporting to increase farmers’ doubts about the prospects of the socialist perspective. 58 The West exploited existing challenges and discontent in the DDR, which mainly stemmed from the population’s struggle to overcome the consequences of the war, and weaponised them against its government. Seeing how tense the political situation was becoming at the beginning of June 1953, the DDR’s leadership implemented comprehensive relief measures for the population. Nevertheless, protests broke out weeks later on 17 June 1953, which were incited into a counter-revolutionary coup attempt with the help of the West. In the aftermath of the unrest, the SED leadership reflected critically and publicly on its policy mistakes, including in regard to the cooperative movement that had begun one year earlier. In a newspaper article, the SED Central Committee wrote: The party, which had taken the right course towards building the foundations of socialism in the DDR, took the wrong path of accelerating the solution of this task without taking into account the real internal and external conditions. This… had detrimental consequences for the population’s food supply and to a certain extent led to the disruption of proper relations between the party and the working masses. Administrative methods were employed where broad and diligent educational work amongst the masses should have taken place. It was right for the party organisations to support the movement to form agricultural production cooperatives that emerged in the DDR following the initiative of working farmers. However, in some circles, party organisations tolerated the violation of the strict principle of voluntariness and tried to increase the number of cooperatives without devoting the necessary attention to the organisational and economic consolidation of the existing cooperatives, which is the main task of the party when advancing the development of cooperatives in the villages. 59 Eventually, the effort required to establish new LPGs made further expansion difficult, leading the state to shift toward consolidating existing LPGs, supporting their efforts to raise production capacity, and integrating additional farmers, while also providing greater support to private farms whose productivity was still essential to meeting overall agricultural targets. During this period, there was a renewed debate about the future of agriculture in the DDR, which largely revolved around the extent to which the state should continue to promote cooperatives. In the mid-1950s, most cooperatives were still less productive than medium and privately owned large-scale farms and had to be supported by state subsidies. Some politicians and analysts used this as an opportunity to campaign against state support for ‘unproductive’ cooperatives and for strengthening privately owned farms. These proposals were sharply rejected by the SED leadership under Walter Ulbricht, who declared in 1957 at a conference of the Central Committee: It is being said that the orderly development of agriculture and the systematic promotion of socialist agriculture should be abandoned… Priority, they say, should be given to the development of [private] family farms… This proposal paves the path back to a capitalist economy, whereby the large farmers would become the main economic force in the village. 60 These disputes reveal that, throughout the entire period of the cooperative movement, there were deep conflicts and debates about how to balance the relationship between voluntariness and the active and determined promotion of cooperatives by the state. Administrative exaggerations by government agencies and political leaders that violated the principle of voluntariness were criticised and opposed. Yet so too were ideas of a self-sustaining process that assigned the state a passive role towards the farmers. There was also an active struggle against the forces that set out to sabotage the development of socialist agriculture. Socialist Springtime: The Height of the Cooperative Movement By 1958, six years after the first LPG was founded, there had been a gradual consolidation and stabilisation of productive capacity within the cooperative movement, though state support remained necessary in most cases. Yet, as long as private farms existed alongside the cooperative sector, the socialist transformation of the countryside remained incomplete. The coexistence of different socioeconomic structures in agriculture – cooperative production and individual farming – was bound to hold back the growth of productivity. At the same time, the mere existence of private enterprises in the countryside gave political opponents ammunition to argue against the project of socialist agriculture as a whole. Pressure increased to bring private enterprises into the cooperative movement and create a unified system. From 1958 to 1960, commonly referred to as ‘socialist springtime’, almost all remaining private farmers were brought into LPGs through initiatives led by bloc parties, 61 mass organisations, the Democratic Farmers’ Party of Germany, the Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association, and MTSs. This consolidation was reinforced by economic measures that strengthened the sector’s technical capacity, such a marked increase in the agricultural machinery available. As the LPGs’ productivity visibly rose and state support became more effective, medium- and large-scale farmers who had long held reservations began to By the time the campaign was declared complete in the spring of 1960, its scale was visible in the statistics: the predominantly small, privately owned farms that had characterised agriculture in 1950 had given way to over 19,000 LPGs, each cultivating an average of around 280 hectares. Eighty-four percent of all agricultural land was now farmed cooperatively. 62 Image 18. Industrial workers help with the potato harvest. Cooperatives were assigned industrial enterprises at an early stage to provide support and workers helped with the harvest and repairs. Women farmers played a key role in the expansion and consolidation of the cooperative movement, as they were often the key actors winning their families over. Women, who had traditionally worked in the time-consuming and physically taxing field of animal husbandry while also managing their households and childcare, certainly stood to gain the most from joining LPGs and the social benefits granted to their members. Though the vast majority of farmers had agreed to join LPGs by the spring of 1960, in some cases, they only came to truly appreciate the benefits of cooperative production in the years that followed. One farmer, Elli I., reflected on her scepticism towards the cooperative in the early years: As individual farmers, we had to work from dawn to dusk. In 1957, my daughter was born prematurely because I had to help my husband load thirty sacks of fertiliser. In the cooperative, we no longer had to worry about such things. We had joined the cooperative quite late and, after years, I thought to myself, my goodness, how stupid had we been? You can’t live any better than here. The work got easier. We had medical care, every woman could go to the doctor, we had housekeeping days, and vacations. When I think back, … those were my best years. 63 The socialist agricultural sector includes publicly owned estates and cooperatives. Other small state-owned agricultural enterprises are not shown separately, which accounts for the difference between the total share of socialist farms and the combined shares of publicly owned estates and cooperatives. Data from State Central Administration for Statistics, Statistisches Jahrbuch der DDR 1960/61 [Statistical Yearbook of the DDR 1960/61], VEB Deutscher Zentralverlag, 1962, 419. Data from Gerhard Seidel, Die Landwirtschaft der DDR [Agriculture in the DDR] (Berlin: VEB Deutscher Landwirtschaftsverlag, 1961), 37; Collective of authors under the direction of Volker Klemm, Von den bürgerlichen Agrarreformen zur sozialistischen Landwirtschaft in der DDR [From Bourgeois Agrarian Reforms to Socialist Agriculture in the DDR] (Berlin: VEB Deutscher Landwirtschaftsverlag, 1985), 220; State Central Administration for Statistics, Statistisches Jahrbuch der DDR 1989 [Statistical Yearbook of the DDR 1989] (Berlin: VEB Deutscher Zentralverlag, 1990), 196. Data from State Central Administration for Statistics, Statistisches Jahrbuch der DDR 1971 [Statistical Yearbook of the DDR 1971] (Berlin: VEB Deutscher Zentralverlag, 1971), 222; State Central Administration for Statistics, Statistisches Jahrbuch der DDR 1955 [Statistical Yearbook of the DDR 1955] (Berlin: VEB Deutscher Zentralverlag, 1956), 224. Cooperatives Drive Social and Cultural Progress From their origin, the tasks of LPGs were not limited to agricultural production but to the improvement of rural life in general. Over time, they developed into hubs of social, communal, cultural-political, and economic development in rural communities. In coordination with the municipalities and urban enterprises, LPGs built apartments, cultural centres, kindergartens, and sports facilities. They improved roads and constructed new ones, installed water pipes and sewage systems, dug drainage ditches, and opened stores and restaurants. LPGs built on and expanded the cultural work of MTSs. Performances by theatre groups, dance nights for the youth, sporting events, and film screenings brought a new level of cultural life to the villages. Hans Luft, an agronomist and former employee of the DDR’s Ministry of Agriculture, reflects on the cultural transformation brought about by the new production relations of the LPG system: The joint work in the cooperative and [resolving] the emerging problems such as the use of complex machinery to bring in the harvest as quickly as possible, difficulties with repairs and procuring spare parts for agricultural machinery, completion of construction and irrigation systems, [and] epidemic-free management of large livestock quickly broke down generations of enmity in the villages. The farmers’ children grew into the new forms of production with new knowledge, experience, and, above all, higher levels of qualification. 64 Cooperatives vastly improved the rural population’s living conditions, reducing the physical strain on farmers through communal work and the use of machinery. 65 In addition to national programmes that guaranteed access to national social, health, and insurance systems and so on, the LPGs achieved a number of important social advances for their members, which were impossible for previous generations of farmers to even dream of and still unavailable to the few remaining private farms, such as: Equal pay for equal work Special remuneration for work on Sundays and public holidays Sick pay Child benefits, such as monthly cash payments and birth grants Paid vacation, with the option of spending it in a subsidised LPG-owned vacation site Fixed and predictable work hours, except during the harvest months The inclusion of all cooperative farmers in a uniform social insurance system that covered benefits such as healthcare, pensions, and accident insurance ‘Exemption’ from income tax contributions 66 In addition, child labour, which had been a common practice on family-run farms, disappeared with the advent of LPGs. Central schools replaced the single-class schools in the villages. Children and young people had access to the DDR’s polytechnic education system, which enabled them to choose an independent career and provided a foundation for agricultural studies. Comprehensive education and training opportunities were created for cooperative farmers, from which female farmers benefited significantly as they were able to gain professional qualifications and take on managerial roles in the cooperative farms for the first time. Margarete Müller, an agronomist and SED politician, described the progress that her village achieved: I have lived in Kotelow since 1960. Back then, at the age of 29, I was elected LPG chairwoman. When we started, there were no paved roads and our boots got stuck deep in the mud. Bit by bit, we modernised the village and improved it. We were happy about every new house, every new stable, every new barn, about the dairy farm and the pigsties that we built together. We celebrated together and also got frustrated together when things didn’t go the way we wanted them to. We were a community. Even in the summer, farmers could go on holiday, and youth who qualified at universities and technical colleges sometimes moved back because they saw a future for themselves and their families in little Kotelow. 67 From 1960, the height of the cooperative movement, until the dissolution of the DDR in 1990, women accounted for roughly 40–45% of LPG members. 68 While only 8.5% of women working in agriculture had a degree or vocational training in 1963, compared to 18.1% of men, by 1984 this figure had reached 88.6% in the field of agriculture, compared to 90.2% of the general population. 69 In 1966, 26% of all board members of the LPGs were women. 70 Concrete structures such as the Women’s Committees and Women’s Development Plans were set up comprehensively in the LPGs and intended to strengthen the role of women, advocate for their interests, and increase their leadership positions. By 1978, the proportion of women in leadership positions had reached 35%, in part due to the increase in their professional and educational qualifications. However, the process of increasing the number of women in leadership stagnated in the late 1980s and women remained underrepresented among LPG chairs until the end of the DDR. 71 Image 19. Two teachers and an educational assistant look after forty-seven children in the kindergarten of the LPG Walter Ulbricht in Jahna, founded in 1952. Image 20. The Peasants’ Mutual Aid Association Women Farmers’ Commission in the Stollberg district followed the resolutions of the Farmers’ Congress to support women farmers and girls in their continuing education by offering special training courses. For fifteen weeks, forty-eight women and girls came to the district town for further training, including lectures, seminars, practical exercises, and field trips. The focus was on cattle breeding and husbandry because most of the participants worked in this field (February 1962). Image 21. Anneliese Hartmann, a mechanisation expert from the V. I. Lenin LPG in Öttersdorf, operates a swather over green fodder areas in June 1978. Anti-Imperialist Solidarity in Agriculture As the DDR sought to bring the quality of life in rural and urban areas closer together, another pillar of socialist construction was cultivated among farmers: international solidarity. On 1 September 1960, the Institute for Tropical Agriculture was founded at the Karl Marx University in Leipzig. Why was such an institute established in Germany, a country situated in a cool temperate climate zone? After losing its colonies at the end of the First World War, there was no longer any state interest in research and education relating to tropical agriculture. This changed with the DDR, albeit with an altogether different premise: young nation states that had emerged from anti-colonial struggles were to be actively supported on their path to economic liberation. Thousands of students from over sixty countries in the Global South were trained to develop agriculture in their home countries at the Leipzig Institute and a handful of other educational establishments, such as the School of Agricultural Engineering for Tropical Agriculture in Altenburg and the VEB Interagrarkooperation. DDR citizens also attended courses and seminars designed specifically to prepare them for agricultural assignments abroad. Groups of DDR specialists, working in close collaboration with local experts and officials, supported the complex and protracted process of transforming feudal, colonially distorted, and backward agricultural structures into sovereign, state-controlled agricultural production. Examples include pig specialists in Vietnam attempting to combine the fertility of the Vietnamese pot-bellied pig with the fattening capacity of German breeds, a tropical farmer in the People’s Democratic Republic of Yemen who, together with local officials, carried out the first thorough statistical surveys of livestock production, or a group of thirty-eight DDR specialists who, on the basis of a friendship treaty between the DDR and Mozambique and in close cooperation with local forces, helped to establish a state farm – eight of whom were killed by counter-revolutionary forces in 1984. 72 Furthermore, the development of the first coffee-growing regions in Dak Lak, Vietnam – supported by 32 million roubles 73 – enabled the country to become the world’s second-largest coffee producer. 74 The dispatch of tractors and agricultural machinery, as well as the setting up of repair workshops, was financed by donations from DDR citizens, including contributions from farmers. In thousands of cooperatives, farmers in the DDR set up solidarity groups and supported campaigns such as ‘Farmers Help the Farmers of Vietnam’, which sent spraying equipment to Vietnam to rehabilitate crops poisoned by the US chemical attacks. Image 22. Members of the Oskar Schneller LPG in Mansfeld express their solidarity and friendship with the Vietnamese people in the presence of Bui Tru, the second secretary of the embassy of the Democratic Republic of Vietnam in the DDR (July 1972). The Path to Large-Scale Agricultural Production In the late 1960s, when land reform had been fully implemented and the cooperative movement had reached its height, a third central phase of agricultural development began. During this phase, strategic goals were established that would guide agricultural planning until the beginning of the 1980s: the consolidation of small- and mid-sized farms into larger cooperatives, specialisation in parts of the production process, and collaboration with processing industries as prerequisites for large-scale industrial-like production in the countryside. Hans Reichelt, long-time minister of agriculture, explained the necessity of industrial agricultural production under the conditions of a socialist society: This was the path towards creating larger economic units in agriculture – small-scale farming is as ineffective as keeping livestock in small stables. Some may object here: ‘Yikes, large-scale livestock farming!’ But industrial-like production in agriculture is not bad per se . It is objectively necessary to feed humanity – this is not possible with nineteenth-century production methods. What is bad is when all moral and ethical rules, including animal welfare, are ignored for the sake of profit. However, this stems from the character of the society. When corporate wholesale buyers force farmers to sell their produce below the production price, and when shareholders and banks speculate with food on the stock exchanges, this inevitably has an impact on production. 75 As a result of the increased investment capacities and efficiency that came with years of accumulated experience in cooperative-style production, all LPGs gradually developed into Type III LPGs (the most advanced cooperative structure, through which farmers incorporated all of their land and homestead into the cooperative, which functioned as an independent enterprise). Through a 1963 decree of the Council of Ministers, most MTS machinery was handed over to the Type III LPGs free of charge while the Type I and Type II LPGs were able to purchase it at a low price and in instalments. 76 Many MTS workers, tractor drivers, and technicians became members of cooperatives. This meant that the use of technology could be coordinated more effectively and integrated into the work of LPGs. Finally, LPGs also merged to form larger ‘joint LPGs’. The total number of cooperatives decreased, while the agricultural area farmed by each cooperative increased. Where previously there were 19,313 LPGs working roughly 5.4 million hectares of land in 1960, in 1988 3,855 LPGs worked the same amount of land. 77 Data from a collective of authors, 40 Jahre erfolgreiche Entwicklung zu einer leistungsfähigen sozialistischen Landwirtschaft in der DDR [40 Years of Successful Development Towards Efficient Socialist Agriculture in the DDR] (Eggersdorf: agrabuch, 1989), 58. This increase in agricultural productivity was driven by the need to meet the growing demand for food and raw materials, which were necessary inputs in a number of industries, such as the textile, leather and fur, pulp and paper, chemical and pharmaceutical, woodworking, and cultural industries. In 1988, almost 65% of the DDR’s primary raw materials were produced in the agricultural sector. 78 This increase in agricultural productivity also freed up urgently needed labour that could be deployed to other sectors of the economy; promoted the efficient use of agricultural machinery; and created the conditions to increase the level of training that farmers and agricultural workers received – all factors that in turn further increased productivity. Image 23. Specialists Klaus Ulrich and Franz Mally check a computer-controlled irrigation system in an LPG in Dambeck. Image 24. Workers use a combine harvester in an LPG in Werben. Agricultural planning took place at the central, local, and farm levels. Until then, LPGs had been relatively self-contained, now they become increasingly integrated into the overall social planning and management of the DDR’s economy. The Agricultural-Industrial Associations (Agrar-Industrie-Vereinigungen, AIVs) that emerged in the mid-1970s were the culmination of this process. AIVs were an institutionalised form of cooperation between various LPGs, Publicly Owned Estates, inter-enterprise facilities used by more than one organisation also known by their German acronym VEGs, and the upstream and downstream enterprises in the service, processing, and manufacturing sectors. By 1989, thirteen such production complexes (AIVs) had formed in the DDR, which together farmed 6.6% of the DDR’s total agricultural land. 79 By the early 1980s, concrete problems arising from the trend towards specialisation and excesses in large-scale production led to course corrections and debates. For example, the specialisation and separation of plant and animal production disrupted the direct exchange of natural fertiliser and animal feed and led to logistical difficulties. Increased transport expenditures and costs for large estates strained production. In animal production, the effort to tend to veterinary hygiene and disease prevention noticeably increased. Ecological issues caused by the excessive use of mineral fertilisers rather than environmentally friendlier options were corrected over time. Energy-intensive machine systems were susceptible to energy price spikes on the global market, which had a particularly severe impact on the DDR, especially in the beginning of the 1980s. The effort to improve the integration of LPGs into the DDR’s social and economic plans through AIVs did not signal a plan to change the ownership structure of the land. As an official information brochure on agriculture explained: Time and again, visitors to our republic ask if and when land will be nationalised. This question is not on the table, because nobody is thinking of changing the forms of property in the countryside. Most of the land is the private property of the farmers. Its cooperative use, and the distribution of its yield to the LPG members according to their share of the work performed, creates every opportunity to further advance the [DDR’s] developed socialist society. 80 Though private ownership undoubtedly had an influence on the consciousness of individual farmers, on the whole, the increasing integration of the LPGs into the unitary structures of economic planning and democracy in the DDR prevented private land ownership from developing economic or political significance. The relationship between the different actors in rural areas and agricultural production was not without friction. As Klaus Schmidt, former director of the DDR’s Institute for Agroeconomics, pointed out: Particularly where the AIVs managed the territory of an entire municipality or where their sphere of influence even extended beyond the municipality’s boundaries, there was a tendency for them to increasingly take on the official duties that had hitherto been managed by state organs. A fundamental problem that arose with the development of the AIVs was the question of the prospects and opportunities of individual units within the AIV, specifically to what extent they could maintain their independence. 81 Such contradictions and problems, alongside the misguided separation of plant and animal production in the 1970s, should have resulted in lively debate about the future of agriculture. The issue of balancing land ownership and independence with the further integration and centralisation of LPGs would certainly have remained the subject of political debate had the DDR continued to exist. As long as the SED and the wide-ranging democratic structures in rural areas – from the agricultural councils from the municipal to central levels to the farmers’ organisations, and numerous scientific institutions and agriculture publications of the time – responded to these contradictions and tasks in an agile, self-critical and proactive manner, they were able to drive development forward. 5. Conclusion The development of socialist agricultural relations was brought to an abrupt end with the dissolution of the DDR in October 1990. Structural changes were imposed at breakneck speed, transforming the social and economic reality of farmers. At the time of the DDR’s dissolution, 55% of the land cultivated by the LPGs was owned by individual LPG members. The remaining 45% was owned by the state or leased to the LPG by private owners. After 1990, this 45% was rapidly privatised, removing land from the LPGs’ existing economic structures and providing a lever for their dissolution in favour of private farming. LPGs seeking to continue to produce had to lease or purchase this land, often at additional cost, while completing the transition to a new legal form used for cooperatives in the West by the end of 1991. The transformation process placed considerable pressure on existing LPG structures. 82 This and other policy shifts in the post-1990 period created a rural exodus, especially of young and qualified workers. Of the former 923,000 agricultural workers in the DDR, 743,900 were laid off by 1993 and 4 out of 5 were pushed to leave the countryside. 83 The social and cultural infrastructure was destroyed. Although the transformation in land ownership established by land reform was legally protected by the so-called reunification treaties signed by the last DDR government, the landowning elite that had controlled the countryside before 1945 was able to use loopholes to reclaim parts of their expropriated land. Yet, despite the radical capitalist turnaround, the history of the DDR has left a visible mark on agricultural structures in Germany. On average, farms in East Germany cultivate much larger areas than those in West Germany and have maintained elements of the structures established by LPGs, such as machine sharing under the new legal categories of the market economy. 84 In less than fifty years, revolutionary ruptures in the living and economic relations of the countryside were seen in East Germany, from the remnants of a semi-feudal, hierarchical social and property order to a collective way of working and living based on cooperation. The land reform that began in 1945 created the conditions for the transformation in the countryside and the dismantling of the political and economic monopoly of the landowning classes. Only by ending their rule was it possible to establish and develop new economic and social relations in East Germany. With the expropriation of the large Junker estates, land reform in the DDR ended the rule of the feudal aristocracy and agrarian capitalist classes. Agricultural workers, landless and smallholder peasants, and the general village population were liberated from the system of economic exploitation and semi-feudal disenfranchisement that had been in place for centuries. Under the conditions created by the reality of Soviet occupation after the Second World War and the leadership of the workers’ movement and parties, land reform was achieved in this part of Germany, and the oppressed became the protagonists shaping agriculture and village life. The phase of collectivisation in the post-war years (approximately 1952–1960) illustrates the complexity of social development when a change in the thinking of those involved is crucial to its success. The SED had to manoeuvre between the strategic goals of socialist construction and the adverse conditions on the ground. Between retreat and advance, external and internal contradictions, and the necessities and prospects of economic development, the SED constantly reassessed the speed and force of its leadership in this comprehensive development process. The complexity of the path to socialism, the protracted nature of some processes, especially in people’s consciousness, and the influence and sabotage of political opponents had to be taken into account and counteracted. This required agility and clarity of purpose, but also the ability to be self-critical, which the political leadership generally demonstrated over long periods of time. While social hardship and individual cases of injustice cannot be denied, it is necessary to understand such instances in the contradictory and complex context of post-war East Germany and to understand that they represent exceptions, not the rule. Without the leading role of the peasantry, the transition towards cooperatives would have failed. Naturally, from the bourgeois perspective, the transition away from the dominance of private property is to be condemned on principle. Narratives of ‘forced collectivisation’ that dominate the public discourse in Germany today do not seek to understand the processes in the DDR but rather to promote anti-communist prejudices. Individual fates, in which there were also tragic conflicts and entanglements, are used to obscure the view of the broader development and prevent a serious examination based on historical facts. This method of framing can be seen over and over in narratives about socialist states. Cooperative forms of agricultural production were a cornerstone of East Germany and a decisive building block for the development of the DDR as a socialist state. Existential financial angst, which is inevitable under the ‘freedom’ of private property and competition, was overcome in the DDR through mutual aid among peasants and their integration into state planning. While some see the continued existence of cooperative ownership alongside state ownership as a major obstacle to the deepening of planned economic relations, this was not the case in the DDR. The experiences of the AIVs certainly pointed to contradictions between the different forms of ownership that needed to be resolved, but the cooperatives were already deeply integrated into the overall economic processes of the DDR through jointly developed plans and thus binding agreements on the distribution of funds as well as the fixed-price system. While the LPGs could exercise some economic autonomy regarding their internal workings, there would be no significant effect of these autonomous decisions on the broader economy. The cooperative way of thinking, working, and living in the DDR democratised the villages by eroding social hierarchies, expanding leisure time, and promoting extensive opportunities for cultural, sporting, and social activities. Cultural centres, kindergartens, village stores, cafeterias, comprehensive medical care, continuing education opportunities, vacation sites, sports teams, music groups, and much more were an integral part of village and community infrastructure. It was the rural population itself that played a key role in building these structures and sustaining them. The DDR was largely able to produce its own agricultural products, and hunger and poverty became things of the past. Today, in view of soaring rates of global hunger, the omnipresent overexploitation of nature, and the market dominance of agricultural multinational corporations, some political movements seek the solution in small-scale production. The DDR followed a different path, which – despite a decreasing workforce – was able to avoid the negative effects of capitalist farming while increasing yields by industrialising large-scale agricultural production on the basis of socialist relations. The abrupt termination of this project in 1990 and the developments in the years since elucidate the essential characteristics of capitalist transformation: privatisation, the dismantling of ‘unprofitable’ social and cultural infrastructure, land speculation, and rural exodus. Farmers had and still have to fight their own way through the chaos of liquidation, privatisation, and fierce competition, pitted against a constructed false enemy/scapegoat of ‘cheap’ labour provided mostly by migrants. The dismantling of socialist infrastructure in the countryside furthered the tendency towards isolation among rural populations. While the renewed reign of private property postured itself as offering increased freedom of choice to farmers, this ‘freedom’ is inherently limited by constraints of the market and comes at the expense of farmers’ roles as co-creators of society. Along with it came a new rule of order: profit as the determining factor in agriculture. While the conditions today differ greatly from those in 1945, the slogan ‘The land to those who work it’ is yet again on the agenda in East Germany and across the world. Kolkhozy were established in 1929 during the state programme of expropriating private holdings. Unlike the cooperative model in the DDR, farmland in the Soviet Union was state-owned and was leased to the kolkhozy for an indefinite period. Peasants worked the land collectively and received compensation based on their labour contributions.
